Physical AI:
A Survey of Models, Learning, Systems, and Open Challenges
Bin Gao1, Anuj Gautam2, Aritra Bagchi1, Yunkai Liang3, Wentao Li3, Ronghui Li3, Zhi Zhou3, Yancan Mao4,
Xiao Yan5, Xin He5, Qingyun Zou1, Wenshuo Yue1, Dan Chen1, Zhanglu Yan1, Fei Xu6, Huize Li7, Weijie Wang8,
Tao Ouyang9, and Tulika Mitra1
1National University of Singapore, Singapore
2Indian Institute of Technology Bombay, India
3Sun Yat-sen University, China
4Huazhong University of Science and Technology, China
5Wuhan University, China
6East China Normal University, China
7University of Central Florida, USA
8Fondazione Bruno Kessler, Italy
9Central South University, China
Abstract
Physical AI aims to enable intelligent systems to perceive, reason, act, and learn through interaction with the physical
world. Unlike purely digital intelligence, physical agents must operate under partial observability, uncertain dynamics,
embodiment constraints, and real-time requirements, while their actions continuously reshape the environment and subse-
quent observations. Recent advances in foundation models and robot learning have expanded the capabilities of physical
agents, yet achieving reliable autonomy requires more than improvements in individual models. This survey presents a
comprehensive, system-level review of Physical AI, organized around the closed-loop process of physical interaction. We
examine the foundations and recent developments in task representations and memory, predictive and action-generating
models, data and learning methods, embodiment and control, computing systems, evaluation, and safety. Beyond re-
viewing individual advances, we investigate how these components interact and collectively shape the capabilities and
limitations of Physical AI systems. Building on this integrated perspective, we identify fundamental challenges in gener-
alization, resource-efficient learning and execution, reliable state tracking, safety, recovery, and continual adaptation. We
argue that advancing Physical AI requires moving beyond isolated model performance toward integrated systems capable
of robust, efficient, safe, and autonomous physical interaction under changing real-world conditions.
Keywords: Physical AI; robot learning; vision–language–action models; world models; embodied control; real-time sys-
tems; evaluation and safety.
1
Introduction
The introduction moves from physical interaction (§ 1.1) and its challenges (§ 1.2) to the survey’s scope and organization
(§ 1.3), then its relationship to existing reviews and contributions (§ 1.4). Figure 1 maps this progression.
Figure 1: Introduction map. Physical interaction motivates the challenges, organization, and contribution of the survey. The four
panels follow the introductory subsections; arrows show the progression of the argument.
1.1
Physical AI and Physical Interaction
Physical AI aims to enable intelligent systems to perceive, reason, act, and learn through interaction with the physical
world. Physical agents must operate under partial observability, uncertain dynamics, embodiment constraints, and real-
time requirements. Their actions change both the environment and the observations available for subsequent decisions,
making physical interaction a closed-loop process. This survey focuses on robotic systems: a robot combines a physical
body with the sensing, models, control, and computing needed for physical interaction. The body is its physical structure
and mechanisms; the environment comprises the objects, surfaces, and other actors with which it interacts. To clarify

these roles, the discussion follows an execution cycle, then distinguishes how pretrained models, feedback, training, and
memory support it.
A task specifies a goal and constraints on reaching it. Sensors measure the body and environment, providing observa-
tions; representations encode this information for models that select actions. Controllers compute commands from selected
actions and feedback, and actuators apply them to the body. New observations provide evidence of the resulting physical
effects and inform the next decision. Task completion depends on those effects, including changes in objects and contact,
rather than on the production of a plausible description or action sequence.
Pretrained models extend the knowledge available within this process. Language models can select among skills a
robot can execute [1]; vision–language–action (VLA) policies connect visual and linguistic knowledge to robot-action
outputs [2]. Visual pretraining supplies reusable representations [3, 4], while demonstrations across robots, simulated
interaction, and feedback from real execution supply experience for learning physical behavior [5–8]. These advances in
foundation models and robot learning expand the capabilities of physical agents, but reliable autonomy also depends on
how they work with sensing, memory, control, and computing. Recognizing an object, selecting a feasible action, and
learning from its outcome remain distinct requirements that must be coordinated through feedback.
Figure 2 separates execution from model training. During execution, new observations can update estimates of physical
state and task progress, and thereby change the next action, without changing model parameters. Model training uses
records and supervision to update parameters for later decisions. Memory can also retain records or skills for later reuse
without updating model parameters. The functions can share one model or be distributed across learned and engineered
components.
Figure 2: Physical interaction and experience updates in a mug-placement example. (a) Instructions, observations, and optional
memory inform action selection. (b) A controller executes requested actions; new observations inform subsequent decisions. The
outlined mug is a goal, not an observed outcome. (c) Recorded experience can support training and memory maintenance through
separate paths. Candidate weights require validation before release, and release does not itself activate a policy on a robot. Memory
updates need not change model parameters.
1.2
Challenges of Physical Tasks
The interaction cycle creates difficulties in what the robot can know, what it can execute in time, how it acquires experience,
and what remains useful when conditions change. The four challenges below separate these sources of difficulty, while the
cup-task example shows why a single failure may involve several of them.
Incomplete and Heterogeneous Information. Cameras, joint encoders, and touch sensors measure different aspects of
the body and environment at different rates, with different noise and delay. An image can provide evidence of an object’s
shape while leaving its mass, friction, or contact state uncertain. Sensor placement and the body’s geometry also determine
what can be observed. The system must therefore combine measurements with prior knowledge, retain useful history, and
distinguish estimated quantities from confirmed task outcomes. Acting can itself provide new evidence: a small push can
reveal how an object responds to force, while a reach may occlude it from the camera.
Physical Feasibility and Real-time Response. A selected action must be realizable by the body’s joints, grippers, and
support contacts. The body and environment continue to change during sensing and computation, so a geometrically
appropriate target can become outdated before execution. The useful response time depends on the physical process: slow
task planning and rapid contact correction can coexist, provided that their outputs remain consistent. Executing actions
while computing the next decision is one way to accommodate inference latency [9], but it also requires deciding when
2


new observations should replace an earlier action sequence.
Costly Experience and Persistent Consequences. Real attempts consume time, equipment, energy, and often human
effort for supervision or resets. A failure may damage an object or leave the robot in a state from which the original plan
cannot continue. The difficulty is both to learn from limited attempts and to make further attempts feasible. This requires
usable records of observations, action commands, and observed outcomes, together with recovery, safe termination, or
assistance when execution fails.
Changes in Tasks, Environments, and Bodies. A skill learned under one set of conditions may fail when the task,
environment, or body changes. Recognizing a new cup does not establish how to grip it, and a motion feasible for one
arm may be unreachable for another. Changes can also occur together, making it difficult to identify what must be adapted.
Reusing experience therefore requires determining which representations and behaviors transfer, how much additional
interaction is needed, and whether adaptation retains earlier capabilities.
Consider a robot instructed to “find the cup, wash it, and place it at the specified location”; Figure 2 illustrates the
placement stage. This hypothetical task provides a running example for distinctions that recur across the survey. Suppose
the gripper closes without securing the cup. We denote this hypothetical missed-grasp event by E1; it records an outcome,
not its cause. The position estimate may be stale, the grasp may be unsuitable, or execution may be late. Distinguishing
these explanations requires evidence from the relevant components. Even successful motions do not establish that washing
is complete: cleanliness needs its own observable criterion.
1.3
Scope and Organization
This survey presents a system-level review of Physical AI organized around the closed-loop process of physical interac-
tion. It focuses on robotic systems that use learned representations or learned decision-making, together with the sensing,
control, data, and computing components needed to complete physical tasks. Manipulation supplies the main detailed
technical evidence. Examples from other robotic systems illustrate how bodies, dynamics, and outcome requirements al-
ter the mechanisms under discussion. Classical estimation, planning, and control are included where they implement or
support these systems. This scope is followed by the assessment dimensions used throughout the review and the chapter
organization built around the functions needed for physical tasks.
The overall objective is sustained, reliable task completion under changing conditions. Four dimensions guide its assess-
ment: robustness, maintaining task performance under disturbances and changes; efficiency, the time, data, computation,
energy, and human effort required to acquire and use that capability; safety, keeping risks within stated limits during learn-
ing and operation; and autonomy, the extent to which task execution, recovery, and continued operation depend on human
assistance. These dimensions can interact: fewer interventions may reduce cost, but withholding necessary assistance can
undermine safety or completion. These assessment dimensions describe what must be measured, not assumed properties
of current systems.
Incomplete observations, costly experience, and physical execution constraints affect several of these assessment di-
mensions at once. The review first examines the information and mechanisms needed for decisions, then how they are
learned and physically executed, and finally how their combined behavior is assessed. This order separates questions for
analysis; it does not prescribe a processing pipeline or require separate modules. For example, learning can update several
components, while evaluation and safety apply throughout development and operation.
• Information and Models. To make decisions from incomplete and heterogeneous observations, the system must estimate
and retain task-relevant information (§ 2), then use it to predict outcomes, plan, and generate actions (§ 3). These chapters
examine what information each decision requires and how feedback can revise it.
• Data and Learning. To learn from costly and uneven experience, the system needs sources that supply observations,
action correspondence (the mapping from source data to the robot’s action interface), and feedback (§ 4). Learning
methods use these signals to acquire behavior, adapt to new tasks, environments, and bodies, and retain earlier capabilities
(§ 5). Simulation supplies one source of experience; its use for testing is treated separately in Section 8.
• Physical Execution and Computing. Embodiment describes how the body’s geometry, dynamics, sensing, and actuation
shape physical interaction (§ 6). Action interfaces and feedback control connect selected actions to that body. Timely
response depends on where computation runs and how its results enter execution (§ 7). Computing systems also support
experience collection and policy updates, linking execution costs to learning and adaptation.
• Evaluation and Safety. Evaluation tests completion under changing conditions, sustained performance, constraint vio-
lations, and resource and human costs (§ 8). Safety mechanisms address the consequences of physical action through
constraints, monitoring, recovery, and human takeover (§ 9). Together, these chapters distinguish evidence about behavior
from mechanisms that constrain it.
• Open Challenges and Synthesis. Open challenges connect the remaining limitations across the four assessment dimen-
sions (§ 10), and the conclusion draws together the conditions for sustained task completion (§ 11).
3


Within this organization, mechanism comparisons ask what information a method uses, what it produces, and what
assumptions its use requires. Reported results then establish which benefits have been tested and under what conditions.
The open challenges use these dependencies to connect local improvements to system-level capability.
1.4
Related Surveys and Contributions
We compare the coverage of related reviews, then state this survey’s contributions and study-selection approach.
Existing surveys approach learning-enabled robotics through complementary perspectives. Foundation-model reviews
organize models for perception, decision-making, and control [10–12], while broader embodied-AI reviews examine re-
search targets and paradigms [13, 14]. More focused reviews examine VLA and manipulation methods [15, 16], predictive
models [17, 18], and the data and learning methods used to acquire behavior [19, 20]. System dependencies are also es-
tablished concerns: Grover et al. [21] connect sensing, memory, scheduling, power, and safety in edge deployment, and
Brunke et al. [22] connect learning-based control, safe reinforcement learning, and policy certification.
This survey brings these topics together to explain how their interaction shapes the capabilities and limitations of Physical
AI systems. Its contributions are threefold: comparing mechanisms under their reported conditions, tracing dependencies
between their interfaces throughout the physical interaction loop, and identifying the cross-component limitations that
shape the research agenda. The comparisons distinguish measured results from expectations based on how a mechanism
works. The interface analysis examines how action representations depend on control interfaces, how computation changes
the timeliness of feedback, and how execution records support learning and recovery. The resulting synthesis connects chal-
lenges in generalization, resource-efficient learning and execution, reliable state tracking, safety, recovery, and continual
adaptation. It identifies which dependencies still limit robustness, efficiency, safety, and autonomy, and what evidence is
needed to establish progress.
We use a problem-driven narrative review, selecting studies for their relevance to physical action, identifiable mecha-
nisms, and traceable evidence. Reported results are interpreted under each study’s tasks, training access, and physical
conditions.
2
Task Information and Representations
Incomplete and heterogeneous observations raise a basic question: what information does a robot need to complete a task,
and how can it obtain and retain that information? The answer depends on the task goal, constraints, and the decision being
made: selecting a target, correcting a grasp, and confirming completion require different evidence.
Observations provide partial measurements of the body and environment, from which a robot estimates physical quanti-
ties such as object poses and contact conditions. These estimates do not by themselves establish task progress, which also
depends on task history and completion criteria. This section separates three related questions: how perceptual represen-
tations encode relevant information (§ 2.1), how task-state tracking uses available evidence to estimate progress (§ 2.2),
and how memory retains and maintains information across time (§ 2.3). The order moves from representing observations
to interpreting task progress and supporting later decisions; these functions can share representations. Figure 3 organizes
the chapter around information sufficient for the next decision: current perceptual evidence, estimated task progress, and
retained context constrain one another rather than forming a compulsory serial pipeline.
Figure 3: Information for physical decisions. Perceptual representations provide current evidence, task-state tracking estimates
progress, and memory retains context. Their connections express information exchange rather than a compulsory processing sequence.
2.1
Perceptual Representations
To wash the correct cup, the robot must identify the target, locate its handle, track its motion, and detect slipping during
grasping. The following account moves from what is identified to where and how interaction unfolds: semantic grounding
links instructions to targets (§ 2.1.1), spatial representations retain geometry for action (§ 2.1.2), and temporal representa-
tions use history to resolve changing or hidden conditions (§ 2.1.3). The account then compares where and how multimodal
fusion combines signals to affect a decision (§ 2.1.4), and closes with contact and force representations of local physical
interaction (§ 2.1.5). These are complementary information needs; their sensing and processing costs must be weighed
against their task value.
4


Figure 4 illustrates these information needs through a mug-grasping example: a target mask, a partial point cloud, joint
and tactile measurements, an observation history, and an explicit task record. These are complementary views of the
information needed for a decision, rather than mutually exclusive representation classes or a required processing pipeline.
The figure selects representative encodings; the following discussion also covers learned features, recurrent states, and
other alternatives. Explicit forms such as masks, points, and task records can also be produced by learned models.
Figure 4: Complementary information representations for a mug-grasping task. (a) A binary mask M identifies the image region
associated with the instruction; H and W are the image height and width. (b) A partial point cloud P encodes observed 3D geometry;
occluded surfaces remain unknown. (c) Joint angles q and velocities ˙q describe body configuration and motion, while tactile signals
provide local contact evidence. (d) Observations ot−2:t record gripper closure over time. (e) An explicit task record distinguishes
target selection and an attempted grasp from a confirmed secure grasp. Illustrations are schematic: tactile intensity does not directly
specify force, and gripper closure alone does not confirm a secure grasp.
2.1.1
Semantic Grounding
Semantic grounding links instructions to objects, properties, and relations in a scene. Beyond recognizing categories,
the robot must identify the target object among similar ones and preserve local or relational details, such as handles or
occlusion, for action selection. Three key design choices are how visual features align with language, how much spatial
detail they retain, and how they are integrated into the decision model. These functions can be combined within a single
encoder.
Vision–language Alignment. Image–text learning provides broad semantic knowledge before robot-specific training.
CLIP [3] learns image–text correspondence from large-scale data, enabling recognition of categories and attributes beyond
predefined robot action labels. SigLIP [23] instead uses a pairwise sigmoid loss, avoiding CLIP’s batch-wide normaliza-
tion. Both improve visual–language alignment but do not directly determine how accurately a downstream policy localizes
the target object. The source of alignment also matters. R3M [4] combines time-contrastive learning and video–language
alignment on human videos to learn manipulation-relevant representations. Unlike image–caption pairs, videos capture
temporal relationships and interactions, but transferring these features to robots still requires robot data or an appropriate
action interface. The key perceptual question is whether pretraining captures appearance, language references, temporal
relationships, or their combination.
Spatially Resolved Features. Identifying the target object does not necessarily reveal its parts, such as a cup’s handle.
Local features and segmentation provide finer spatial information. DINOv2 [24] learns self-supervised features for image-
and pixel-level tasks, while SAM [25] generates prompt-conditioned masks. Dense features require downstream inter-
pretation, whereas masks explicitly identify image regions. Modular pipelines can combine open-vocabulary detection
and segmentation: Grounding DINO [26] integrates text and image features through a transformer to localize referring
expressions as bounding boxes, which SAM can further refine into masks. Spatial detail and language alignment can be
combined. CLIPort [27] uses a CLIP-based semantic stream to identify what to manipulate and a Transporter-based spatial
stream to determine where, fusing both to predict pick-and-place locations. Similarly, OpenVLA [28] combines DINOv2
and SigLIP features in its visual encoder. However, neither provides a complete physical-state estimate: masks may isolate
objects without revealing their constraints, while local features may capture handles without identifying feasible grasps.
Language-model Integration. The model that interprets the task must be able to use the visual features. PaLM-E [29]
projects visual and continuous-state information into a language model’s input as multimodal sequences, so the model
that interprets a request or produces a plan also processes the perceptual content. In action-generating VLAs, image and
5


instruction information similarly enter a shared model, but an action interface determines how the resulting representation
affects control. Placing perception inside a language model therefore specifies an integration point, not an alternative
to language-aligned or spatially resolved features. Selecting the wrong cup indicates a grounding failure, missing handle
details reflects insufficient spatial granularity, and unusable perceptual information indicates an interface failure. Language-
aligned features help identify the target, but grasp selection also requires local geometry and an action mapping.
2.1.2
Spatial Representations
Spatial representations help policies connect observed structures to possible actions. Grasping requires identifying an
approach, aligning it with the robot’s workspace, and handling uncertainty at contact. Some methods explicitly represent
geometry using depth, points, voxels, or spatial targets, while others infer spatial information from image features. The key
difference lies in how geometry is represented and supervised, as 3D inputs can be inaccurate and image-based policies
can still reason spatially.
Explicit Geometry.
PerAct [30] converts multiview RGB-D observations into a voxel grid and predicts language-
conditioned keyframe actions, including translation, rotation, gripper state, and collision avoidance. A motion planner
executes these keyframes rather than directly predicting low-level controls. By selecting translation targets within the
voxel grid, PerAct aligns action coordinates with scene geometry, reducing the need to infer metric positions from
images. However, its accuracy depends on voxel resolution and calibration, as discretization limits spatial precision and
reconstruction errors can shift predicted targets. 3D Diffusion Policy [31] uses a compact point-cloud representation to
condition continuous action sequences executed by a lower-level controller. Unlike PerAct, which selects keyframes from
an explicit voxel grid for motion planning, it encodes 3D geometry into a latent representation. Both leverage measured
3D structure but differ in scene representation and action interface. Their performance also depends on depth quality,
preprocessing, and training setup, so differences cannot be attributed to 3D input alone. Explicit geometry is limited by
sensor coverage, while higher voxel resolution or point density increases computational cost. RVT [32] addresses this
trade-off by reconstructing a point cloud from RGB-D observations and re-rendering it into virtual views containing color,
depth, and world coordinates. A transformer processes these views and predicts heatmaps that are back-projected into 3D
to select the gripper translation. This avoids a full voxel grid while preserving measured geometry. On RLBench, RVT
reports higher success rates and lower training and inference costs than PerAct, although these results reflect differences
in the overall architectures. RVT remains sensitive to depth errors, calibration, and occlusion. Ultimately, the required
geometric resolution depends on task precision.
Geometry Inferred from Images. Image-based models rely more on learned spatial inference. SpatialVLM [33] uses syn-
thetic question–answer supervision to improve metric and relational reasoning, while RoboPoint [34] predicts instruction-
conditioned image keypoints as spatial affordances. The former provides spatial knowledge, whereas the latter identifies
actionable locations. MolmoAct [35] instead predicts depth-aware perception tokens from images and instructions, fol-
lowed by an editable image-space trajectory trace and actions conditioned on both. This incorporates depth reasoning
without requiring measured depth as input. Embodied-R1 [36], like RoboPoint, uses pointing to connect reasoning with
action primitives. This allows target selection to be inspected separately from execution. However, converting image-space
points into feasible motions still requires depth and calibration, learned mappings, or sensor-guided action primitives. Ex-
plicit geometry reduces learned spatial inference but is limited by measurement errors, occlusion, and computational cost.
Image-based methods rely more on learned spatial relationships and downstream action mapping.
2.1.3
Temporal State Representations
Similar images can represent different physical states, such as a moving versus stationary ball or a slipping versus firmly
held cup. Contact history and occluded objects introduce further ambiguity. Temporal representations reduce this partial
observability by preserving past information through either an explicit history window or an updated internal summary.
Whereas stored history records earlier evidence, prediction estimates states or outcomes that have not yet been observed.
Predictive supervision can enhance either approach independently of how history is stored.
History Windows. A history window provides the model with a bounded sequence of observations, preserving recent
motion and information about occluded objects. Its effective horizon depends on sampling frequency and window size,
while subsampling may miss brief contact events. History can also reveal conditions hidden from the current observation.
For example, RMA [37] uses the last 50 state–action pairs (about 0.5 s) to estimate latent terrain, payload, and other condi-
tions for quadruped locomotion. Rather than tracking object motion, this window captures body–environment interactions.
The adaptation module updates the latent from a rolling window, while the policy reuses it between updates. A history
window loses events once they fall outside its span, while longer windows increase computational costs and make relevant
observations harder to identify. They can also harm imitation learning through the copycat problem, where a policy repeats
previous actions rather than predicting the next. Wen et al. [38] address this causal confusion by adversarially removing
previous-action information while preserving features useful for predicting the next action. Multiscale designs such as
MEM mitigate window limitations by retaining recent details and selectively preserving older information.
Recurrent State Updates. A recurrent model updates a latent state with each new observation instead of repeatedly
6


processing the full history. PlaNet [39] combines deterministic and stochastic transitions to maintain state estimates and
represent uncertainty under partial observability. PlaNet uses this state for planning, while Dreamer uses a similar recurrent
state for direct action selection. However, what information the state retains depends on the training objective, model
capacity, and available training evidence.
Either history representation can be trained with predictive supervision. Predicting future observations or latent states
can help representations capture temporal changes. GR-1 [40] jointly predicts future images and actions. However, the
benefit depends on which changes are emphasized: background motion and impending grasp failure require different
information. Prediction can also compensate for execution delays. AHEAD [41] estimates motion across observations to
predict future patch tokens for a frozen VLA, providing its action decoder with a more up-to-date scene representation.
Unlike predictive training losses, this prediction occurs during execution. Its effectiveness depends on delay, prediction
accuracy, and uncertainty handling, and differs from action-conditioned prediction for planning.
2.1.4
Multimodal Fusion
During grasping, vision locates the cup, proprioception tracks hand configuration, and touch or force detects slipping.
These signals arrive at different rates and contribute at different moments. Their influence depends on the fusion stage:
input, internal features, or outputs. This subsection compares where signals meet; the following subsection examines
what contact and force signals themselves encode. Figure 5 compares input, feature, and estimate fusion for the same
mug-grasping task. These patterns can coexist within a system.
Early Fusion. Input-level fusion allows modalities to interact through shared processing. PaLM-E adds visual and state
encodings to language-model inputs [29]; RT-2 and OpenVLA combine images and instructions to generate actions [2, 28];
and Octo uses a transformer policy with a diffusion action head [42]. Although all fuse modalities at the input, they differ
in outputs, training, and embodiment interfaces. Shared processing lets task context shape sensory information use, but its
cost depends on token count, attention structure, and feature recomputation. Attention masks control token interactions.
For fast-changing signals like touch, responsiveness depends on when new measurements reach the model and affect
actions. A slow backbone cannot provide corrections faster than its update rate.
Intermediate Fusion. Feature-level fusion allows separate encoders or branches to exchange information within a model.
TransFuser uses attention to combine image and LiDAR features [43]. In π0, an action expert combines a reusable vision–
language prefix with proprioceptive state [44]. Reactive Diffusion Policy similarly separates slow planning from fast tactile
feedback [45]. A latent diffusion policy generates action chunks from visual and tactile or force inputs at 1–2 Hz, while a fast
decoder uses current tactile or force feedback to update actions at 24 Hz. This allows rapid corrections without rerunning
the slower policy. In Reactive Diffusion Policy’s asymmetric tokenizer, tactile input reaches the decoder but not the action
encoder. This lets the latent chunk represent a general feedback strategy while the decoder adjusts individual actions, such
as lifting a peeler when contact force increases. In Reactive Diffusion Policy’s peeling, wiping, and bimanual lifting tasks,
adding tactile input to a standard Diffusion Policy performed similarly to vision alone because action chunks remained open
loop. The slow–fast design performed better across all three tasks. This shows that a modality’s effectiveness depends on
where it enters the model and how often its branch updates. New feedback can only correct an action if the model allows
it to be revised.
Late Fusion. Output-level fusion combines results from separately processed modalities. Swift combines visual–inertial
pose estimates with corrections from detected race gates [46]. Similarly, a manipulation system might confirm a grasp
when gripper width, load, and vision agree. Outputs remain independently usable, but fusion is limited to the information
they retain. Missing uncertainty, timing, or alternative hypotheses can reduce reliability, so the combining module must
handle differences in coordinates, timing, validity, and missing or inconsistent inputs.
Fusion location determines where information is combined, while the update mechanism determines when new mea-
surements can influence an action. The slow–fast policy example above illustrates how current contact measurements can
revise actions while reusing slower context; this schedule is a separate design choice, not an automatic consequence of
feature-level fusion. Across fusion stages, additional information helps only when it can influence decisions at the right
time. Fusion cannot recover information lost during encoding or compensate for delayed feedback.
2.1.5
Contact and Force Representations
Contact information describes gripper–object interactions, including contact occurrence, load distribution, and potential
slip. The discussion separates learning reusable tactile features from integrating contact measurements into a policy. These
are complementary choices: an encoder determines what information is available, while the policy interface determines
how and when it influences actions. Sensor frame, calibration, and sampling time are essential for interpretation.
7


Figure 5: Multimodal fusion locations in a mug-grasping example. The same visual, joint-state, and tactile inputs support three
conceptual patterns. (a) Early fusion combines modality encodings for processing by a shared policy. (b) Intermediate fusion
exchanges separately encoded features before action generation. (c) Late fusion combines modality-specific estimates of cup pose, jaw
opening, and contact before action selection. These patterns may coexist within a system. Feature cells and tactile signals are
schematic; the inward arrows illustrate closure commands, not confirmed grasp success.
Learning Tactile Features. Because labeled tactile data are scarce and sensor-specific, Sparsh [47] uses self-supervised
learning on unlabeled tactile images to train a reusable encoder; in its frozen-encoder evaluation, task-specific decoders are
trained while the encoder is fixed. These small decoders predict three-axis contact forces from indentation data or detect
slip from 80 ms of tactile frames labeled using a friction-cone model. Output accuracy and physical meaning depend
on task-specific labels and calibration, so performance validated on probe sliding may not directly generalize to object
grasping.
Contact Inputs for Policies. ViTaL [48] combines egocentric vision and tactile sensing for local contact control after
vision–language target localization. Its ablations demonstrate the benefit of tactile input in contact-rich tasks. While
learned tactile embeddings can guide action selection, explicit physical contact estimates require corresponding supervision
or calibration. For wrist force input, ForceVLA [49] integrates six-axis wrist force–torque sensing into a π0-style policy by
fusing a force token with vision–language features through a small mixture-of-experts module before the flow-matching
action expert. Across five contact-rich tasks, it achieves 60.5% average success, compared with 37.3% without force and
40.2% when force is appended to proprioception. Ablations show that post-VLM fusion outperforms earlier injection,
highlighting that where contact information enters the policy matters as much as its presence. For the wet cup, object
identity and pose alone cannot detect slip. Tactile, force, and proprioceptive history provide the necessary feedback, but
must reach the controller in time to prevent grasp failure. Contact therefore requires dedicated sensing and timely feedback
updates.
Representations should preserve information needed to resolve task-relevant ambiguities. Part localization can identify
candidate handles, while explicit geometry supports metric reasoning at the cost of depth, calibration, and computation.
Image-conditioned control remains viable when reconstruction offers little benefit. Hidden contact requires timely tactile,
force, visual, or proprioceptive feedback. Longer histories help only when past observations remain relevant, while multi-
modal fusion must account for differences in sensor timing and reliability. The choice therefore depends on the ambiguity
to resolve, the available sensors, and when the resulting estimate must affect an action. Open questions include robustness
to calibration errors and occlusion, history relevance after unexpected changes, and sensor reliability under missing or de-
layed measurements. Active reobservation can refresh spatial information, while fast local controllers can process contact
feedback without routing it through a shared backbone.
2.2
Task State Representation and Tracking
Task state is the actual progress toward the goal, including completed and remaining stages and the conditions required for
the next action. It depends on both physical state and task history: a cup’s pose describes its current physical configura-
tion, while a confirmed washing event records earlier progress. The account first describes how progress and completion
conditions can be represented, then how observations and memory update the task-state estimate and check conditions
for subsequent actions. Figure 4(e) illustrates an explicit task record: target selection is confirmed and a grasp attempt
is observed, while whether the object is secured remains unconfirmed. The closure sequence in panel (d) therefore does
not by itself justify advancing to transport. Task state can be represented by subtasks, confirmed predicates, event records,
or learned summaries, each providing different information to the policy. Explicit records distinguish planned, attempted,
8


and confirmed actions, while learned summaries may encode these distinctions implicitly. Crucially, action attempts do
not establish completion: after the missed grasp E1, transport requires confirmation that the object is held.
Task-state updates combine current observations, memory, and completion criteria. MEM [50] tracks task stages through
text memory and short-horizon video, while Hi Robot [51] revises high-level decisions using observations and feedback.
Both preserve past progress for future decisions through different architectures. Task-state tracking must account for un-
certainty and changing conditions. A successful grasp may fail after slip, while a past cleaning event does not guarantee
the object remains clean. Thus, tracking must distinguish historical events from current states. Memory maintains relevant
records, planning uses updated estimates, and evaluation defines evidence of task completion.
2.3
Memory
Memory stores and retrieves observations, events, estimates, and reusable skills over time to support state estimation and
behavior. It extends the observation history illustrated in Figure 4(d) with mechanisms for storage, retrieval, maintenance,
and reuse. The first three parts distinguish what is retained: past events for later decisions (§ 2.3.1), persistent information
about objects and places (§ 2.3.2), and reusable behaviors (§ 2.3.3). Memory maintenance then addresses the shared
problem of updating, retrieving, checking, or invalidating those records and skills (§ 2.3.4). These are complementary
design dimensions: different memory types may share retrieval mechanisms but have distinct requirements for remaining
useful.
2.3.1
Episodic Memory
Retrieving a cup after visiting another room may require recalling its location, a failed grasp, or completed steps. Episodic
memory preserves relevant past observations and events for future decisions, requiring clear rules for storage, compression,
and task-driven retrieval.
Learned and Multiscale Memory. A history window fails when relevant events fall outside its range. Learned memory
mechanisms address this by retaining selected events in memory banks or compressed summaries. MemoryVLA [52]
retrieves relevant perceptual and semantic entries and merges redundant ones during updates. MEM [50] combines com-
pressed video for short-term detail with text memory for task progress, enabling tasks lasting up to fifteen minutes and
context-dependent manipulation. Their key difference lies in memory format: learned tokens in MemoryVLA versus
readable text in MEM.
Caption-based Retrieval. ReMEmbR [53] stores robot observations as video captions with spatial and temporal metadata.
A vision–language model periodically generates captions, which are embedded and stored with the robot’s position and
timestamp. At query time, a language-model agent iteratively retrieves records by text, location, or time to answer questions
or guide navigation. Retrieval keeps query time roughly stable across tested video lengths, but performance depends on
caption accuracy. For example, a soda-machine query led the robot to a water fountain mislabeled as a “silver machine”,
illustrating how incomplete or misleading records can cause navigation errors.
2.3.2
Spatial Memory
A robot returning to the kitchen must recall the cup’s last known location, even if it is out of view or has moved. Spatial
memory links observations to persistent objects and places, enabling retrieval across viewpoints. This requires suitable
representations, cross-view associations, and updates when stored locations become outdated. Dense maps and scene
graphs provide complementary spatial representations. VLMaps [54] projects vision–language features into a top-down
grid, enabling language-based localization. ConceptGraphs [55] instead builds an open-vocabulary 3D scene graph of
objects and their semantic and spatial relations. SayPlan [56] uses hierarchical scene graphs for building-scale planning,
retrieving task-relevant regions while a classical planner handles navigation. However, constructing spatial representations
does not ensure their validity over time: moved objects require re-association, changed relations must be updated, and
unobserved regions may become outdated.
Maintaining spatial memory requires associating new observations with existing objects and updating outdated records.
DynaMem [57] addresses this using a voxel map that stores vision–language features, observation counts, source images,
and timestamps. New RGB-D observations update the map, while depth evidence of free space removes outdated voxels
within a 2 m range. Object queries use voxel features or stored images, with exploration triggered when objects are not
found. In dynamic environments, DynaMem achieves 70% pick-and-drop success versus 30% for static OK-Robot [58].
However, moved objects remain at their old locations until new observations provide evidence for removal.
2.3.3
Skill Memory
Skill memory, or procedural memory, stores reusable behaviors such as policies, programs, or task procedures. Unlike
state memory, it supports action reuse across tasks through retrieval and composition. Its effectiveness depends on se-
lecting applicable skills and verifying that their preconditions match the current state. LOTUS [59] discovers skills from
9


unsegmented demonstrations, expands a skill library, and trains a meta-controller to compose skills for new manipulation
tasks. Voyager [60] similarly stores and reuses self-verified executable programs in Minecraft. However, retrieving a skill
does not guarantee that its preconditions hold, nor does executing an action guarantee success.
A reusable skill requires defined inputs, parameters, preconditions, and completion criteria. Retrieval by task similarity
alone may select inapplicable behaviors, so planners must verify their suitability against the current state. Skill libraries
can grow through new modules or programs, but retaining them requires storage and maintenance. Effective reuse requires
retrieving, composing, or acquiring skills as needed; library size alone does not guarantee applicability.
2.3.4
Memory Maintenance
An issued command does not guarantee successful execution. Recording “cup held” without confirming the grasp can
mislead later planning. Memory maintenance must therefore check the evidence for stored claims, resolve conflicts, and
invalidate outdated information. Retrieval, recording, and maintenance have distinct failure modes: writing may treat
inferred outcomes as observed, retrieval may miss relevant records, and maintenance may preserve stale information or
make incorrect updates. When an object reappears elsewhere, data association must determine whether it is the same
instance. Timestamps, provenance, confidence, and validity conditions help detect such conflicts. MemoryVLA merges
redundant entries, while DynaMem removes voxels contradicted by new observations. Memory updates should distinguish
observations, inferred outcomes, and confirmation status. A “cup held” claim should be confirmed only by sufficient
visual, gripper, or contact evidence. Failure explanations also depend on retained records. OpenEQA [61] evaluates
environmental understanding through recorded episodes and active exploration, but not memory-guided manipulation.
Closed-loop evaluation, as in RoboMemArena [62], is needed to assess how memory affects subsequent behavior.
Figure 6 illustrates the distinction between historical truth and a valid current-state claim: a mug observed at A can later
be observed at B. Updating the usable location does not erase the earlier event or require retraining the policy. The update
depends on identifying the same object and observing the relevant locations; lack of visibility alone cannot invalidate the
old record.
Figure 6: Maintaining spatial memory when an object moves. (a) A retrieved location retains its observation time. (b) A later view
reveals an empty location A and the mug at B, contradicting the old current-location claim. (c) The usable record is updated while the
earlier observation can remain in history. This schematic assumes correct object association and visibility of both locations; an
occluded location is not evidence of absence. The memory change does not require a policy-parameter update.
Episodic, spatial, and skill memory are effective when stored information remains relevant and valid. Structured events
compress long histories but may omit useful details. Task-local spatial memory reduces maintenance costs but requires
updates when objects move. Skill libraries support reusable behaviors and modular updates, while shared policies reduce
management overhead at the cost of greater reliance on shared parameters. In all cases, retrieval is useful only when stored
facts or skill preconditions remain valid. Task-state tracking uses past and new evidence to update progress estimates.
Together with physical-state estimates and memory, these estimates provide the inputs for predicting outcomes and selecting
actions in Section 3.
10


3
Models for Prediction and Action
Models use observations, state estimates, task conditions, and memory to predict outcomes and select actions. Predictive
models predict state changes, policies select actions, and planners explore alternatives. These functions may be separate
or share parameters. Their distinction depends on the output and its use, rather than the model architecture or sequence
generation.
The chapter first distinguishes model roles by their inputs and outputs (§ 3.1). It then examines three functions: predicting
physical outcomes (§ 3.2), planning and selecting actions (§ 3.3), and generating actions at a policy interface (§ 3.4). The
final subsection compares how architectures combine or separate these functions (§ 3.5). This progression establishes the
functional distinctions before examining their integration; it does not require every system to use each function as a separate
stage. Prediction may support planning, policy conditioning, or learning; observed physical effects provide evidence of
actual outcomes. Figure 7 maps the roles of prediction, planning, and action generation and the architectures that combine
them.
Figure 7: Model roles and architectures. Prediction, planning, and action generation are distinguished by their inputs and outputs and
can be organized in shared or modular architectures. Dashed arrows indicate possible dependencies; direct policies need not use
explicit prediction or planning.
3.1
Model Inputs, Outputs, and Roles
An encoder converts observations, task conditions, and optional history into features. A world model predicts future states
or observations of the body and environment, often conditioned on actions. A policy selects actions or action sequences,
while a planner selects skills, proposes subgoals, or evaluates alternatives. These functional roles come first; the next
paragraph relates vision–language and vision–language–action models to their inputs and outputs. Explicit prediction and
planning are optional, as a policy can act without a separate world model or planner.
A vision–language model (VLM) processes visual and linguistic inputs, while a vision–language–action (VLA) policy
generates robot actions, as in RT-2 and OpenVLA [2, 28]. A VLA may share a model across modalities or add an action-
generation component to a VLM backbone. The key distinction lies in the output: predicting future object states differs
from generating robot actions, even when both use generative modeling.
3.2
Predicting Physical Outcomes
World models predict physical state changes and, when conditioned on actions, their consequences, such as whether a grasp
remains stable during lifting. Prediction is distinct from action selection. Alternatively, a task-conditioned future can define
a goal, with a controller or inverse model determining the actions to achieve it. We compare predictive representations in
observation space, explicit physical variables, and learned latent features (§ 3.2.1); this representation choice is distinct
from how predictions are conditioned or used. World models serve multiple purposes: UniSim, Cosmos, and GAIA-1
generate future observations or simulations [63–65]; DreamGen generates training experience [66]; and WorldEval, Ctrl-
World, and Veo-based simulation evaluate policies [67–69]. These applications impose different requirements [18].
3.2.1
Predictive Representations
The prediction space determines what information is available for decision-making. Observation-space models predict
future measurements, structured models represent quantities such as object positions and relations, and latent models
predict learned features. These approaches trade representation cost against task-relevant information, without determining
whether the model supports planning, training, or evaluation. Figure 8 aligns the three prediction spaces at the current time
and two hypothetical future times, conditioned on closing the gripper and lifting. Its structured readouts track mug–table
separation and contact; neither predicted contact nor a plausible lifted image verifies a secure grasp. Candidate actions
can condition consequence prediction, while a task goal can condition a desired future; the conditioning and downstream
use are separate from the representation.
Observation-space Prediction. Generated images provide visual predictions for controllers, policies, or learners, as in
UniPi’s video plans, SuSIE’s goal images, and DIAMOND’s generated Atari experience [70]. However, visual plausibility
11


Figure 8: Predictive representations of an illustrative action-conditioned grasp-and-lift sequence. Columns align current input with
two predicted future times. (a) Observation-space prediction depicts future images. (b) Selected physical variables describe the
mug–table gap and gripper contact. (c) Latent prediction evolves learned features without requiring named physical meanings for
individual dimensions. Teal marks current observations, estimates, or encodings; purple and hats mark predictions. Contact alone does
not establish grasp security, and new observations are needed to establish actual outcomes.
does not guarantee physical feasibility, and similar predictions may require different control capabilities. Generating pixel-
level detail also increases computational cost across predicted steps.
Structured Physical Prediction. A structured model predicts selected physical variables directly. RoboCraft [71] rep-
resents deformable plasticine as particles reconstructed from RGB-D point clouds, with graph edges connecting nearby
particles and gripper–object pairs. A graph network predicts state changes through repeated message passing, allowing
forces to propagate through the material. Trained on about ten minutes of real interaction, the model supports model-
predictive control to shape plasticine into targets unseen during training. However, particle states must be estimated from
partially occluded point clouds using shape and physics priors. Since particles lack temporal correspondence, training
compares point distributions rather than individual trajectories. Moreover, the model cannot predict interactions involving
entities absent from its graph.
Latent-state Prediction. A latent world model encodes history as zt = eϕ(ht) and predicts future states through zt+1 ∼
pϕ(zt+1 | zt, at), where ht is the interaction history or its summary, zt is its learned encoding, at is an action, and
ϕ denotes model parameters. Prediction heads interpret these states as rewards, values, goal distances, or observations.
PlaNet and Dreamer learn latent dynamics for control [39, 72], while V-JEPA 2 and LeWorldModel support planning
without rendering future observations [73, 74]. Alternatively, DINO-WM [75] freezes a pretrained DINOv2 encoder and
trains a transformer to predict action-conditioned patch features. Its separately trained decoder visualizes predictions but
is not used for planning.
Compression is useful when it preserves task-relevant information. Reward-based models may discard irrelevant ap-
pearance details, while goal-image planners require visual correspondence to track progress. Reconstructing unnecessary
details increases model cost. Models trained on action-free video may need action-conditioned training to predict inter-
vention outcomes, and one-step accuracy does not guarantee reliable multi-step predictions. The prediction space should
match its intended use: images for visual control or evaluation, structured variables for explicit physical interactions, and
latent states when task progress can be assessed without pixels. Any information omitted from the representation also
limits the predictions and downstream decisions.
3.3
Planning and Action Selection
Planning translates task goals into executable behavior through complementary operations. Skill-based planning selects or
composes available behaviors (§ 3.3.1), while subgoal interfaces specify what a lower-level policy should achieve (§ 3.3.2).
Planning with world models compares predicted consequences of candidate actions (§ 3.3.3), and feedback-driven replan-
ning revises a choice when new evidence changes the task-state estimate (§ 3.3.4). This order moves from the plan’s
executable units and handoff to prospective evaluation and revision, without requiring every system to use every operation.
12


3.3.1
Skill-Based Planning
Skill-based planning composes executable robot behaviors by selecting predefined skills, generating programs that invoke
perception and control functions, or searching symbolic actions with feasible continuous parameters. All approaches
depend on available skills, valid execution conditions, and observable completion.
Skill Selection. Skill-based planning lets language models select from predefined robot behaviors. SayCan [1] combines
language-based skill relevance with learned value functions estimating feasibility, enabling long-horizon tasks on a mo-
bile manipulator. SayPlan extends this approach to building-scale environments using 3D scene graphs [56]. However,
execution depends on current skill feasibility, and tasks requiring skills absent from the library remain unsupported.
Code Generation. Program-based planning supports loops, perception-dependent conditions, and numerical parameters
beyond simple skill sequences. Code as Policies [76] uses a language model to generate programs combining perception
and control functions, enabling feedback loops, reactive control, and parameter selection. In Minecraft, Voyager stores
generated programs as reusable skills [60], while VoxPoser and ReKep generate code for geometric subgoals. Although
code makes planning logic explicit and testable, reliability depends on runtime execution, available functions, and explicit
checks of necessary preconditions.
Symbolic Planning with Feasibility Checks. Task and motion planning integrates feasibility into action search. PDDL-
Stream [77] combines symbolic actions and preconditions with samplers that generate and validate continuous parameters,
including grasps, placements, and collision-free motions. It searches over symbolic plans, fills missing parameters through
sampling, and revises plans when sampling fails. However, its guarantees depend on manually specified actions, conditions,
and geometric models. Text2Motion [78] similarly evaluates skill-sequence feasibility using learned skill-value functions
and geometric reasoning.
3.3.2
Subgoal Interfaces
A lower-level policy needs an interpretable, actionable target. A planner can provide a verbal instruction, a geometric
or visual goal, or a learned feature rather than just a skill name. These formats vary in interpretability and execution
requirements, and can coexist within a hierarchy. The key questions are what is communicated, when it can be executed,
and what feedback triggers a new subgoal.
Language Subgoals. Language can serve as an interface to lower-level policies. RT-H [79] uses language-described
motions (e.g., “move arm forward”) as an intermediate representation between tasks and actions, enabling shared low-
level behaviors and learning from human corrections. Hi Robot [51] uses a vision–language model (VLM) to decompose
complex tasks into executable steps based on instructions and user feedback, which are passed to a learned low-level policy
across diverse robotic platforms and tasks. π0.5 [80] integrates both levels in one model, predicting a language subtask
(e.g., “pick up the plate”) before generating conditioned action chunks, with less frequent high-level inference. Gemini
Robotics 1.5 [81] interleaves actions with natural-language reasoning, supported by a separate model for planning and
progress estimation. Language subgoals enable human interpretation and correction but require reliable grounding and
execution feedback to detect mismatches.
Reasoning within a single policy does not necessarily establish a hierarchical interface. ECoT [82] adds task reasoning
and visual grounding to OpenVLA without additional robot data. It reuses high-level reasoning for five steps while updating
low-level reasoning and actions at each step, making inference cost dependent on reasoning depth and refresh rate.
Geometric and Visual Subgoals. Spatial targets reduce ambiguity. VoxPoser [83] uses language-generated code and
VLMs to construct 3D value maps that guide model-based trajectory planning without predefined motion primitives.
ReKep [84] encodes tasks as 3D keypoint constraints optimized into end-effector poses.
Similarly, MolmoAct and
Embodied-R1 use image-space targets, while SuSIE generates goal images for goal-conditioned policies. These repre-
sentations enable geometric verification before execution but cannot capture physical factors such as grip force or object
slippage.
Latent Subgoals. Learned features also enable multi-rate hierarchies within a VLA. HiRT [85] uses a low-frequency VLM
to guide a high-frequency vision policy, doubling control frequency with comparable static-task success and improving
dynamic-task success from 48% to 75%. Fast-in-Slow [86] reuses the slow VLM’s final layers for fast control, sharing
pretrained knowledge. Hi Robot and π0.5 similarly separate slow language planning from fast actions. However, asyn-
chronous updates risk stale or inconsistent guidance. The slow policy periodically refreshes a subgoal or feature, while
the fast policy uses that guidance together with the latest observation. Under periodic updates, guidance can age by one
slow-policy update interval, with inference and communication adding further delay. UniFS [87] balances this staleness–
efficiency trade-off by updating VLM layer groups at different frequencies, reducing average LIBERO inference latency
from 36.5 to 17.8 ms.
Skill names invoke predefined behaviors, programs specify control flow, language enables correction, geometric tar-
gets support verification, and latent features enable joint learning. Regardless of format, an effective interface requires
executable goals and observable completion conditions. A valid unfinished step can continue, while advancing requires
evidence of completion and support for the next step’s preconditions. Level-specific success distinguishes planning from
execution failures, while tracking subgoal age helps identify stale guidance at the interface.
13


Figure 9 makes this handoff concrete. A grasp instruction specifies what should happen; observed lifting and grasp
evidence determine whether the planner can advance to transport. If completion is not established, the current skill may
continue or be revised. A changed task state or invalid next-step precondition instead requires reassessing the plan. These
branches separate a planning decision from evidence of its execution.
Figure 9: An evidence-dependent subgoal handoff. (a) A grasp instruction specifies an intended state. (b) Observations distinguish a
held-and-lifted mug from a grasp whose completion is not established; contact alone is insufficient. (c) Advancing to transport requires
completion evidence and valid next-step preconditions. Otherwise the system may continue or revise the grasp, or replan when the task
state changes. The scenes illustrate alternative branches, not a guaranteed sequence from a command to successful execution.
3.3.3
Planning with World Models
Prediction supports planning by either generating future goals for a controller to achieve or evaluating the outcomes of
candidate actions. Both require grounding in current observations but impose different demands: goal prediction requires
a controller capable of realizing the imagined state, while action evaluation requires a model that accurately captures
action-dependent outcomes.
Generated Plans and Subgoals. UniPi [88] generates task-conditioned videos for planning and extracts actions through
inverse dynamics. This separates future-state prediction from action execution but introduces two failure modes: physically
unreachable predictions and inaccurate action extraction. Evaluating the full pipeline is therefore essential to distinguish
executable plans from merely plausible videos. SuSIE [89] generates visual subgoals using a pretrained image-editing
model, which a goal-conditioned policy executes. Compared with video plans, subgoals offer greater control flexibility
but may hide infeasible transitions. Its CALVIN and real-world manipulation results evaluate the combined system without
isolating subgoal-generation quality from low-level execution capability.
Candidate Evaluation. Action-conditioned world models plan by predicting and ranking candidate action sequences. A
representative reward-based objective is
a⋆

(1)
Here, ˆrϕ predicts immediate reward, ˆV estimates value beyond horizon H, and γ discounts future returns. The system
replans after execution using new observations. Goal-conditioned planners may instead minimize predicted distance to a
target. Both require reliable ranking of candidate outcomes.
Sampling-based planners approximate this optimization. PlaNet [39] uses the cross-entropy method (CEM), iteratively
sampling action sequences and refitting a Gaussian to high-return candidates. TD-MPC2 [90] uses model predictive path
integral (MPPI) control, weighting elite sequences by predicted return, incorporating a learned policy prior, and estimating
value beyond the planning horizon. DINO-WM applies CEM to minimize predicted goal distance in frozen feature space.
V-JEPA 2-AC [73] similarly plans toward image goals using action-conditioned representations adapted from video pre-
training. Sampling-based optimization can exploit model errors by favoring actions with overestimated returns. Longer
horizons and more candidates increase both error exposure and computational latency. LeWorldModel [74] addresses
computational cost through compact prediction. Effective planning therefore requires accurate ranking of relevant actions
and sufficiently fast replanning to avoid stale observations.
14


3.3.4
Feedback-Driven Replanning
Hierarchical systems should correct errors at the appropriate level: minor offsets within a skill, changed targets through
replanning, and ambiguous intent through human clarification. Each requires accurate task-state estimation, since issuing
an action does not guarantee success.
Local Correction. Local correction avoids frequent replanning. Code as Policies [76] supports feedback loops and reactive
controllers, while VoxPoser [83] generates closed-loop trajectories robust to perturbations. ReKep [84] continuously re-
optimizes keypoint constraints for reactive control without task-specific data. However, local correction is limited to the
current skill’s goal: a moved cup can be tracked, but a fallen cup may require a new subgoal or skill.
Replanning. When a subtask becomes invalid, replanning requires reliable execution feedback. SayCan [1] uses learned
skill feasibility to guide selection but does not verify completion. Inner Monologue [91] feeds success detection, scene
descriptions, and human feedback into a language-model planner, improving long-horizon task completion. RoCo [92]
uses environment feedback, including collision checks, to revise plans and waypoints. REFLECT [93] instead analyzes
summarized multisensory experience after failure to improve subsequent attempts. Effective replanning requires feedback
that changes future decisions, not merely periodic planner calls. Its reliability also depends on accurate outcome detection,
since false success reports propagate incorrect task-state estimates.
Help-seeking. When uncertainty prevents reliable decisions, the system may seek human assistance. KnowNo [94] uses
conformal prediction to request help when multiple plans remain plausible. Hi Robot [51] incorporates situated user
feedback to revise hierarchical decisions, while RT-H accepts language-based motion corrections. KnowNo uses feedback
to determine whether autonomous execution can proceed, whereas Hi Robot uses it to update subsequent choices. Effective
help-seeking requires questions humans can answer and responses that the robot can translate into executable actions.
Across local correction, replanning, and help-seeking, response time determines which failures can still be corrected.
Feedback operates at different timescales: language-model deliberation may take seconds, while physical control requires
faster updates. Excessive communication and reasoning can therefore increase latency without improving success [95, 96].
Practical systems reduce this overhead through event-triggered reasoning, cached plans, compact memories, and reactive
control [95]. ReKep exemplifies this by re-solving predefined constraints at control rate, while Fast-ThinkAct [97] distills
explicit reasoning into compact latent representations for faster inference. Reducing deliberation time is useful only if
deviations are detected soon enough to trigger local correction, replanning, or help-seeking, so runtime monitoring must
support the chosen correction timescale.
Across the planning interfaces considered here, effective planning requires knowing the controller’s capabilities and
when control returns. Skill libraries simplify selection and failure attribution but limit available behaviors. Flexible sub-
goals offer greater freedom, explicit constraints expose infeasibility, and latent interfaces reduce manual design at the
cost of interpretability. These interfaces can coexist with clear completion conditions, allowing local feedback to correct
routine errors and replanning to address significant deviations. Checking skill preconditions can prevent failures from in-
appropriate selection more effectively than increasing planner complexity. Event-triggered or distilled replanning reduces
deliberation cost, but its effectiveness depends on whether the robot resumes progress after deviations.
3.4
Action Generation
An action generator outputs actions at the policy interface, such as joint or end-effector targets, either as a single action
or an action chunk. The execution system then converts these targets into control inputs at a specified layer. Since regres-
sion, token-based, diffusion, and flow-matching methods can all generate chunks, the action horizon must be specified
independently of the generation method. We define the local policy as:
at:t+H−1 ∼πθ(· | ht, g),
1 ≤K ≤H.
(2)
Here, ht is the interaction history, g is the task condition, and θ represents model parameters. H is the generated action
horizon, while K is the number of steps executed before replanning. Actions may represent poses, joint targets, or other
interface quantities, which are converted into control inputs u as described in Section 6.3. Deterministic regression is
included as a special case. Equal horizons do not necessarily imply equal physical durations or feedback intervals, as these
also depend on the interface frequency.
For a VLA policy, Eq. (2) typically involves a pretrained vision–language backbone that encodes visual, language, and
optional proprioceptive inputs [23, 98], followed by an action-generation head that produces an action chunk. This is a
functional decomposition and does not imply separately trained or executed networks. We denote the task-conditioned
representation by c and the action-head parameters by ψ. Proprioception is optional and can be incorporated through
either component. RT-2 [2] and OpenVLA [28] use transformers for multimodal processing and action generation without
requiring proprioception, whereas π0 [44] and GR00T N1 [99] employ separate action experts. Both input availability and
architectural choices influence policy behavior [42, 100], as further examined by VLANeXt [101] under a unified setup.
Output representation and generation mechanism are separate choices (Figure 10). Action sequence modeling first
distinguishes a generated chunk from its executed prefix (§ 3.4.1). An action chunk can be represented as continuous val-
15


ues, coordinate-wise discrete tokens, or temporally compressed tokens. Continuous commands may be regressed directly
(§ 3.4.2) or generated iteratively (§ 3.4.4); discrete tokens may be predicted in parallel, autoregressively, or through itera-
tive decoding (§ 3.4.3). Discrete outputs must be decoded into numerical commands. The encoding therefore determines
neither the generation process nor the execution horizon. The figure shows representative mechanisms rather than every
encoding–decoder pairing; its direct row is a continuous example, and its execution strip is a separate scheduling choice.
Figure 10: Representative mechanisms for generating an action chunk from condition c: (a) direct continuous prediction, (b)
autoregressive token decoding, (c) iterative token decoding, and (d) continuous diffusion or flow sampling. Discrete tokens are
decoded into numerical commands. Small continuous sketches show command-vector slots; the shared output expands five commands
into seven dimensions: three translation increments, three rotation increments, and one gripper command. The unlettered execution
example uses K = 3 of H = 5 steps before reobservation; local control feedback may continue. Token positions, sampling iterations,
and physical time are distinct.
3.4.1
Action Sequence Modeling
Jointly generating an action chunk captures dependencies across steps, enabling coordinated behaviors such as obstacle
avoidance and bimanual manipulation while reducing inference frequency. However, longer executed prefixes rely on
increasingly outdated observations. In Eq. (2), H defines the predicted horizon and K the executed prefix; selecting K
and connecting successive chunks are execution-level decisions. At a fixed action rate, increasing K lengthens the interval
before the next policy update, while increasing H alone does not set that interval. Local controller feedback may continue
during prefix execution. Figure 10(a)–(d) shows generation alternatives, while the unlettered bottom strip illustrates prefix
execution: the generated horizon H and executed prefix K need not be equal, and new observations inform the next request.
ACT [102] uses a transformer and a training-time latent variable to model demonstration variability (§ 5.2.2), but adopts
the prior-mean latent at inference rather than sampling behaviors. In contrast, Diffusion Policy can sample multiple valid
behaviors [103]. OpenVLA-OFT [104] combines chunking, parallel decoding, and an L1 objective. Thus, temporal mod-
eling and multimodal behavior selection are distinct: chunking alone does not resolve regression-induced mode averaging.
Attention access determines which information each output position can use. Next-token generation uses an available
prefix, whereas a bidirectional refinement step can access the current noisy chunk; both can be conditioned on c. These
dependencies, reusable conditioning, and repeated head computations determine chunk-generation cost (Section 7.2.3).
3.4.2
Direct Action Prediction
Direct action prediction maps task-conditioned representations to numerical control targets, either as single actions or
sequences. Its effectiveness depends on whether the regression objective and conditioning can preserve valid behaviors
when demonstrations contain multiple solutions. Let a = at:t+H−1 denote a demonstrated action chunk of dimension
H × d, and ˆaθ(ht, g) its prediction. OpenVLA-OFT [104] uses the L1 regression objective:
