16


The L1 norm sums absolute errors over all H ·d entries; replacing it with squared L2 error yields the L2 objective. When
demonstrations disagree, L1 favors the coordinate-wise median, whereas L2 favors the mean. This deterministic formula-
tion is a special case of Eq. (2), while probabilistic policies use the likelihood objective in Eq. (6) (§ 5.2.1). Regression
performance depends on how well conditioning determines behavior. When demonstrations contain distinct strategies, L2
may average incompatible trajectories, while L1 may combine coordinates into an invalid chunk. Neither inherently pre-
serves multiple modes, motivating latent-variable, discrete-sequence, and continuous distributional methods. Evaluating
mode averaging requires distinct strategies under matched conditioning, with the loss, H, K, and action-interface rate
specified.
3.4.3
Token-Based Action Generation
Token-based policies discretize continuous actions into tokens, generate them with a sequence model, and decode them
into control commands. RT-2 [2] and OpenVLA [28] leverage VLM vocabularies and training objectives for action pre-
diction. Preserving distinct valid behaviors depends on conditioning, the learned distribution, and decoding. Tokenization
must balance control precision and generation cost, while binning, token order, and temporal compression determine the
dependencies the model learns. These designs separate action representation from generation. Representation determines
whether tokens encode individual coordinates or compressed action segments, while generation determines token depen-
dencies through autoregressive, parallel, or iterative decoding. Hybrid architectures may also combine token-based training
with continuous action generation.
Action Tokenization. RT-2 [2] and OpenVLA [28] autoregressively generate coordinate tokens for a single action (H = 1),
encoding six end-effector displacement components and a gripper command; RT-2 additionally predicts episode termina-
tion. Per-dimension quantization produces long, redundant token sequences, particularly for high-frequency dexterous
actions. FAST [105] addresses this using temporal discrete cosine transforms, coefficient quantization, and byte-pair en-
coding, while retaining autoregressive decoding. This compresses smooth trajectories into fewer tokens. With comparable
backbones and training data, π0-FAST matches flow-matching π0 using roughly five times fewer training GPU-hours,
though autoregressive inference remains sequential. Encoding choices therefore affect token count and reconstruction
error as well as the sequence length presented to the decoder.
Token Decoding. For the task-conditioned representation c and action tokens α1:N, the autoregressive factorization is
pθ(α1:N | c) =
N
Y
j=1
pθ(αj | α<j, c).
(4)
Here, each token αj depends on preceding tokens α<j and c, and the generated sequence is decoded into numerical
actions. For coordinate-wise tokenization, N = H · d, while temporal compression reduces token count. Although this
formulation supports longer chunks, chunking is optional; RT-1 [106] also discretizes actions without autoregressive token
decoding. Tokenization supports heterogeneous outputs but introduces quantization error; autoregressive decoding adds
sequential computation. As H increases, token count and decoding latency grow, with early prediction errors potentially
propagating through the sequence. When trained with teacher forcing, the decoder conditions on demonstrated prefixes
but must use its own generated prefixes at inference, introducing a further source of error beyond quantization.
Action tokens can also be generated in parallel. PD-VLA [107] retains an autoregressive model but updates all tokens in
a chunk through parallel fixed-point iterations. Discrete Diffusion VLA [108] instead starts with fully masked action tokens
and iteratively refines them within the VLM backbone using bidirectional attention, fixing high-confidence tokens while
re-masking uncertain ones. On LIBERO-Goal, instruction paraphrasing reduced its success by only 0.8 points, compared
with 8.0 for parallel discrete decoding. The authors attribute this robustness to sharing the pretrained VLM’s token space
and cross-entropy objective.
Hybrid training connects these choices to continuous action generation: token targets can train a backbone whose fea-
tures condition a separate continuous head. Adding a newly initialized flow-matching or diffusion expert to a pretrained
VLM may slow training and disrupt its knowledge. Knowledge insulation [109] addresses this by training the backbone
on FAST tokens and vision–language data while blocking expert gradients, allowing the flow-matching expert to generate
continuous actions. Thus, discrete and continuous outputs are handled by separate components. Token-based designs can
also be categorized by token type: language, code, latent action, or raw action [110].
3.4.4
Continuous Generative Policies
Continuous generative policies sample action chunks from a learned conditional distribution, capturing multiple strategies
without averaging them, though physical feasibility is not guaranteed. Diffusion heads reverse a gradual noising process,
while flow-matching heads learn a velocity field along a noise-to-data path (Eq. (5)). Standard implementations use iterative
sampling but differ in training objectives and generation dynamics; distilled variants can reduce the number of sampling
steps (§ 7.2.3).
17


Diffusion Heads. Diffusion Policy [103] models visuomotor control as conditional denoising diffusion, learning a condi-
tional score through denoising and iteratively refining noisy action sequences for receding-horizon execution. It reports
an average improvement of 46.9% across 12 tasks from four manipulation benchmarks, attributing its gains to multimodal
action modeling, high-dimensional action handling, and stable training. Training uses noised actions and denoising su-
pervision; inference generates actions through reverse sampling. Diffusion-transformer heads demonstrate the scalability
of action modules. RDT-1B [111] uses a 1.2B-parameter diffusion transformer pretrained on multi-robot data with a
unified action space and fine-tuned on over 6,000 bimanual episodes, enabling skill learning from 1–5 demonstrations.
CogACT [112] attaches a separate diffusion action transformer to a 7B VLM, showing favorable scaling and outperform-
ing OpenVLA by over 35 and 55 percentage points in simulation and real-world tasks, respectively. However, these gains
cannot be attributed solely to head size.
Flow-matching Heads. Flow-matching heads, as in π0 [44], learn a velocity field that transports Gaussian noise into
action chunks. Built on a pretrained VLM, π0 is trained on single-arm, dual-arm, and mobile manipulators for tasks
including laundry folding, table cleaning, and box assembly. Given an action chunk a (Eq. (3)), noise ϵ ∼N(0, I), and
the interpolated state aσ = σϵ + (1 −σ)a at σ ∈[0, 1], a velocity-matching objective for this linear interpolation is
LFM(ψ) = Eσ,ϵ,(ht,g,a)∼D
vψ(aσ, σ, c, sprop
t
) −(ϵ −a)
2.
(5)
Here, vψ predicts velocity conditioned on the task representation c and optional proprioceptive input sprop
t
describing
the body’s configuration and motion, while D denotes demonstrations and ϵ −a is the target velocity. Sampling inte-
grates aσ−∆σ = aσ −∆σ vψ(aσ, σ, c, sprop
t
) from σ = 1 to 0 over Nσ Euler steps, with ∆σ = 1/Nσ. When c remains
fixed, only the action head is repeatedly evaluated. Thus, Nσ controls expert evaluations and can be reduced at deploy-
ment to trade sample quality for latency without retraining, although total latency also includes encoding and runtime
overhead. GR00T N1 [99] applies the same objective to humanoid control, combining a vision–language module with
a flow-matching diffusion-transformer action module. Trained jointly on robot trajectories, human videos, and synthetic
data, it enables language-conditioned bimanual manipulation on the Fourier GR-1 humanoid.
Under the Gaussian linear path, velocity, noise, and clean-action predictions are equivalent parameterizations away from
singular endpoints, e.g., ϵ = aσ + (1 −σ)v. Thus, diffusion and flow matching overlap, differing mainly in path and noise
schedule, loss weighting, and sampling rules rather than inherently stochastic versus smooth control.
The choice between these generative objectives and direct regression requires task-level evidence. Representing multiple
behaviors is not always necessary. On LIBERO, OpenVLA-OFT reports similar average success rates for continuous L1
regression and diffusion heads [104]. This suggests that the need for richer action distributions depends on the task and
whether chunking and output design already address key challenges, without ruling out their benefits for contact-rich,
dynamic, or multimodal tasks.
Action representations and generators should match the task’s behavioral complexity. Direct regression avoids itera-
tive generation but may collapse incompatible trajectory modes unless richer conditioning or strategy selection resolves
ambiguity. Token-based methods leverage sequence modeling at costs shaped by precision, compression, and decoding.
Continuous generative policies preserve multiple behaviors but incur sampling overhead. ACT lies between these ap-
proaches, modeling latent variation during training while using a fixed latent at inference. The key question is how much
distributional complexity remains necessary after conditioning and strategy selection. Separately, adaptive prefixes, event-
triggered updates, and local feedback can improve responsiveness while retaining chunked execution.
3.5
Model and System Architectures
Once methods are assigned to each role, the architectural choice is how to connect them: a shared network can jointly pro-
cess information and generate actions, while separate components expose interfaces for independent updates and execution
rates. In the cup task, planning, VLA control, and memory can be unified or modular. The discussion begins with how
policies and planners connect to action paths and update at different rates (§ 3.5.1), then examines how future prediction
can be coupled to action generation during training or inference (§ 3.5.2). It closes by comparing architectures against
differences in scale, training coverage, action interfaces, and evaluation conditions (§ 3.5.3).
3.5.1
Policy and Planner Organization
Planners can select skills or generate executable programs, as in SayCan and Code as Policies [1, 76]. Alternatively, shared
models can generate action tokens, as in RT-2 and OpenVLA [2, 28], or condition continuous action experts, as in π0 [44],
where the backbone and expert are coupled through attention within one transformer. Components may operate at different
rates. GR00T N1 passes features from a slower vision–language module to a faster action module [99], while π0.5 and
Gemini Robotics 1.5 use explicit subtask text or reasoning [80, 81]. Text provides interpretable intermediate decisions,
whereas latent features rely on downstream interpretation; both may become stale before execution.
18


3.5.2
World–Action Models
World–action models connect future observation or state prediction with action generation. Video provides broad predic-
tive supervision, while robot interaction grounds actions. These targets can be combined through shared training, joint
generation, or predictive features conditioning a policy. The architecture determines whether future prediction is required
at inference. The prediction spaces in Figure 8 can support different roles: evaluating candidate actions, conditioning a
policy, or supplying training targets and imagined experience. A policy may also select actions directly without an explicit
future prediction at inference.
Shared Training Targets. GR-1 [40] pretrains a GPT-style transformer on future-frame prediction and fine-tunes it to
jointly predict images and actions from language, visual history, and proprioception. Video prediction shapes the repre-
sentation, but only actions are needed at inference. GR-2 [113] extends this approach with 38 million video clips, while
Seer [114] conditions inverse-dynamics action prediction on predicted future visual states, requiring prediction at infer-
ence. WorldVLA [115] jointly generates image and action tokens autoregressively, making action quality sensitive to
output ordering, masking, and prediction errors. Thus, the benefits of predictive supervision depend on training objec-
tives, attention structure, and inference dependencies, rather than parameter sharing alone. Ablating a predictive training
objective requires retraining; bypassing an optional inference branch tests computation under fixed learned parameters.
Joint Generation. UWM [116] unifies action and video diffusion in one transformer with modality-specific timesteps.
Setting a timestep to zero conditions on that modality, holding it at maximum noise approximately marginalizes it, and
denoising generates it, enabling policy, forward-dynamics, inverse-dynamics, and video-generation tasks. Cosmos Pol-
icy [117] extends pretrained video generation to actions, future observations, and values, using predictions to evaluate
candidates at inference. While sharing representations across roles, these designs risk correlated errors: agreement among
a policy, simulator, and evaluator sharing the same flawed prediction provides weaker evidence than independent observa-
tion.
Predictive Features as Policy Input. Policies can use predictive features without generating future images. VPP [118]
learns actions from video-model representations, while the Unified Video Action model [119] and GigaWorld-Policy [120]
allow action inference without video generation. LAPA and Genie [121, 122] learn latent actions from action-free videos
but still require embodiment-specific action mapping. Fast-WAM [123] finds that video co-training can help without
inference-time generation. On real-robot towel folding, its inverse-dynamics variant achieves the highest success among
its variants, while action-only inference improves completion time; pretrained π0.5 outperforms both in the reported com-
parison. In contrast, AHEAD uses execution-time prediction to address delays in a frozen policy. These results suggest
that prediction should be used where it informs decisions, without establishing a universal deployment rule.
Across these designs, evaluation should establish whether prediction improves learning, action selection, or data effi-
ciency, specifying what is predicted, which component uses it, how real observations correct errors, and what computation
remains at inference [17]. Images retain visual detail but incur generation and interpretation costs; compact latent states
or physical quantities reduce computation but may omit critical contact information. Compare these representations under
a fixed decision procedure. Online search adds latency and model-error risks, while imagined learning shifts simulation
to training, where short rollouts and real corrections can limit errors. Predictive supervision can support action-only infer-
ence, as Fast-WAM suggests, although its real-world evidence remains limited. Prediction is therefore most useful online
when it changes consequential decisions; it cannot verify task completion. The following discussion examines model scale,
training coverage, and architectural comparisons.
3.5.3
Architecture Comparisons
VLA performance depends on more than parameter count, requiring a balance of semantic capacity, action precision,
horizon, control frequency, and robustness. Compact models such as TinyVLA, SmolVLA, and VLA-Adapter [124–126]
depend on suitable action interfaces and training distributions. Larger models such as RT-2 and π0.5 [2, 80] leverage
broader web, robot, and subtask data, though their gains cannot be attributed solely to size. Cross-embodiment approaches
include body-specific tokenizers (HPT), cross-body training (CrossFormer), soft prompts (X-VLA), and latent actions
(UniVLA) [127–130]. Blind randomized evaluations of large behavior models based on Diffusion Policy [131] show that
multitask pretraining improves success, robustness, and data efficiency on new tasks, with gains increasing under the tested
scale and diversity settings. However, these findings do not establish universal advantages for particular backbone sizes or
action heads.
Representative developments span planning and grounding, robot policies and resources, and world or world–action
models. Early modular systems linked language reasoning to executable skills (SayCan, Code as Policies, Inner Mono-
logue) [1, 76, 91]. Later work expanded shared robot data and reusable policies (Open X-Embodiment, Octo, Open-
VLA) [5, 28, 42], followed by advances in continuous action experts, broader co-training, deployment learning, prediction,
and memory (π0, π0.5, π∗
0.6, Cosmos Policy, MEM) [44, 50, 80, 117, 132]. This historical progression does not imply
replacement, causal lineage, or performance ranking; related surveys cover these overlapping developments [12, 15, 16].
The reviewed architectures differ in training data, action interfaces, and deployment conditions, preventing a definitive
ranking. High LIBERO scores demonstrate progress under specific protocols but do not establish open-world instruction
19


following, recovery, or cross-embodiment robustness. Comparing shared and modular designs therefore requires reporting
the data used to train each role, the action interface, feedback timing, and the physical conditions under which completion is
measured. Two questions remain: what information and prediction horizon are needed for robust decisions across changing
objects, scenes, and embodiments; and whether geometry, memory, prediction, and multimodal action modeling justify
their costs over simpler alternatives under matched data and computation. Section 4 examines the data needed to train and
compare these learned roles.
4
Data for Physical AI
Robots learn skills from experience, but available data is limited and uneven. Real robot interactions capture observations,
actions, and outcomes, yet are costly to collect and cover only specific situations. Images, human videos, simulation, and
synthetic data can expand coverage, but each provides only partial information. Their learning value depends on which
fields are recorded or inferred, how actions correspond to the target robot, and whether outcomes can be checked.
These sources contribute different aspects of a skill: for example, a labeled image identifies a cup, a teleoperated demon-
stration shows how to scrub it, and a failed grasp followed by correction teaches recovery. The chapter first identifies record
fields and learning signals (§ 4.1), providing a common basis for comparing robot interaction data (§ 4.2) with sources
beyond robot interaction (§ 4.3). It then examines how simulation (§ 4.4) and synthetic generation (§ 4.5) expand available
records, before addressing the alignment and integrity checks needed to use these records (§ 4.6). The organization moves
from what a record contains to how it is obtained and what makes it usable; the collection and generation methods can
overlap. Figure 11 connects the chapter’s three organizing questions: what a record contains, which sources can supply it,
and which checks make it usable for learning.
Figure 11: Data sources and record quality. A common record schema organizes robot interaction, non-robot sources, simulation, and
synthetic generation. Alignment and integrity apply across all four sources, whose categories may overlap and whose available fields
may differ.
4.1
Data Records and Learning Signals
A recorded robot trajectory typically contains timestamped observations, action commands, and task conditions, and may
include states, rewards, outcomes, or operator interventions. The discussion first identifies these fields and their learning
signals, then compares their availability across data sources in Table 1, before considering situation coverage, label validity,
and collection cost. Observations support representation learning and prediction, actions supervise policies, outcomes
evaluate consequences, and interventions capture corrections. Not all datasets contain every field, and recorded commands,
measured motion, and inferred actions must remain distinguishable. Action correspondence defines how source actions or
motions map to the target robot’s action interface.
Table 1 summarizes typical fields across sources. Images, annotations, and human videos support visual and temporal
learning [3, 25, 73, 133]; pose and handheld-tool data add motion information [134–137]; and robot datasets provide
actions and body-specific outcomes [5, 138–141]. Simulation and generation further expand coverage under different
validity assumptions [66, 142–144]. Dataset scale, physical correspondence, and collection cost require explicit evaluation.
This source hierarchy connects the data-pyramid perspective of GR00T N1 [99] with the broader survey by Ye et al. [19].

The key question is what missing information each source provides. Diverse object appearances improve recognition,
while corrective demonstrations cover failure states often absent from successful trajectories. Shared representations and
embodiment-aware interfaces help integrate heterogeneous data, as shown by HPT and Octo [42, 127]. Alignment ensures
consistency across recordings, while selection and mixture weights control training exposure. Dataset utility depends
on both situation coverage and available action and outcome labels, whereas collection cost includes human effort and
discarded data.
4.2
Robot Interaction Data
Robot data collection aims to capture missing behaviors and failure states while reducing human effort. The parts are orga-
nized by who controls the robot and what supervision the record supplies: demonstrations provide expert actions (§ 4.2.1),
autonomous collection captures policy-visited states and their outcomes (§ 4.2.2), and interventions record deviations, cor-
rections, and control handoffs (§ 4.2.3). In the cup task, these correspond to human-guided washing, autonomous attempts,
and operator corrections when the cup slips. A single collection process can combine all three.
4.2.1
Demonstration Collection
A demonstration is shaped by what the control interface allows and what the recording preserves. Three factors matter:
the human control interface, diversity across scenes, tasks, and operators, and recorded data channels.
Demonstration Interfaces. In leader–follower teleoperation, operator motions are mapped to robot actions. ALOHA
enables low-cost bimanual demonstrations for training ACT [102], while ALOHA 2 improves hardware robustness and
ergonomics [145]. GELLO uses a kinematically matched input device across robot platforms [146]. Matching leader
and follower joint structures makes motion mapping explicit and facilitates coordinated control. Other interfaces expand
operator perception and control. Open-TeleVision [147] combines stereo VR feedback with head, wrist, and hand pose
retargeting for humanoid teleoperation. Mobile ALOHA [148] integrates waist-guided base motion with bimanual arm
control. Both require explicit mappings from operator motion to robot commands, including coordinate frames, scaling,
constraints, and feedback, as these determine how human motions translate into robot actions. Human motion following
can also produce robot demonstrations. HumanPlus [149] first trains a motion-following policy on human data, then
collects demonstrations through real humanoid execution. Unlike human pose data, these records capture actual robot
behavior, but should preserve deviations between intended and executed motions introduced by the following controller.
Collection Organization. Once an interface is established, collection can scale across scenes, tasks, operators, and robot
platforms. DROID emphasizes diversity across sites and operators [139], while BridgeData V2 covers varied environments,
tasks, and objects on a low-cost platform [138]. Lin et al. [150] show that generalization scales roughly as a power law
with environment and object diversity, whereas additional demonstrations within the same setting yield diminishing returns.
AgiBot World combines large-scale collection with human verification [151], while Open X-Embodiment aggregates data
across heterogeneous robots [5]. However, differences in calibration, timing, annotations, operator practices, and action
interfaces remain. Platform, site, task, and operator diversity therefore require distinct consideration and alignment.
Recorded Modalities. Images and action commands capture only part of an interaction. Motion measurements verify
execution, contact sensing reveals visually ambiguous events, and task and outcome labels support different learning ob-
jectives. RH20T [140] combines visual, force, audio, and action data across robots and viewpoints. The value of these
channels depends on calibration, synchronization, and their availability during training or deployment. Timestamps, ter-
mination causes, and intervention records help interpret mismatches between actions, measurements, and outcomes, while
missing data should be explicitly marked.
21


Demonstration datasets are most useful when they expand behavioral, environmental, or sensing coverage rather than
merely increasing trajectory counts. Repeated demonstrations may yield diminishing returns, while collection costs include
setup, calibration, resets, and rejected data alongside recording time.
4.2.2
Autonomous Collection
Expert demonstrations provide target behavior but rarely cover the states a deployed policy encounters, including unex-
pected failures and recovery situations. Autonomous collection captures these states but requires task selection, outcome
labeling, scene resets, and safety constraints without constant human supervision.
Task Selection. AutoRT [152] automates task selection in unfamiliar environments. A vision–language model describes
the scene, a language model proposes tasks, and a robot constitution filters proposals based on safety and embodiment
constraints. Tasks are matched to the capabilities of the selected collection policy, including teleoperation, scripted control,
or RT-2. However, filtering rejected only 17 of 31 unsafe proposals, requiring human oversight. The dataset includes both
successes and failures, with success rates of 82% for teleoperation, 21% for scripted policies, and below 5% for RT-2.
Outcome Labeling and Resets. QT-Opt [153] demonstrates scalable autonomous collection through simple outcome
labeling and resets. Seven robot arms collected over 580,000 grasp attempts in roughly 800 robot-hours. Success was
automatically determined by comparing bin images before and after grasping, while dropping objects back into the bin
served as a reset. Collection shifted from scripted to learned policies once success reached 50%. Fine-tuning with 28,000
on-policy grasps improved success on unseen objects from 87% to 96%, although the image-based test misclassified some
successful grasps. More complex tasks, such as washing a cup, lack such simple success checks and resets. For tasks
without simple engineered labels or resets, these functions can be learned. RoboCat [154] enables autonomous collection
across tasks using learned success detectors and reset policies. Fine-tuned policies collect task-specific data, while vision-
based reward models trained on crowd-sourced annotations detect success. Reset policies reuse complementary tasks,
such as gear removal after insertion, to restore initial states. Human operators initiate and terminate collection, and stored
episodes train successive generalist models, enabling improvement across generations rather than during execution.
Collection Coupled to Learning. When the learner drives collection, as in SERL and HIL-SERL [8, 155], policy updates
continuously change the collected experience. Episodes may end in success, recoverable failure, unsafe termination, or
reset, each providing distinct information. Recording policy versions, termination causes, and reset procedures is therefore
essential for interpreting outcomes.
Autonomous collection scales only when outcome labeling and resets are reliable. QT-Opt embeds both in the task,
RoboCat learns them, and AutoRT relies on human supervision. Remaining reset and supervision effort must therefore be
included in collection costs.
4.2.3
Intervention Records
Human takeovers can obscure policy failures unless intervention records distinguish human and autonomous control. In
the cup task, an operator may prevent a drop, reposition the cup, or complete the wash, each providing different supervi-
sion. Records should preserve autonomous, intervention, and resumed segments, along with takeover triggers, proposed
and executed actions, and outcomes. These fields enable corrective learning and analysis of control authority and phys-
ical response. Sirius [156] learns from deployment logs using weighted behavior cloning. This requires distinguishing
policy and operator actions, preserving intervention-triggering states, and identifying correction purposes. Protective with-
drawals and task-recovery demonstrations provide different learning signals, making intervention segmentation and intent
essential. HIL-SERL [155] integrates demonstrations, interventions, and autonomous experience, highlighting the need to
preserve action provenance and outcomes. Human takeovers may or may not complete a task, so reward labels, termination
causes, resets, and control handoffs are essential for distinguishing these cases. These records identify expert supervision,
autonomous failures, and resumed autonomy.
Figure 12 follows an autonomous miss, a teleoperated correction, and resumed autonomy within one episode. During
takeover, the proposed policy motion and the action actually executed must remain separately identifiable. A successful
final segment does not retroactively make the episode autonomous; the intervention remains part of both its supervision
and its evaluation record.
Demonstrations, autonomous collection, and interventions provide complementary experience at different human and
robot costs. Demonstrations teach behaviors difficult to discover autonomously, with the control interface determining
what can be shown and recorded. Autonomous collection exposes policy failures but requires reliable outcome checks
and resets. Interventions target deviations with expert corrections, requiring clear control and recovery records. Fewer
interventions do not necessarily indicate improvement if failures increase. The appropriate collection method therefore
depends on the missing behavior, reliability of outcome checks and resets, and total collection cost, rather than episode
count alone.
22


Figure 12: Action provenance within one assisted episode. (a) An autonomous attempt misses the mug. (b) An operator takes control
and corrects the robot motion, so the policy proposal and executed action differ. (c) Autonomous control resumes and the task is
completed. The authority log preserves all three segments, together with takeover triggers, proposed and executed actions, and
outcomes. Completion after an intervention is an assisted episode even when the final segment is autonomous; human teleoperation
here is distinct from physically repositioning the object.
4.3
Data Beyond Robot Interaction
Human data broadens coverage but lacks directly executable robot commands. The discussion moves from image–text
corpora that support object and instruction understanding (§ 4.3.1), to human videos that also reveal temporal task structure
(§ 4.3.2), and then to motion and tool records that measure human movement more directly (§ 4.3.3). In the cup task, these
sources can support recognition, task steps, or movement respectively; using them for robot actions requires additional
correspondence, with increasing demands but no strict ranking of sources. Measured tool motion, mapped robot targets,
and recorded robot outcomes must remain distinct (Table 1); these are part of the source-to-record alignment problem in
Figure 11.
4.3.1
Image–Text Corpora
Image–text corpora provide object recognition and spatial understanding without robot execution, helping robots identify
targets such as “the cup” and their locations. However, they lack the robot states, action commands, forces, and timing
needed to learn executable behavior directly. CLIP learns visual representations aligned with language from image–text
pairs [3], supporting perception and learned rewards. DataComp [157] shows the importance of data filtering: under fixed
training code and compute, a filtered subset of 1.4 billion pairs improved zero-shot ImageNet accuracy by 3.7 points over
a comparable CLIP model. However, benchmark gains do not guarantee preservation of fine-grained features needed for
robotic control, such as cup handles or wet rims, which require robot-level evaluation.
Web captions often lack the metric spatial information needed for robotic control. SpatialVLM [33] generates spatial
question–answer pairs using object detection, depth estimation, and segmentation, enabling quantitative reasoning and
reward annotation, though accuracy depends on depth estimates. RT-2 [2] and π0.5 [80] integrate web vision–language
data with robot training. In π0.5, removing web data had little effect on familiar mock-home tasks but reduced generalization
to unseen object categories, highlighting the importance of data mixture weights. Evaluation should therefore distinguish
recognition and instruction following on novel objects from physical execution. Web data provides semantic knowledge
but not embodiment-specific control parameters, such as grasping force.
4.3.2
Human Activity Videos
Human activity videos capture motion, contact, object changes, and temporal order absent from static images, but lack
executable robot commands. First- and third-person views provide complementary interaction information. Videos can
support representation learning, subgoal extraction, and action inference, each requiring progressively stronger correspon-
dence to the target robot.
Representation Learning. Ego4D [133] provides diverse egocentric daily-life videos for understanding past, present, and
future activities, with some recordings including audio, gaze, 3D meshes, and multi-camera views. R3M [4] uses Ego4D
for representation learning through time-contrastive and video–language objectives. Such videos capture task sequences
and scene changes without robot action labels, reducing reliance on robot execution while still requiring capture, consent,
23


annotation, and curation efforts.
Plans and Subgoals. MimicPlay [158] learns latent plans from human play videos to guide a low-level controller trained on
limited robot demonstrations. Across 14 long-horizon real-world tasks, it improves success, generalization, and robustness
over imitation baselines. Human videos provide goal sequences, while robot demonstrations teach their execution, as
further reviewed by Ma et al. [159]. This transfer depends on whether the robot can physically achieve the demonstrated
goals.
Action Recovery. Visible hand motion does not directly translate into robot actions. Two approaches infer action-like sig-
nals from unlabeled videos. Latent-action methods, such as LAPA [121], encode visual changes and ground them in robot
actions using limited demonstrations (Section 5.1.2). UniVLA [130] further uses DINOv2 features and language guidance
to suppress irrelevant visual variation. Point-tracking methods, such as ATM [160], predict future point trajectories from
images and instructions, then use limited robot demonstrations to train policies. ATM outperformed video-pretraining
baselines across over 130 tasks, including transfer from human videos with relatively small domain gaps. However, latent
codes and 2D tracks remain pseudo-labels that require grounding in robot actions.
4.3.3
Motion and Tool Records
Unlike videos that capture motion as pixels, hand-tracking and handheld-tool records directly measure human motion.
Hand tracking captures body and finger movements, while handheld grippers record task-space trajectories and tool states.
Both require temporal alignment, coordinate mapping, and feasibility checks for robot execution. Unlike robot demonstra-
tions collected through real-time motion following, these records originate from human or handheld-device actions.
Tracked Hands and Gloves. DexCap [137] tracks wrist and finger motion with camera-based localization and occlusion-
resistant electromagnetic sensing, and it also records 3D observations of the scene. Its DexIL algorithm replicates the
human actions on a robot hand through inverse kinematics and point-cloud imitation. It is evaluated on six dexterous
tasks, with optional human-in-the-loop correction during rollouts. The measurement is direct, but each motion must still
be retargeted to a robot hand with different proportions and joints.
Hand-motion records can also be collected at larger scale with egocentric tracking. Head-mounted capture enables
scalable hand-motion collection without gloves or external trackers. EgoDex [134] pairs egocentric video with 3D hand
tracking across tabletop tasks, evaluating trajectory prediction rather than robot execution. EgoScale [135] trains a VLA
model on 20,854 hours of action-labeled human video, finding log-linear scaling of validation loss and a correlation with
real-robot performance. After human–robot alignment and task adaptation, it improves success by approximately 54 per-
centage points over no pretraining on a 22-DoF hand. Even its one-robot-demonstration setting uses additional aligned
human data. Thus, human-data scaling reduces robot demonstration needs but still depends on alignment quality and
interaction coverage.
Whole-body Motion Capture. For humanoids, whole-body motion capture provides movement data. AMASS [161]
unifies marker-based datasets using a common parametric body model but lacks object interaction and force information.
Transferring these motions to robots requires retargeting for body proportions, joint limits, and balance.
Handheld Gripper Tools. UMI [136] collects manipulation demonstrations using handheld grippers with wrist cam-
eras, linking human recordings to robot execution through relative trajectories and latency matching. Its policies transfer
across robot arms and generalize to unseen environments and objects. Shared gripper geometry reduces retargeting needs,
but calibration, motion feasibility, and timing alignment remain essential. Actions beyond the instrumented tool require
additional sensing or annotation for supervision.
Non-robot sources differ in the knowledge they supply and the correspondence needed for transfer. Web data support
semantics; human videos provide representations, subgoals, or inferred actions; motion and tool records offer more direct
action correspondence when geometry matches. All require task-level validation, with robot-specific mapping, calibration,
or target data as needed. Missing force and contact measurements may require robot interaction, and human demonstrations
may omit failures caused by robot geometry and control. Source selection should therefore follow the missing knowledge
and supervision needed for executable behavior. Simulation provides a complementary route through controlled interac-
tions that deliberately vary configurations, contacts, and challenging states.
4.4
Data Collection in Simulation
Simulation supports perception, task planning, and policy learning, each requiring different levels of fidelity. The discus-
sion separates the physics simulation that governs transitions and contact (§ 4.4.1) from environment construction that
determines objects, layouts, tasks, and coverage (§ 4.4.2). Slip-sensitive manipulation depends on accurate contact dynam-
ics, while household tasks benefit from diverse objects and layouts. Under fixed compute, more detailed physics limits the
number and diversity of simulated interactions. These task-dependent fidelity requirements call for validation of physical
transitions, task outcomes, action interfaces, and control timing as well as visual appearance.
24


4.4.1
Physics Simulation
Small physics errors can cause sim-to-real failures, such as a grasp that succeeds in simulation but slips on the real robot.
Physics simulators model dynamics, contact, actuation, and sensing under explicit assumptions. Their required fidelity
depends on the task: friction and compliance are critical for contact-rich manipulation but less important for free-space
motion. Rigid-body simulators model motion and contact through discrete integration, friction models, and constraint
solvers. Their accuracy depends on friction, compliance, collision geometry, and actuator latency, while policies may
exploit numerical artifacts absent in reality. More complex interactions require deformable and fluid simulation. Soft-
Gym [162] uses particle-based models for cloth, rope, and liquids, but simplifies grasping through particle attachment or
direct object control. OmniGibson [163] supports fluids, deformable objects, and cloth, enabling over half of BEHAVIOR-
1K’s 1,000 activities that would otherwise be unsupported. However, cleaning remains rule-based: dirt particles disappear
upon tool contact, and soaking depends on liquid-particle contact. Thus, simulated cleaning success does not necessarily
reflect realistic scrubbing forces or physical cleaning dynamics.
GPU-based frameworks expand data collection: Isaac Gym integrates physics and learning; Isaac Lab adds rendering,
sensors, and collection tools; ManiSkill3 combines GPU physics and rendering for manipulation; MuJoCo Playground
builds on MJX with batched environments [6, 164–166]. Their throughput does not remove physics errors. Resource
bottlenecks are discussed in § 7.6.1. Simulators provide privileged states and controllable parameters for automatic la-
beling, curricula, and teacher–student training, as demonstrated in quadrupedal locomotion [167, 168] and Rubik’s-cube
manipulation [169]. Domain randomization expands training diversity [170], while Eureka uses language models and
simulator code to generate reward functions [171]. These applications determine which states and parameters simulators
must expose.
SimOpt [172] calibrates simulator parameters using paired real and simulated trajectories, requiring data that captures
relevant dynamics. Lin et al. [173] extend this to humanoid arms and hands by matching joint trajectories across real and
simulated robots with varied dynamics parameters. Using under four minutes of real data, eight of ten policies trained
with the best-matching model transferred a real grasp, compared with none using the worst model. However, calibration
primarily captures robot dynamics, while hand–object contact remains approximated through primitive shapes. Simulation
evaluation should distinguish observation, state-transition, and action-execution gaps, as each requires different transfer
methods. Simulator adequacy depends on whether task-critical errors remain within policy tolerance, verified through
paired real-world tests rather than simulation throughput or visual realism alone.
4.4.2
Environment Construction
Accurate physics alone cannot ensure generalization without diverse environments. Assets, layouts, robots, sensors, initial
conditions, and success criteria define training coverage. Environments can be generated procedurally or constructed from
real-scene references; reference-based construction may reproduce a particular scene or preserve its relevant affordances
using similar assets.
Procedural Scenes. ProcTHOR [174] procedurally generates diverse indoor environments by varying layouts, lighting,
object placements, colors, and textures. Scaling training environments from 10 to 10,000 houses improved downstream
performance, highlighting the value of scene diversity for household tasks.
Construction from Real-scene References. RialTo [175] reconstructs real scenes as digital twins, transfers real demon-
strations into simulation through inverse distillation, and improves policies with simulated reinforcement learning before
hardware deployment. This enables additional practice with varied object poses, distractors, and disturbances without
further real-world collection. However, transfer depends on accurate scene geometry and articulation, and the digital
twin remains specific to the reconstructed environment. A reference can also guide construction without an exact replica.
ACDC [176] generates “digital cousins” from a single RGB image by matching detected objects to similar simulation as-
sets while preserving geometric and semantic affordances. In cabinet manipulation, an exact digital twin achieved 100%
simulated but only 25% real-world success; stronger randomization improved real success to 55%, while digital cousins
reached 90%. Simulation suites such as LIBERO [177] and RoboCasa [144] also provide standardized benchmarks.
Simulator design balances physical fidelity, environment coverage, and calibration cost. Scene diversity, including
digital cousins, can reduce overfitting to simulation-specific details more effectively than exact reconstruction, but adequate
contact physics remains necessary. Detailed modeling matters when contact or actuation errors affect decisions; targeted
calibration and robust control may address specific discrepancies more efficiently. Hardware-in-the-loop testing and real-
world trials complement these choices by revealing timing, interface, and dynamics errors.
4.5
Synthetic Data Generation
Synthetic data reduces collection costs only when generated action and outcome labels remain valid. The preceding sub-
section examines interactive simulation environments; here, the organizing question is how training records are created or
transformed, including methods that use simulation. The discussion covers transforming existing demonstrations (§ 4.5.1),
generating scenes and appearances (§ 4.5.2), or producing new behavioral videos or trajectories (§ 4.5.3). For the cup task,
25


these change positions, visual conditions, or behavior respectively. This order distinguishes reuse of an existing behavior,
modification of its observation context, and generation of behavioral records; depending on the method, actions may be
preserved, executed after transformation, or inferred from generated observations. Greater visual diversity supplies useful
action supervision only when correspondence remains valid.
4.5.1
Demonstration Augmentation
A single demonstration covers only one object pose, such as washing a cup at a fixed position. Demonstration augmen-
tation extends this behavior to new poses by preserving the gripper–object relationship rather than copying the original
actions. The pipeline identifies invariant coordinate relations and contact constraints, transforms the reference trajectory,
and verifies that the resulting behavior remains successful.
Transformation with Execution Validation. MimicGen [142] divides demonstrations into object-centered subtasks,
adapts trajectories to new object poses, and retains successful executions. From roughly 200 human demonstrations, it
generates over 50,000 demonstrations across 18 tasks, varying scenes, objects, and robot arms. Policies trained on this
data perform well on long-horizon and precision tasks, including multi-part assembly, and compare favorably with collect-
ing additional human demonstrations.
Generation alternates between trajectory transformation and execution at each subtask boundary. For example, after
grasping a cup at a new position, its pose is re-observed before moving it to the sink. Each source segment is transformed
for the current scene, executed, and followed by a fresh state estimate before transforming the next segment. Successful
full-task executions are retained as generated demonstrations. Because later transformations depend on earlier executions,
object poses must be estimated at every boundary. Key limitations include invalid transformation assumptions (e.g., shape-
dependent grasps), low success rates under difficult initial conditions, and simulator-to-real-world gaps. Success filtering
also biases the generated dataset toward already feasible behaviors. Importantly, the method stores executed trajectories
rather than transformed action sequences, making it an augmentation of experience rather than labels.
The same augmentation principle extends to coordinated and dexterous manipulation. DexMimicGen [143] extends
demonstration generation to humanoids with dexterous hands, producing 21,000 demonstrations from 60 sources across
simulated bimanual tasks with varying coordination demands. It also evaluates generation and policy-learning choices and
demonstrates a real-to-sim-to-real pipeline for humanoid can sorting. Like MimicGen, it evaluates data quality through
downstream policy performance. However, bimanual manipulation imposes additional constraints: transforming one arm’s
motion restricts the other’s, reducing the space of feasible configurations.
Editing Without Execution. DemoGen [178] eliminates real-world execution during generation. From a single human
demonstration, it transforms contact segments with the object, replans free-space motions, and updates point clouds ac-
cordingly. This enables computation-only augmentation but relies on planning and controller assumptions rather than
execution-based success filtering. Single-view point clouds further limit appearance consistency, causing performance
gains to saturate.
Overall, demonstration augmentation generalizes existing skills to new conditions but depends on accurate object poses,
subtask segmentation, and transferable action relations. It cannot directly handle new grasp or contact strategies, and
subtask recombination alone does not create fundamentally new skills.
4.5.2
Scene and Appearance Generation
Visual editing is inexpensive, but preserving action validity is challenging. Appearance changes, such as recoloring a
counter, can preserve the original action, while geometric changes, such as altering a cup’s shape, may invalidate its grasp.
Two approaches introduce visual diversity under this constraint: editing recorded episode images and generating assets for
simulated scenes.
ROSIE [179] uses text-guided diffusion to edit robot demonstration images while preserving recorded actions. It detects
and masks target regions, excluding the robot arm, and replaces objects, containers, or backgrounds, updating instructions
when needed. In one experiment, 779 can-placement episodes were edited to replace a drawer with a sink, enabling a policy
to achieve 60% success on real sink placement versus 0% without augmentation. However, unchanged actions remain valid
only if the edited geometry supports the original motion. ROSIE modifies appearance rather than motion or physics, and
frame-wise editing can introduce temporal inconsistencies.
RoboCasa [144] diversifies simulated kitchens using text-to-3D object assets and text-to-image textures while preserving
physics-based interaction. However, generated geometry and materials can affect contact dynamics and sim-to-real transfer,
requiring physical validation beyond visual inspection. Across these appearance-generation approaches, the added visual
diversity still relies on recorded or simulated actions for supervision.
4.5.3
Synthetic Behavioral Data
A realistic robot video does not necessarily provide executable actions. Synthetic behavioral data must link observations
to actions over time, specifying task conditions, motion sources, action labels, and validation procedures. Two main
26


approaches differ in how actions are obtained: executing generated tasks in simulation or inferring actions from generated
videos.
Simulator-executed Behavior. RoboGen [180] uses language models to generate tasks, configure scenes, select assets,
decompose tasks, and define rewards. It then solves subtasks through reinforcement learning, motion planning, or tra-
jectory optimization in simulation. Although the resulting actions are executable in simulation, task specifications may
be incorrect: 6 of 155 generated tasks contained faulty decompositions or rewards. Large-scale skill verification and the
sim-to-real gap remain key limitations.
Video-generated Behavior. DreamGen [66] generates photorealistic robot videos across tasks and environments, then re-
covers pseudo-actions using latent action or inverse-dynamics models to train policies. From teleoperation data for a single
pick-and-place task, a humanoid learns 22 new behaviors across seen and unseen environments. DreamGen evaluates data
through downstream policy success, with benchmark scores correlating positively with RoboCasa performance. Unlike
execution-based filtering in § 4.5.1, this does not validate individual trajectories. The method still requires robot data for
action inference and manually supplied initial frames, while generation cost and task complexity remain limitations.
The two approaches have distinct trade-offs: simulation provides executable actions but inherits specification errors and
sim-to-real gaps, whereas video generation offers realistic visual diversity but relies on inferred actions without verified
physics. Ultimately, data quality depends on validation and downstream real-world performance, not generation volume
alone.
Across demonstration augmentation, observation generation, and behavioral generation, the key issue is action-label va-
lidity. Demonstration augmentation extends existing skills but cannot supply missing contact strategies without additional
source behavior. Observation generation adds visual diversity, while geometry changes require action revalidation. Behav-
ioral generation offers greater novelty but requires action inference, filtering, and physical validation; direct collection may
remain more efficient for complex contacts. Generated records should retain their source, transformation, and action-label
provenance so that curation can distinguish valid variations from unverified new behavior.
4.6
Data Alignment and Record Integrity
Data preparation first aligns observations, actions, and outcomes across space, time, and embodiment (§ 4.6.1), then checks
record quality to distinguish valid failures from corrupted or unsupported labels (§ 4.6.2). This order matters because
selection and sampling determine what the learner sees only after correspondence and integrity are established. Each
record must also match its training objective: an image labeled “cup” supports recognition but lacks the actions and next
states needed for imitation or dynamics learning.
4.6.1
Data Alignment
Action vectors of equal length may differ in physical meaning, reference frame, or control frequency, which normal-
ization alone cannot resolve. Before combining datasets, alignment must establish consistent semantics across four di-
mensions: spatial alignment (coordinate frames), action alignment (command meanings), temporal alignment (measure-
ment–execution timing), and embodiment metadata (robot configuration).
Combining 15 Hz measured base-frame poses with 10 Hz commanded camera-frame displacements requires spatial,
action, temporal, and embodiment alignment. Coordinate transforms reconcile reference frames, while measured motion
must remain distinct from commanded actions. Streams are synchronized before resampling, and embodiment metadata
distinguish grippers from dexterous hands. Resampling, discretization, and cross-embodiment mapping may irreversibly
lose contact details, precision, or finger motion, so these conversions must remain traceable in training records.
Formats, metadata, and versioning support data alignment and traceability. Open X-Embodiment [5] standardizes
datasets from 21 institutions but still requires compatible action and sensor fields, as illustrated by Re-Mix’s normalization
and discretization. Similarly, DROID’s multiview data [139] may not all be used by a given policy. Each policy-specific
view must distinguish decision-time inputs from supervision targets and record target availability and action source sepa-
rately. LeRobot [181] integrates timestamps and embodiment metadata into its robot-learning framework, enabling consis-
tent training and deployment. However, metadata make misalignment detectable rather than impossible. Version tracking
must preserve repairs, data subsets, transformations, and separate paper, code, and data versions and usage terms, since
citing the same dataset does not guarantee identical training data.
4.6.2
Data Quality
A dropped cup and a frozen camera may both yield unsuccessful episodes, but only the former provides useful behavioral
information. Quality processing must distinguish corrupted records (e.g., sensor failures, timestamp errors, calibration
drift, or invalid actions) from informative task failures, interventions, and recoveries. Integrity checks should precede
outcome labeling, preserving valid failures that reveal the limits of robot competence while excluding unusable data.
BridgeData V2 [138] supports both offline reinforcement learning and imitation, while MimicGen [142] retains success-
ful executions for behavior cloning. Quality criteria therefore depend on training objectives rather than a universal success
27


threshold: failed episodes can support reward learning, while their successful segments may still provide imitation targets.
Failures, interventions, and safety constraints reveal competence boundaries, especially when their causes are recorded.
RoboMIND [141] provides 107k teleoperated trajectories across 479 tasks and four embodiments, plus 5k cause-annotated
real-world failures for policy correction. REFLECT [93] automates failure explanation using hierarchical multisensory
summaries and language models. Learned success detectors, as used in RoboCat, can scale outcome labeling but require
evaluation of false accepts and rejects to ensure reliable data selection.
Cleaning must preserve three labels: record integrity, task outcome, and control authority. Human interventions should
also distinguish pre-intervention, intervention, and post-intervention segments. This enables reuse for imitation, reward
learning, and failure detection without attributing human corrections to the policy, while supporting sustained-execution
evaluation.
Alignment and integrity checks establish usable observation–label relationships, while selection and sampling determine
the learner’s data distribution. Valid failures may remain informative without corrective actions, whereas corrupted records
require repair or exclusion.
Different data sources provide different supervision: visual data support representation learning, robot interactions
provide action–outcome relationships, and generated data expand coverage only when these relationships remain valid.
The resulting training mixture must preserve action source, outcome evidence, and the transformations applied to each
record; Section 5 examines how learning methods use these signals.
5
Learning and Adaptation
Training updates representations, predictions, policies, or value models from experience. This differs from updating
execution-time state or memory without changing parameters. Figure 13 separates three axes of learning and adapta-
tion. Representation learning uses visual or predictive targets (§ 5.1), imitation fits demonstrated actions (§ 5.2), and
reinforcement learning optimizes rewards (§ 5.3). The same records can support several objectives. Pretraining and post-
training describe when objectives are applied (§ 5.4). Transfer identifies changes in tasks, environments, or bodies (§ 5.5),
while continual learning concerns accumulation and retention across successive changes (§ 5.6). A policy can therefore
be pretrained by imitation, refined with rewards, and updated with replay to retain earlier skills.
Figure 13: Three axes of learning and adaptation. Objectives describe what is learned, stages locate pretraining and post-training, and
change over time distinguishes transfer and adaptation from continual learning. The rows organize complementary choices, not
successive steps.
5.1
Representation Learning
Representation learning moves from general image and image–text objectives (§ 5.1.1) to temporal and action-conditioned
objectives (§ 5.1.2). The comparison asks what each signal adds for control, separately from encoder adaptation and
deployment-time prediction. Figure 14 compares predictive, imitation, and reward-based objectives through their targets
and parameter updates. These objectives can be combined at different training stages.
5.1.1
General Visual Objectives
Web-image pretraining learns features that may not capture grasp-relevant details. Two common approaches, image-text
alignment and image-only self-supervised learning, preserve different types of information.
Vision–language Correspondence. CLIP and SigLIP learn visual-semantic representations from image-text pairs [3, 23].
However, action learning must still connect objects to robot actions, and localization accuracy depends on the spatial detail
retained by the encoder.
Self-supervised Visual Structure. Self-supervised methods like DINOv2 learn visual features without captions or action
labels [24], while masked reconstruction encourages spatial information by predicting missing patches. MVP [182] uses
this objective to pretrain a vision transformer on 4.5 million images, then freezes the encoder and trains a controller with few
demonstrations. Its comparisons show better transfer than CLIP, supervised ImageNet pretraining, or training from scratch
28


Figure 14: Learning objectives illustrated through a mug task. (a) Predictive learning compares a future prediction with an
observation recorded at the same future time. (b) Imitation compares a policy proposal with an expert reference under the same current
information; arrows schematically depict motion. (c) Reward-based learning uses evaluated outcomes to update policies or value
models. Dashed arrows denote parameter updates. Future observations are training targets, not deployment inputs. Objectives can be
combined across training stages; the scenes are illustrative rather than experimental results.
on tested tasks, highlighting the importance of pretraining objectives beyond encoder capacity. However, demonstrations
are still needed to identify action-relevant features.
The control benefit of either objective depends on adaptation and evaluation. Two studies qualify these gains. Hansen et
al. [183] find that a simple encoder trained from scratch with random-shift augmentation can match frozen pretrained repre-
sentations across diverse tasks. They attribute pretrained models’ limitations to domain gaps, which fine-tuning can reduce,
while freezing mainly saves training cost. Similarly, the VC-1 and CortexBench study [184] finds no universally superior
encoder across 17 tasks, though task-specific adaptation substantially improves performance. Thus, encoder comparisons
depend on adaptation protocols and demonstration budgets. For control, representations must preserve action-relevant
distinctions, not merely object categories.
5.1.2
Interaction and Predictive Objectives
Interaction data support learning temporal structure and learning the relation between actions and state changes. Within
either group, predictions may use pixels or latent features; the prediction space is a separate choice from whether actions
are observed. Force or touch signals provide additional contact supervision when recorded.
Temporal Structure from Observations.
R3M [4] learns visual representations from human videos using time-
contrastive learning, video–language alignment, and sparsity regularization. Its frozen encoder supports robot learning
from 20 demonstrations. In contrast, GR-1 [40] uses video generation pretraining and retains future-image prediction
during robot fine-tuning. While R3M learns temporal relationships between representations and GR-1 predicts future
visual content, both provide temporal information without directly learning robot motor commands. Temporal prediction
can also operate in feature space. V-JEPA 2 predicts masked video representations, with an action-conditioned variant for
image-goal planning [73]. Avoiding unpredictable pixel details can reduce irrelevant modeling, but latent features must
still retain action-critical geometry.
Relations Between Actions and State Changes. With aligned state–action transitions, forward models predict pϕ(zt+1 |
zt, at) (§ 3.2.1), while inverse models infer actions from surrounding states or observations. Forward prediction supports
action evaluation and imagined experience, as in PlaNet and Dreamer [39, 72]. Inverse prediction can turn unlabeled
video into action-labeled data. Both depend on action representations, such as joint angles or end-effector motions, and
the time interval between states. Inverse or latent-action prediction can supply labels for further learning. VPT trains
inverse dynamics on labeled Minecraft gameplay, then labels a larger video corpus for imitation [185]. LAPA learns
discrete latent actions from frame transitions and maps them to robot commands using limited trajectories [121]. Such
labels expand training access but can lose embodiment-specific detail; LAPA underperforms true action supervision on
fine-grained tasks such as grasping.
Across visual and interaction objectives, general pretraining supplies reusable visual knowledge; interaction data and
encoder adaptation address missing control-relevant distinctions. Predictive targets help when they capture consequences
beyond action labels. Comparisons should hold parameter updates and deployment-time prediction fixed to isolate this
benefit, rather than treating richer reconstruction as inherently better for control.
29


5.2
Imitation Learning
Imitation learning trains policies from reference behavior. The account starts with behavior cloning, which fits actions
in existing demonstrations (§ 5.2.1), then examines objectives for preserving alternative valid behaviors under similar
conditions (§ 5.2.2). Interactive imitation adds expert supervision at states the learned policy visits (§ 5.2.3). This sequence
separates errors in fitting available actions, averaging distinct strategies, and policy-induced distribution shift; the methods
can be combined.
5.2.1
Behavior Cloning
Behavior cloning learns a policy from demonstrated observation–action pairs and task conditions. Demonstrations capture
hard-to-specify behaviors, such as how firmly to grasp a wet cup. Supervision can use trajectories, action chunks, task
segments, or labeled skills, with targets predicting either individual actions or chunks (Eq. (2)). For probabilistic policies,
behavior cloning minimizes the negative log-likelihood of demonstrated actions:
LBC(θ) = −E(h,g,a)∼D

log πθ(a | h, g)

.
(6)
Here, D denotes demonstrations, h the history, g the task condition, and a a single action or chunk (Eq. (2)). Determin-
istic policies use regression losses, while diffusion and flow matching use specialized objectives (Eq. (5)). All variants
share a limitation: training covers demonstrated states, not unseen states arising from the policy’s own errors. Deploy-
ment is sequential, so each action affects subsequent states. Small errors, such as a misaligned grasp, can lead to unseen
observations, causing policy-induced distribution shift. Thus, low imitation error does not guarantee closed-loop success,
which depends on whether demonstrations cover the states encountered during execution.
The robomimic study [186] compares six offline algorithms across five simulated and three real multi-stage manipulation
tasks. Results highlight the importance of algorithm design, demonstration quality, and training duration, as low validation
loss does not guarantee task success. Offline human demonstrations also solve tasks that the compared reinforcement-
learning methods could not. Imitation failures should therefore be diagnosed in terms of data coverage, history or chunk
length, training choices, and deployment distribution shift rather than supervised learning itself. The data-collection study
by Lin et al. [150] distinguishes diversity across environments and objects from repeated demonstrations under the same
conditions. It suggests prioritizing new conditions when additional repetitions yield diminishing returns, depending on
deployment coverage. Coverage alone is insufficient, however: demonstrations may contain errors and varying operator
styles, so validation loss must be complemented by full-task rollouts that assess sustained progress.
5.2.2
Learning Multimodal Behavior
Demonstrations may contain distinct valid strategies, such as grasping a cup by its handle or rim. Averaging these actions
can produce an invalid grasp. While action representations determine what a policy can express, multimodal objectives
preserve alternative strategies, and chunking improves temporal coordination without preventing averaging [102, 103].
The objectives below preserve alternatives through latent variables, denoising or transport, discrete mixtures, or an energy
function. Decoding must also maintain strategy consistency.
Latent-variable Models. ACT learns a conditional variational model of action sequences [102], but fixes its latent variable
to the prior mean during evaluation. Its success therefore does not demonstrate sampling of distinct strategies.
Diffusion and Flow Objectives. Diffusion Policy instead generates action sequences through iterative denoising [103].
Both offer richer action distributions than direct prediction, but preserving distinct valid strategies depends on conditioning,
training, and test-time decoding. The π0 action expert uses flow matching (Eq. (5), § 3.4.4) to learn a velocity field between
Gaussian noise and demonstrated action chunks [44]. At inference, it integrates this field in ten steps from sampled noise,
allowing different samples to produce distinct strategies rather than averaged actions. However, the objective does not
guarantee coverage or sampling of every demonstrated strategy.
Discrete Mixtures. Behavior Transformers [187] address action averaging by clustering actions into k bins, predicting a
bin from observation history, and regressing a continuous offset.
Energy Models. Implicit behavioral cloning [188] instead learns an energy function that favors demonstrated actions,
representing multimodal mappings through inference-time optimization. On the Insert-Blue task (1 mm tolerance), it
achieves 83.3% versus 6.7% for MSE regression.
Action sequence length and overlapping-prediction aggregation are separate design choices (§§ 3.4.1 and 7.3.3). Multi-
modal policies should be evaluated by strategy-specific success and consistency rather than average trajectory error. Rep-
resenting multiple strategies does not prevent mid-execution switching, while interactive supervision can address failures
caused by distribution shift.
30


5.2.3
Interactive Imitation
Interactive imitation adds expert supervision at learner-visited states. Algorithms can query labels, robots can request help,
or humans can take control; each route trades coverage, safety, and expert effort.
Expert Queries. DAgger aggregates expert labels on states reached by the learner and retrains the policy [7]. Its improved
error bounds rely on no-regret and reduction assumptions and achievable loss at visited states, rather than guaranteeing
finite-data performance.
Robot-gated Requests. Labeling every state requires substantial expert effort. ThriftyDAgger [189] reduces this burden
by requesting interventions at novel states, identified through policy-ensemble disagreement, or risky states, identified
through estimated task-completion probability. Thresholds control the intervention rate, making effectiveness dependent
on the accuracy of both estimates.
Human-gated Takeover. HG-DAgger lets experts intervene when the policy struggles and learns an uncertainty-based
safety threshold. BC-Z scales human-gated collection across tasks, using corrections to teach recovery [190, 191].
After collection, intervention records can be selected and weighted independently of who requested help. Sirius uses
intervention history to approximate trust and weight behavior-cloning samples [156]. Thus, collection authority, query
timing, and sample weighting are separate choices; aggregation helps only when corrections are accurate and cover relevant
failures.
Across the imitation-learning methods, richer distributions and temporal conditioning improve fitting to available be-
havior, while corrective demonstrations supply missing supervision at deployment states. Both need consistent execution.
When expert actions are costly but outcomes are verifiable, reward-based learning offers another source of improvement.
5.3
Reinforcement Learning
Imitation learning fits demonstrated actions, while reinforcement learning (RL) optimizes outcomes through rewards. The
discussion first asks how rewards are constructed and checked (§ 5.3.1), then separates learning from existing interaction
records (§ 5.3.2) from learning with new policy interactions (§ 5.3.3). Policy learning in imagined environments uses
predictive models to add training experience while remaining limited by model coverage (§ 5.3.4). These routes differ in
available feedback and interaction cost, and can be combined with demonstrations and corrections. Tang et al. [20] survey
practical challenges in real-robot RL. The RL objective maximizes expected return:
J(θ) = Eτ∼πθ
