.
(7)
Here, τ is a trajectory generated by the policy and environment, T the horizon, rt the reward, and γ ∈[0, 1] the discount
factor. Unlike behavior cloning (Eq. (6)), RL learns from outcomes rather than action labels and can optimize rewards
using either existing logs or new interactions.
5.3.1
Reward Construction
Rewards may use measured state, fitted judgments, or pretrained models. Dense feedback can aid exploration, but every
source can omit relevant conditions or introduce exploitable errors.
Functions of Measured State. Learning to Walk in Minutes combines terms for tracking, motion, effort, collisions, and
contact [168]. Such terms are cheap in simulation, but unmeasured conditions remain unprotected: object distance can be
computed from poses, whereas cleanliness requires other evidence.
Judgments Fitted to Examples. HIL-SERL fits a success classifier and adds examples to correct errors. VICE instead
updates its classifier adversarially against policy-visited negatives; PEBBLE learns graded rewards from pairwise prefer-
ences and relabels replay as judgments change [155, 192, 193]. These differ in label collection and how they respond to
changing policy behavior. Extending example-based rewards to robot video, RoboReward combines natural failures, coun-
terfactual negatives, and partial-progress examples from robot video [194]. Its trained models outperform larger VLMs on
tested short-horizon tasks, emphasizing failure coverage rather than model size alone.
Pretrained Models as Reward Sources. Eureka generates inspectable reward code from simulator code and task descrip-
tions; VLM-RM directly scores observations with CLIP [171, 195]. Both need independent outcome checks. Accuracy on
existing records does not ensure reliability as optimization drives the policy into new states or exploits reward shortcuts.
5.3.2
Offline Reinforcement Learning
Offline RL uses logged actions and outcomes without new interaction. The methods below distinguish value-based learning,
whose estimates need protection against unsupported actions, from return-conditioned action prediction. Conservative
penalties, in-support updates, and sequence-based value models are choices within the value-based group; none creates
evidence for outcomes absent from the data.
31


Value-based Learning. CQL [196] adds a regularizer to the Bellman error that lowers Q-values for actions outside the
dataset, producing conservative value estimates. Its lower-bound and policy-improvement guarantees depend on specific
assumptions and update procedures. Excessive conservatism may limit improvement, making regularization strength an
important design choice. IQL instead learns values through upper expectile regression and Bellman backups without
evaluating unseen actions during value learning, then extracts a policy through advantage-weighted cloning [197]. Function
approximation can still produce unsupported actions.
The value model can also use a sequence representation. Q-Transformer tokenizes action dimensions for transformer-
based Q-learning on heterogeneous records [198]. It penalizes unsupported actions and uses Monte Carlo returns as lower
bounds; scaling the value model does not remove data-support limits. Cal-QL [199] supports offline-to-online fine-tuning
by retaining CQL’s conservative penalty while calibrating Q-values against a reference policy’s returns, preventing exces-
sive pessimism and loss of offline knowledge during online updates. ConRFT adopts this approach for offline initialization.
Return-conditioned Action Prediction. Decision Transformer [200] avoids value estimation by training a causal trans-
former to predict actions conditioned on desired return-to-go and past states and actions. At inference, the target return is
updated using received rewards. This avoids bootstrapping but remains limited by behaviors represented in the data.
Offline RL is limited by data support. If logs lack successful cup insertions, more failed trajectories may not reveal the
correct contact adjustments. Further improvement depends on whether the data lack state coverage, successful outcomes,
or reliable value estimates. Thus, dataset composition by outcome and controller matters, and data collection should
target these gaps. Offline methods can generalize from supported transitions, but unsupported outcomes require additional
assumptions or new evidence.
5.3.3
Online Reinforcement Learning
Online RL adds transitions from current behavior at the cost of physical time, wear, and resets. Parallel simulation, au-
tonomous hardware collection, and human-supported interaction trade fidelity and exploration scale against safety and
recovery cost. Online RL algorithms differ in how they reuse experience. On-policy methods such as PPO update policies
using recent data and constrain policy changes, but require frequent new samples. Off-policy actor–critic methods reuse
replay-buffer transitions for multiple updates, improving sample efficiency at the risk of value extrapolation errors (§ 5.3.2).
Thus, on-policy learning suits cheap simulation, while off-policy learning is often preferable for costly robot interactions.
RLPD [201] combines prior and online data equally in each batch, using critic layer normalization and a large ensemble
to stabilize frequent updates without offline pretraining.
Massively Parallel Simulation. Isaac Gym places simulation and learning on the GPU [6]. Learning to Walk in Min-
utes uses PPO and a terrain curriculum across thousands of simulated robots, demonstrating rapid learning and hardware
transfer [168]. Cheap interaction favors on-policy updates, while transfer still requires testing the gaps in § 5.5.4.
Autonomous Real-world Interaction. SERL [8] combines RLPD-based off-policy learning with automated rewards,
reset policies, and robot control infrastructure. It learns real-world assembly, cable routing, and relocation tasks in 25–
50 minutes, achieving near-perfect success and recovery under perturbations. DayDreamer [202] instead learns a world
model from real experience and trains policies through imagined rollouts, enabling a quadruped to recover, stand, and walk
without resets in one hour. However, imagined rollouts inherit model errors [16]. Both highlight that automated rewards,
resets, and reliable infrastructure are crucial for efficient real-world learning.
Human-supported Real-world Interaction. HIL-SERL [155] combines 20–30 initial demonstrations with human inter-
ventions during online RL. Intervention segments enter both demonstration and online replay buffers, which are sampled
equally for RLPD updates, while a success classifier supplies sparse rewards. Mean success improves from 49.7% for
the imitation baseline to 100%, though prolonged interventions can cause value overestimation. ConRFT extends this
approach to VLA post-training. Safe exploration still requires human oversight and physical safeguards.
5.3.4
Policy Learning in Imagined Environments
Imagined-policy learning separates real collection, predictive-model fitting, and policy optimization in generated trajec-
tories. The policy can then act without deployment-time search, although estimation and inference remain necessary.
DreamerV3 [72] trains a recurrent latent world model from real experience, then generates imagined trajectories to update
an actor–critic using predicted states, rewards, and episode continuation. Real trajectories also provide critic targets. It
applies a single configuration across over 150 tasks, including Minecraft. DayDreamer [202] extends this approach to
physical robots, continuously updating the world model and actor–critic while collecting new experience with the latest
policy. This feedback loop allows learning from policy errors encountered during deployment.
Imagined learning can also use offline data. World Models [203] trains behavior in a compact generative environment,
Dreamer 4 [204] learns long-horizon Minecraft behavior from offline data, and DIAMOND [70] generates pixel-level
imagined experience using diffusion models. However, these digital results do not capture real-robot constraints, and
imagined trajectories remain limited by model accuracy and data coverage. Online interaction can expand that coverage,
while offline learning remains constrained by existing data.
Planning and imagined learning can coexist, but expose different decisions to model error: planning uses predictions
32


for immediate action selection, whereas imagined learning propagates prediction errors through policy and value updates.
MBPO [205] limits error accumulation by generating short rollouts from real replay states. Evaluating imagined learning
still requires real or held-out interaction and accounting for data, model-training, and rollout costs.
Across offline, online, and imagined-policy learning, offline improvement depends on supported outcomes; online im-
provement additionally requires affordable exploration, rewards, and resets (Figure 14). Imagined experience adds model
coverage and prediction error to these constraints. Missing transitions may call for demonstrations, corrections, or new
trials. Learned rewards need independent validation, and assisted resets must be distinguished from autonomous recovery
when judging reduced supervision.
5.4
Policy Pretraining and Post-Training
Pretraining establishes a policy (§ 5.4.1); post-training refines it. Both use the objectives of §§ 5.1–5.3. The remaining
choices concern records (§ 5.4.2), source weights (§ 5.4.3), update scope (§ 5.4.4), supervised or reward-based refinement
(§§ 5.4.5–5.4.6), and their combination in workflows (§ 5.4.7). These choices recur across stages rather than defining a
mandatory sequence.
5.4.1
Policy Pretraining
Policy pretraining connects general features to robot actions. RT-1 learns from robot trajectories using pretrained image and
language encoders; RT-2 co-fine-tunes a web-pretrained VLM for action prediction [2, 106]. Initialization and subsequent
robot data must therefore be distinguished in comparisons. Recent approaches distinguish pretraining and post-training
by data diversity and quality. π0 [44] pretrains on diverse multi-robot data to learn robustness and recovery, then post-
trains on curated task data for precision, using a flow-matching action expert. π0.5 [80] expands pretraining to mobile
and static robots, cross-embodiment data, subtask prediction, web data, and human instructions. It uses discrete action
tokens during pretraining and adds a flow-matching expert during post-training. Ablations show that cross-embodiment
data improve performance, while web data support generalization to unseen objects.
GR00T N1 combines web, human-video, synthetic, and robot data, using latent or inverse-dynamics actions for unla-
beled videos [99]. This widens access while introducing pseudo-label errors. Open X-Embodiment and Octo pool robot
experience before specialization [5, 42]. Coverage, interface compatibility, and sampling exposure shape the pretrained
baseline against which target improvement and retention are measured.
5.4.2
Sample Selection
Selection allocates limited training resources among valid records, targeting missing conditions or corrections. Scores
differ in what they estimate and whether they require target demonstrations, policy fitting, or rollouts.
Relevance by Retrieval. Retrieval selects prior data based on similarity to target demonstrations. Behavior Retrieval [206]
matches state–action pairs in a learned embedding. STRAP [207] instead matches sub-trajectories using pretrained visual
features and dynamic time warping, enabling reuse of relevant segments from unrelated tasks. RWLA [208] retrieves
scene-relevant demonstrations and upweights segments related to observed failures. All three depend on the prior data
containing the required behavior.
Demonstration Quality Without Rollouts. Belkhale et al. assess expert–policy action divergence and transition diversity,
showing that more state diversity need not help [209]. DemInf instead estimates state–action mutual information from
embeddings, favoring predictable yet diverse actions without fitting or executing a policy for scoring [210].
Influence on Policy Performance. DataMIL [211] selects demonstrations using task-specific surrogate objectives, esti-
mating their value through repeated policy training or metagradients without evaluation rollouts. CUPID [212] instead uses
influence functions and evaluation rollouts to estimate each demonstration’s effect on expected return, enabling harmful-
data filtering and new-data selection. Their main trade-off is computational cost versus rollout requirements.
Retrieval needs relevant prior behavior; rollout-based influence needs interaction; surrogate scoring trades this for mod-
eling or training cost. Sirius additionally weights intervention records [156]. Filtering and weighting can combine with
replay, but excessive target focus may remove earlier coverage.
5.4.3
Training Mixtures
Sampling weights determine exposure after selection. Episode counts alone do not specify it: transition sampling also
depends on episode length. Mixtures can be size-proportional, source-uniform, manually weighted, or learned, but require
aligned action conventions and control rates. For M datasets, the training objective separates source size from sampling
weight:
33

(8)
Here, Di is source i, ℓθ the sample loss, and wi its training weight. Weights control source exposure but cannot compen-
sate for missing behaviors. Zero weight excludes a source from the current objective without deleting it, while excessive
weighting of small datasets may cause overfitting. Re-Mix [213] learns source weights for Open X-Embodiment using
group distributionally robust optimization, minimizing worst-case excess behavior-cloning loss relative to a reference pol-
icy. This distinguishes reducible error from irreducible source difficulty. Weights are set before training, alongside action
normalization, discretization, and early stopping. Re-Mix improves average task success by approximately 38 and 32 per-
centage points over size-proportional and human-designed RT-X mixtures, respectively. However, limited gains on full
BridgeV2 show that its benefits depend on dataset heterogeneity and training budget.
Sim-and-real co-training samples simulated demonstrations with probability α and real ones otherwise (Eq. (8)) [214].
Its CupPnP study shows non-monotonic performance as α increases: greater simulated exposure helps only within a useful
range. No direct size-proportional baseline is reported. Larger datasets can hurt performance when dominant domains
introduce shortcuts or incompatible action conventions. Xing et al. [215] attribute shortcut learning to limited within-
dataset diversity and large cross-dataset differences, showing that targeted augmentation can reduce shortcuts in policies
such as π0. Reweighting can limit source-specific biases but cannot create missing diversity, which requires augmentation
or new data collection.
5.4.4
Choosing What to Update
When adaptation updates parameters, it must select both a parameter scope and the objectives to retain. The same choices
can arise during initial policy training; here they determine how a pretrained policy is refined. Freezing a pretrained encoder
restricts adaptation to the action head or interface modules, preserving existing features but preventing their correction.
Full fine-tuning offers greater task adaptation but risks losing earlier capabilities. LoRA [216] provides a parameter-
efficient alternative by freezing W0 and learning a low-rank update ∆W = BA, which can be merged without inference
overhead. On Franka tasks, LoRA matches full fine-tuning of a smaller OpenVLA variant while updating only 1.4% of
parameters [28]. However, low rank does not guarantee preservation of earlier skills.
Sequential adaptation trains on robot tasks after general pretraining, as in OpenVLA, while retained-objective training
continues general supervision alongside robot learning, as in RT-2 [2]. Retaining objectives may preserve earlier capabili-
ties but consumes training capacity, so retention must be evaluated. Knowledge insulation [109] prevents gradients from a
newly initialized action expert from disrupting the pretrained backbone. It trains the backbone with discrete action tokens
and vision–language data while the flow-matching expert learns continuous actions from backbone features. Without this
separation, π0 required 7.5 times more training steps for comparable table-bussing performance. Thus, objective design
determines which parameters each data source updates. Models producing plans or text, such as PaLM-E [29], also require
different evaluation from those directly controlling robots.
5.4.5
Supervised Fine-Tuning
Supervised fine-tuning uses additional action labels to correct target-task gaps. Unlike the general imitation mechanisms
of § 5.2, the question here is how much new supervision a pretrained policy needs and what it changes. Requirements
vary by task: π0 post-training uses roughly 5 to over 100 hours, while OpenVLA’s Franka adaptations use 10–150 demon-
strations. OpenVLA-OFT also changes action representation and decoding during refinement [104]; gains must therefore
be separated from data exposure alone. RoboCat adapts to new demonstrations, collects trajectories, and incorporates
achieved-goal relabeling into later generalist training [154]. Fitting these action labels remains supervised learning even
when the policy collected them. Corrections must cover failure states; successful episodes alone need not teach recovery.
5.4.6
Reward-Based Fine-Tuning
Reward-based refinement is useful when outcomes are easier to judge than actions to label. Direct policy updates use
likelihood-based or critic-guided objectives [217]; a frozen generator can instead be steered through learned corrections;
and improved specialist behavior can be transferred by distillation. These routes distinguish where improvement enters the
pretrained policy, while parameter scope remains a separate choice.
Direct Policy Updates. Token policies expose action likelihoods. RIPT-VLA uses PPO-style sparse-reward updates,
SimpleVLA-RL uses group-relative advantages, and VLA-RL combines PPO with critic and process feedback; RL-VLA3
adds asynchronous training [218–221]. Implicit diffusion and flow policies need another likelihood interface. DPPO treats
Gaussian denoising as an inner decision process, while πRL introduces learnable noise or stochastic sampling paths [222,
223]. Both enable reward-based updates of pretrained generative policies.
34


Critic-guided objectives provide another route to direct policy refinement. ConRFT freezes Octo-small’s backbone and
fits a consistency head, combining cloning with Cal-QL offline before human-supported online learning; Cal-QL alone
fails to initialize a successful policy [224]. Learning While Deploying likewise uses a critic to refine a flow policy. The
learning objective, rather than the head family, distinguishes these updates.
Corrections to a Frozen Policy. Residual RL learns corrections to a fixed controller [225]. DSRL instead steers a frozen
diffusion or flow generator through its input noise, improving π0 on a held-out LIBERO-90 task with black-box access [226].
Fixed base weights do not imply unchanged behavior or guaranteed retention.
Distillation from Improved Specialists. Reinforcement-learned behavior can also improve generalists through supervised
distillation. RLDG [227] trains task-specific policies with HIL-SERL and uses their rollouts to fine-tune generalists such
as OpenVLA and Octo. Improvements stem mainly from action quality rather than state coverage, though this requires
training a specialist for each task.
A ManiSkill study [228] further shows that RL fine-tuning of OpenVLA, particularly with PPO, generalizes better than
supervised fine-tuning under semantic and execution shifts, while performing similarly under visual shifts. PPO also
outperforms the tested DPO and GRPO implementations.
However, reward-based updates risk eroding pretrained behavior. One mitigation is to penalize deviation from a refer-
ence policy in Eq. (7):
JRL(θ) = J(θ) −λKL K(θ),
K(θ) = E(h,g)∼dtrain
