.
Here λKL ≥0 controls deviation from the reference over dtrain. This requires a tractable KL or suitable estimator; im-
plicit flow and diffusion policies may need alternative surrogates. Such regularization limits drift but cannot guarantee
retention, which requires direct evaluation. Overall, reward-based adaptation depends on the generator’s available inter-
face—likelihoods, critic gradients, or steerable inputs—and how much pretrained behavior the update is allowed to change.
5.4.7
Post-Training Workflows
Two systems illustrate how post-training combines feedback sources rather than selecting a single objective. π∗
0.6’s RECAP
combines demonstrations, on-policy experience, and expert interventions through advantage conditioning, followed by on-
robot specialization [132]. Interventions serve both protection and learning and need records suitable for each use.
Learning While Deploying provides a second workflow. Sixteen dual-arm robots stream autonomous rollouts and exe-
cuted corrections into a shared buffer, with terminal success/failure labels and retained offline data [229]. Distributional
Implicit Value Learning preserves rare successes through quantile-based critic targets; Q-learning via Adjoint Matching
converts critic gradients to flow-regression targets without explicit action likelihoods. Under a four-hour budget per method
(about 60 robot-hours), the workflow scores 0.95 versus 0.76 for supervised fine-tuning across eight real tasks, four scored
by binary success and four by step-wise progress. This is a whole-workflow comparison, not an isolated optimizer result.
Budgets must include initialization, demonstrations, interaction, corrections, and optimization. Compare candidate and
parent on matched held-out new and retained tasks, with independent benefit and safety checks; reused validation tasks
can bias selection.
Across pretraining and post-training choices, diagnose the failure before choosing an update: incorrect rewards need cor-
rection, interface mismatches may need calibration, and missing behaviors need additional supervision. Freezing, adapters,
full fine-tuning, or contextual guidance address different limits; parameter efficiency alone does not establish retention. Fig-
ure 15 separates fixed-weight context changes from parameter updates and matched candidate acceptance. Robot-specific
activation remains a separate compatibility decision (§ 7.6.3).
5.5
Transfer and Adaptation
A robot transferring its cup-handling skills may encounter a new task, such as drying the cup (§ 5.5.1); a changed envi-
ronment, such as another kitchen (§ 5.5.2); or a different robot body and action interface (§ 5.5.3). Sim-to-real transfer
additionally examines mismatches between simulated training and physical deployment in observations, dynamics, and
execution (§ 5.5.4). The order identifies what changed and what information is available for adaptation; these shifts can
coexist. Adaptation may change instructions, retrieved context, calibration, or model parameters, with the post-training
methods in § 5.4 applying when a parameter update is needed.
5.5.1
Task Adaptation
When a robot learns to dry a cup after washing it, adaptation may involve reusing existing skills, selecting them under new
instructions, or learning new behaviors, such as wiping with a towel. Evaluation should distinguish skill acquisition from
selection or recombination. Adaptation methods differ in whether they modify policy inputs, fine-tuning data, or model
parameters through fixed or learned update rules.
35


Figure 15: Context changes and parameter adaptation. (a) Different instructions select different mugs using the same policy weights;
retrieved context also requires applicability checks. (b) New and retained experience produce a candidate policy, which is compared
with its parent on matched, held-out new and earlier tasks. Acceptance depends on benefit, retention, and safety criteria; failed checks
retain the parent. Replay does not guarantee retention. Candidate acceptance is separate from robot-specific activation, and continual
learning requires testing successive changes to the same evolving system.
Adaptation Through Policy Inputs. New instructions can enable task adaptation without parameter updates when the
required skills already exist. RT-2 follows commands absent from its robot training data, such as placing objects on speci-
fied numbers or icons, while π0.5 executes long-horizon cleaning tasks from high-level prompts in unseen homes. Code as
Policies [76] composes existing perception and control functions into programs, using variables to organize intermediate
results. These methods select or recombine learned behaviors, while retrieved examples can further condition a fixed pol-
icy without updating its parameters. Demonstrations offer another form of input conditioning without parameter updates.
ICRT [230] uses a causal transformer trained on sensorimotor trajectories to predict actions. At inference, it conditions
on a randomly selected teleoperated demonstration and the current observation. Franka-arm evaluations show adaptation
to new tasks with familiar and unseen objects, but these tasks reuse learned pick-and-place and poking primitives, leaving
generalization to unseen motion primitives untested.
Task ambiguity can also be resolved without parameter updates. KnowNo [94] uses conformal prediction to calibrate
candidate plan sets and requests human help when multiple options remain. Its completion guarantee assumes matched
calibration and test distributions, successful assistance, and reliable execution; its objective minimizes set size rather than
help frequency. YAY Robot [231] instead accepts real-time verbal corrections, such as “move left”, and immediately
adjusts execution. Both modify policy inputs without updating weights, while persistence depends on how corrections are
stored.
Adaptation Through Parameter Updates. When input-only adaptation is insufficient, target-data updates can learn new
behaviors. Fine-tuning multitask priors such as Octo and RoboCat [42, 154] exploits adaptability acquired through multi-
task training, whereas meta-learning explicitly optimizes for rapid adaptation. One-Shot Visual Imitation Learning [232]
learns an initialization that enables adaptation from a single visual demonstration, but its effectiveness depends on the cov-
erage of prior tasks. Fair comparisons should therefore control target demonstrations and update budgets. Prior records
can supplement the target data used for these updates. Behavior Retrieval supplements a small target set with similar state–
action records [206]. As discussed in § 5.4.2, this improves adaptation only when prior data contain relevant behavior.
The appropriate task-adaptation route depends on whether the required behavior already exists. Instructions, programs,
or in-context examples can select existing skills; retrieval and target-data updates can support partially covered behaviors;
new capabilities require evidence from further learning and task-level execution.
5.5.2
Environmental Adaptation
Environmental change can affect appearance, action feasibility, or hidden dynamics. Randomization broadens anticipated
coverage; conditional policies infer current conditions; calibration or parameter updates fit remaining mismatches. RMA
first trains a simulated locomotion policy with privileged condition latents, then trains a module to infer those latents from
state–action history [37]. On the real A1 quadruped, only the inferred latent changes, so adaptation to terrain, payload, and
wear is conditional inference rather than online parameter learning.
Randomized training broadens coverage, while calibration addresses specific mismatches. RWLA instead adapts to new
scenes by fine-tuning a policy copy on retrieved demonstrations. Unlike conditional policies, this changes parameters and
36


requires target data and retention testing. Appearance and dynamics shifts should be evaluated separately, such as varying
lighting and texture while fixing physics, then changing payload or friction. Training exposure and target-time information
must be reported, since robustness to visual changes does not imply robustness to dynamics shifts.
5.5.3
Cross-Embodiment Transfer
New bodies change observations, action dimensions, reachability, and contact geometry. Multi-robot data broaden cov-
erage, model interfaces accommodate embodiment-specific inputs and outputs, and shared action representations support
reuse. These data, model, and interface choices can be combined. Numerical normalization alone cannot establish feasible
execution.
Training Coverage. Open X-Embodiment [5] standardizes data from 22 robots across 21 institutions, covering 527 skills.
RT-X models trained on this dataset demonstrate positive cross-robot transfer, with RT-1-X outperforming the original
method on four of five small-data domains but not surpassing embodiment-specific RT-1 on large-data domains. Such
transfer depends on compatible observation and action mappings, as standardized data alone cannot resolve embodiment
differences. Its effectiveness also depends on how well the target robot is represented in the training pool.
Model Adaptation. Octo [42] trains a transformer on multi-robot trajectories and supports language or goal-image instruc-
tions, enabling zero-shot transfer and fine-tuning to new observations, action spaces, and embodiments. Related methods
differ in how they handle embodiment-specific interfaces. HPT [127] uses embodiment-specific tokenizers with a shared
trunk; CrossFormer [128] trains across diverse robots without manual observation or action alignment; X-VLA [129] uses
embodiment-specific soft prompts; and UniVLA [130] shares task-centric latent actions across robots. These designs
reduce cross-embodiment adaptation costs, but differences in target data and control interfaces prevent direct zero-shot
performance comparisons.
Executable Interfaces. UMI [136] enables cross-robot transfer through handheld-gripper demonstrations represented as
relative end-effector trajectories executable by different arms. However, transfer still depends on compatible gripper and
contact geometry; reproducing wrist trajectories alone may be insufficient.
Evaluate coverage, model adaptation, and executable interfaces separately: gains attributed to a shared policy may
depend on body-specific calibration or control. Comparisons must state target-data access and the physical differences
between source and target robots.
5.5.4
Sim-to-Real Transfer
Sim-to-real transfer must address observation, dynamics, and execution mismatches. Three complementary choices orga-
nize the discussion: which simulated and real conditions training covers, which privileged information must be replaced
by deployable observations, and how real feedback calibrates training or conditions execution. These choices can be com-
bined; visual randomization alone cannot resolve dynamics or execution mismatches.
Training Coverage. Randomization varies simulator parameters ξ, including textures, friction, mass, and latency, under
p(ξ). Tobin et al. demonstrate real-object localization from randomized synthetic images [170]. A narrow distribution can
miss deployment conditions, while an overly broad one wastes capacity; neither covers unmodeled contact or execution
effects. Real-world transfer must therefore be tested directly. Automatic domain randomization [169] adaptively adjusts
the simulator parameter distribution p(ξ). Starting from calibrated real-world values, it expands or contracts parameter
ranges based on policy performance at their boundaries. This enabled a robotic hand to solve a Rubik’s cube requiring
15 face rotations in 6/10 trials with sensorized face angles and 2/10 with vision-estimated angles. However, adaptation
remains limited to the parameters selected for randomization.
Training coverage can also include real demonstrations. Sim-and-real co-training [214] combines real demonstrations
with simulated data from task-specific digital cousins and task-agnostic multitask environments. Across six real tasks on a
robot arm and a humanoid, it improves average success from 45% to 83%. While exact simulation replicas are unnecessary,
effectiveness depends on sufficient simulated data and an appropriate sampling ratio.
Deployable Observation Interfaces. Lee et al. distill a simulated teacher with privileged terrain and contact informa-
tion into a proprioceptive-history student for ANYmal [167]. RMA similarly trains condition inference from deployable
observations. Distillation cannot recover privileged information that those observations do not identify.
Calibration and Condition Inference. SimOpt uses real rollouts to calibrate the simulator distribution, whereas RMA
infers current conditions without changing policy parameters [37, 172]. They adapt training conditions and execution
inputs, respectively. Calibration and condition inference are limited by observability: physical parameters cannot be reli-
ably identified when they produce indistinguishable trajectories. Sim-to-real evaluation should therefore report real-world
adaptation budgets, robustness to uncalibrated changes, and separate observation, dynamics, and actuation gaps. Success
under calibrated conditions does not guarantee transfer to untested environments.
Different transfer challenges require different adaptation strategies. For new tasks, instructions, in-context examples, or
retrieved data may suffice when relevant skills exist, while novel behaviors require demonstrations and parameter updates.
For new environments, augmentation and randomization address appearance shifts, whereas conditional policies or online
37


estimation handle hidden dynamics changes. For new embodiments, transfer depends on executable action interfaces or
embodiment-specific model adaptation.
5.6
Continual Learning
Continual learning must accumulate and retain capability across successive changes. We first examine where updates
persist and how storage choices affect interference (§ 5.6.1), then how to test retention in the same evolving system (§ 5.6.2;
Figure 15). Independently adapted copies do not establish cumulative learning.
5.6.1
Where Changes Are Stored
Shared updates may improve new tasks while degrading earlier skills. Changes can instead be stored in memory, separate
modules, or shared parameters, each balancing interference, storage, retrieval, and validation costs. Continual learning
must therefore determine which past experiences to retain, how to protect existing capabilities, and when updates are safe
to deploy.
External Records and Skill Libraries. Memory and skill libraries can preserve knowledge without updating shared policy
weights. Corrections should retain their scope, provenance, and validity conditions to support selective reuse. Voyager [60]
demonstrates this approach in Minecraft, storing self-verified programs for retrieval on later tasks without parameter up-
dates. YAY Robot [231] instead uses verbal corrections immediately and later incorporates them through policy retraining.
LOTUS [59] expands a skill library by discovering new skills, updating existing ones, and training a meta-controller to
compose them. While modular expansion can reduce interference, retention still requires evaluating both updated skills
and the meta-controller’s ability to select them appropriately.
Separate Parameter Modules. Isolation mitigates forgetting by freezing or separating model components. TAIL [233]
freezes pretrained encoders and transformer decoder while training adapters and task-specific fusion layers and policy
heads, starting from a policy pretrained on 40 LIBERO kitchen tasks. It stores separate parameters for each task suite or
individual task and restores them when needed. On ten long-horizon LIBERO tasks, low-rank adapters achieved higher
peak new-task success than full fine-tuning while preserving earlier task parameters. In contrast, full fine-tuning, replay,
and EWC showed negative backward transfer. Isolation reduces interference without replay but requires correct parameter
selection at inference, while frozen components cannot improve through gradient updates.
Shared Parameter Updates. Experience replay mitigates forgetting by mixing stored past data into new updates, with
effectiveness depending on how representative the retained data are. RWLA [208] combines replay for skill retention with
retrieval-based local adaptation, emphasizing demonstrations associated with observed failures. It operates without explicit
task identities or boundaries. However, its evaluation initializes separate adaptation copies from the same checkpoint,
demonstrating local adaptation rather than retention across sequential updates. Establishing continual learning requires
evaluating the same evolving system on successive tasks and retesting earlier capabilities. Regularization offers another
way to limit interference in shared parameters. Elastic weight consolidation (EWC) [234] mitigates forgetting without
storing past data. It uses the diagonal Fisher information to estimate parameter importance and applies a quadratic penalty
that restricts changes to parameters important for earlier tasks. LIBERO includes EWC as a lifelong-learning baseline [177].
However, EWC cannot eliminate forgetting: TAIL’s LIBERO experiments show that it retains earlier skills less effectively
and adapts to new tasks less successfully than full fine-tuning.
Storage choices can be combined: expansion can freeze earlier modules and their dependencies while replay or contin-
ued training updates other components. Memory and isolated modules reduce parameter interference but add retrieval,
routing, and maintenance costs; shared updates enable reuse but retain forgetting risks. Evaluation must cover routing and
shared components, storage and optimization costs, and every capability affected by an update. For personalization, test
original tasks, the target user, and other users separately, with explicit release criteria such as parent–candidate compar-
isons (§ 5.4.7). Learning While Deploying illustrates the shared-update case: each round of fleet experience updates and
redeploys the generalist, requiring retention checks across iterations.
5.6.2
Capability Retention
Continual learning requires evaluating retention across task sequences, not just final success rates. Three measures capture
different effects: forward transfer assesses benefits to new tasks, forgetting measures degradation relative to earlier peak
performance, and backward transfer measures positive or negative changes on previous tasks after subsequent learning.
LIBERO [177] standardizes task sequences and protocols for these evaluations, while LOTUS [59] and TAIL [233] exam-
ine skill-based and parameter-efficient retention strategies. Methods should be compared under matched sequences and
storage budgets, since LIBERO shows that sequential fine-tuning can outperform lifelong-learning methods in forward
transfer despite weaker retention. This trade-off requires tracking new-task gains and earlier-task performance together
throughout the sequence, across affected users and environments.
Across continual-learning mechanisms, context and memory retain scoped information; modules isolate learned changes
at a routing and storage cost; shared parameters enable reuse but risk interference. Clearing context, removing records,
38


or restoring weights can test where an improvement persists. Delayed tests on earlier tasks reveal forgetting and stale
information.
Across the learning objectives and adaptation settings in this chapter, demonstrations establish behavior, corrections
cover deployment gaps, and rewards support improvement when outcomes are verifiable. The choice depends on the
failure and the cost of feedback, resets, and supervision. Matched experience budgets and repeated new/retained-task tests
are needed to distinguish local adaptation from sustained learning.
6
Embodiment and Control
The body’s geometry, dynamics, sensing, and actuation shape how a robot senses and acts. A policy produces actions at a
specified interface; action conventions give their numerical values physical meaning, and the execution layer uses feedback
and control to produce actuator commands and physical motion. The amount of control assigned to the policy varies with
the interface. Differences in robot bodies, sensors, conventions, and controllers can therefore cause the same policy output
to perform differently, while also shaping data collection and learning.
The chapter follows these physical dependencies: morphology sets the body’s structural possibilities and constraints
(§ 6.1), sensing and estimation provide information for decisions (§ 6.2), action interfaces specify what policy outputs
request (§ 6.3), and feedback control turns those requests into commands while responding to measured conditions (§ 6.4).
This order explains how the body, available information, and control responsibilities shape execution together. In the cup
task, reaching the shelf, gripping a wet cup, and detecting slip depend on these physical layers, not just the policy. Thus,
some apparent learning failures may be better addressed through changes to the robot’s body, sensors, or controller.
Figure 16 asks when a policy output can become feasible physical behavior by connecting morphology, sensing, action
meaning, and feedback control.
Figure 16: Physical execution and feedback. Morphology constrains the system; action interfaces connect requested actions to
feedback control. Sensing and estimation close the loop from physical execution back to control. Numbered components correspond to
the four subsections.
6.1
Robot Morphology
Morphology refers to the robot’s physical form and structure, which shape what a policy can learn and execute. We move
from kinematic structure, which determines reach and coordination (§ 6.1.1), to end effectors, which determine possible
contact interactions (§ 6.1.2), and then to embodiment interfaces, which describe body-specific capabilities to shared
policies (§ 6.1.3). This order connects physical constraints to contact and then to their representation for policy transfer.
In the cup task, repositioning the base, changing the tool, or moving the shelf may overcome limitations that additional
training cannot resolve.
6.1.1
Kinematic Structure
Kinematics determines whether action targets are physically feasible. Joint limits, obstacles, and redundancy constrain
reachable poses and joint configurations. Adding arms, a mobile base, or legs introduces new capabilities and coordination
constraints. Bimanual manipulation requires coordinated motion under contact and collision constraints, as demonstrated
by ACT [102]. Mobile ALOHA jointly learns base and arm control, extending reach while introducing base-placement
decisions [148]. Legged manipulation additionally requires maintaining balance and contact. HumanPlus uses learned
whole-body shadowing for demonstrations [149], while optimization-based control [235] and learned controllers such as
HOVER and UMI on Legs [236, 237] coordinate manipulation with support constraints. Thus, identical policy outputs
may differ in feasibility and physical meaning across robot bodies.
6.1.2
End Effectors
End effectors determine how robots contact objects and adjust grasps. Parallel grippers use simple jaw closure, dexterous
hands control multiple contacts, suction cups rely on sealed contact, and compliant components accommodate errors.
39


These designs affect grasp precision, post-contact correction, and sensing requirements. However, more actuators do not
necessarily mean greater dexterity; effective control also depends on training data, feedback, and action interfaces.
Parallel Grippers. PerAct and UMI illustrate manipulation with parallel grippers, where a single opening command makes
grasps easy to record and transfer across compatible arms. However, fixed contact points limit post-grasp adjustments, often
requiring controlled slip, regrasping, or environmental contact. Grasp success depends on approach pose, friction, grip
force, and object properties. Thus, changing grippers affects more than opening range: differences in contact geometry
and friction can alter the outcome of identical grasp commands.
Dexterous Hands. Multi-fingered hands can reposition objects without releasing them, but coordinating their contacts is
challenging. A simulation-trained Shadow Hand policy used automatic domain randomization to manipulate a Rubik’s
cube, following a sequence generated by a conventional solver [169]. Lin et al. [173] transferred vision-based humanoid
hand policies from simulation to real-world grasping, lifting, and bimanual handover without further training. Both ap-
proaches relied on calibrating or randomizing hand dynamics, while a dynamics model learned from real experience
enabled in-hand rotation of two balls. Effective dexterity thus requires training and evaluation across diverse contact
configurations.
Suction. Vacuum grippers lift objects through a single sealed contact, avoiding the need to grasp around them. Success
depends on forming a seal and resisting forces and torques during motion. Dex-Net 3.0 [238] models these conditions and
trains a grasp-quality network using synthetic point clouds. Success rates dropped from 98% on basic objects to 58% on
adversarial ones, revealing sensitivity to surface properties and geometry. Its rigid, nonporous object assumption further
limits applicability, while poor depth sensing and unsuitable materials can prevent reliable sealing. Thus, suction simplifies
grasping but sacrifices finger-based correction and depends heavily on surface conditions.
Across these contact mechanisms, passive compliance provides an additional design choice. Compliance can be built
into hardware: soft fingertips conform to objects, while compliant wrists absorb alignment errors during contact. However,
deformation introduces additional states that must be sensed or inferred, especially when manipulating deformable objects.
End effectors differ in their ability to correct errors after contact. While compliance reduces geometric errors, it cannot
detect slip or measure contact forces on its own, making additional sensing necessary.
6.1.3
Embodiment Interfaces
A policy shared across robots must account for differences in reach, joint limits, gripper geometry, sensors, and control,
which may not be fully observable from sensor inputs. A shared action layout specifies which quantities can be commanded;
learned body-specific components or explicit descriptions can condition the policy on selected body differences; and an
execution layer maps selected targets to the particular body. These roles differ: a command layout does not encode physical
limits, policy conditioning does not itself make a target feasible, and target conversion does not ensure suitable contact.
Cross-embodiment learning also draws on pooled multi-robot data, as in Open X-Embodiment [5], and body-specific
modules around a shared policy trunk, as in Octo [42].
Unified Action Layouts. The action-vector layout defines what a policy can command. RDT-1B [111] uses a unified
128-dimensional action space with fixed physical meanings, such as joint positions or base velocities. Each robot fills its
applicable entries and pads the rest, enabling shared supervision across bodies. However, the layout specifies available
quantities, not their limits: an identical joint target may be feasible for one robot but unreachable for another.
Conditioning on the Body. HPT uses embodiment-specific stems to encode robot observations for a shared trunk [127],
while X-VLA uses per-source soft prompts [129]. Both implicitly capture body differences from data but lack explicit
limits for feasibility checks. Adapting to a new robot also requires data to train its body-specific components. Body
conditioning can also encode selected attributes through explicit embodiment descriptions. The Embodied Navigation
Foundation Model [239] uses identifier tokens to represent camera configurations and temporal context across quadrupeds,
drones, wheeled robots, and vehicles. However, these tokens describe sensing configurations rather than joint limits or
full-body kinematics.
Execution Layers. Below the policy, an execution layer translates shared targets into body-specific motions. UMI [136]
uses gripper-relative end-effector trajectories that each robot converts into joint commands. UMI on Legs [237] extends
this interface to a quadruped through whole-body control, enabling zero-shot transfer from a fixed-base arm. HOVER [236]
similarly supports multiple command modes for humanoid whole-body control. These layers reduce kinematic and coor-
dinate differences but do not guarantee target feasibility or resolve differences in gripper properties and workspace.
Across morphology and embodiment interfaces, mechanical capability must be balanced against learning complexity.
Passive compliance simplifies force control, while independent contact manipulation may justify additional actuation and
coordination. Embodiment interfaces should expose physical limits and reject infeasible targets. Evaluation on unseen
robot bodies, materials, and geometries helps determine when simple tools suffice and when greater dexterity is necessary.
6.2
Sensing and State Estimation
Sensor placement determines what a robot can observe. In the cup task, the robot must locate the cup, detect contact, and
track it through occlusion. The discussion separates measurement sources from the estimation that uses them: exteroceptive
40


sensing measures the environment (§ 6.2.1); proprioceptive and contact sensing provide complementary evidence about
body motion and interactions (§ 6.2.2); and state estimation integrates these measurements across frames and time (§ 6.2.3).
Adding sensors, adjusting their placement, and improving estimation address different limitations in observation. The goal
is to provide timely state estimates and uncertainty for policy and control.
6.2.1
Exteroceptive Sensing
Before contact, the robot must locate the cup, surrounding obstacles, and free workspace. However, images lack direct
metric depth, occlusions hide objects, reflective or transparent surfaces challenge depth sensing, and measurements become
outdated during motion. Exteroceptive sensing must therefore provide sufficient coverage, geometric accuracy, contact-
level resolution, and timely updates. Sensor modality determines what is measured: RGB cameras capture appearance,
depth and RGB-D sensors provide metric geometry, and LiDAR measures longer-range distances. Placement determines
the viewpoint: fixed cameras provide stable views, wrist-mounted cameras offer close-up observations, and body-mounted
cameras support navigation. Field of view and occlusion further constrain what each sensor can observe.
Appearance and Depth Cameras. RGB-D sensing enables policies to predict metric 3D actions. PerAct [30] uses
multiview RGB-D observations and language goals to classify actions in a voxel space, while 3D Diffusion Policy [31]
generates actions conditioned on sparse point clouds. Both depend on reliable depth, which can fail on transparent or
reflective surfaces. ClearGrasp [240] addresses this limitation by predicting surface normals, transparent regions, and
boundaries from RGB images, then reconstructing missing depth through optimization. On transparent objects, depth
repair improved suction grasp success from 64% to 86% and parallel-jaw grasp success from 12% to 72%. These results
show that depth errors can create material-dependent failures even when policies perform well on opaque objects.
Range Sensing at Vehicle Scale. For large-scale navigation, LiDAR provides long-range geometry while cameras capture
visual context. TransFuser [43] combines image and LiDAR features through attention to capture both local geometry and
global context, such as traffic lights and approaching vehicles. In CARLA simulations, it reduced collisions compared
with geometry-based fusion, demonstrating the value of global context in urban driving.
Beyond sensor modality, viewpoint is a separate configuration choice. Camera placement determines whether observa-
tions remain fixed in the workspace or move with the robot. PerAct uses a fixed workspace representation, ViNT [241]
relies on an onboard camera, and UMI [136] uses a gripper-mounted camera to align demonstration and deployment view-
points. Hsu et al. [242] found that hand-centric views improve learning efficiency and out-of-distribution generalization
when they provide sufficient task visibility. Otherwise, third-person views may be necessary but can reduce generaliza-
tion, an effect mitigated by information bottlenecking. Camera placement also affects policy robustness, as examined by
LIBERO-Plus [243]. Fixed cameras provide stable workspace views but may suffer occlusion, while wrist cameras cap-
ture local contact details but lack global context. Moving-camera observations also require pose transformations. Sensor
placement should therefore match each task stage’s visibility needs.
6.2.2
Proprioception and Contact Sensing
As the hand approaches the cup, vision may fail to reveal occluded contacts, grasp stability, or small slips. Rapid contact
changes and forces require sensing beyond vision. Proprioceptive, wrist force-torque, and tactile sensors provide comple-
mentary information about body configuration, overall interaction forces, and local contact states. Sensor choice depends
on what remains unobservable and the feedback speed required for control.
Proprioceptive Measurements. Joint encoders measure positions, with velocities estimated through filtering and differ-
entiation. IMUs measure angular rate and specific force for orientation estimation, while motor currents or torque sensors
indicate joint effort. Gripper aperture and current provide indirect evidence of grasping but cannot confirm grasp stabil-
ity. These signals describe the robot’s internal state regardless of contact and typically enter policies as proprioceptive
input sprop
t
(§ 3.4.4). Proprioceptive signals can also detect contact without dedicated sensors. Generalized-momentum
observers [244] estimate external joint torques from discrepancies between predicted and applied torques, using joint
positions, velocities, and motor torques without acceleration measurements. Thresholding the filtered residual detects
sufficiently strong contacts, but some forces remain unobservable. Contact localization requires additional geometric as-
sumptions, while model errors can cause false alarms. Thus, detection thresholds balance sensitivity and reliability.
Force and Tactile Sensing. Wrist force–torque sensors measure overall interaction loads but cannot localize finger con-
tacts, whereas tactile sensors capture local contact information. ReSkin [245] uses replaceable magnetic elastomer skins
with 400 Hz sensing and self-supervised recalibration to estimate contact location and force. Sparsh [47] learns transfer-
able visual-tactile representations from over 460k unlabeled images across gel-based sensors. It improves performance
on six TacBench tasks, including force, slip, pose, and grasp-stability estimation, compared with task-specific training.
However, representation-level improvements do not necessarily imply better manipulation success.
Using these contact measurements in a policy requires task-level validation. Touch Begins Where Vision Ends [48]
combines vision-based object localization with a reusable local policy using egocentric vision and tactile sensing for
contact-rich manipulation. Ablations show that tactile feedback substantially improves success, particularly when occlu-
sion limits vision. However, tactile data remain scarce: Sparsh learns from unlabeled tactile images, while DexCap [137]
41


records human wrist and finger motions without directly measuring contact forces.
Joint-torque residuals detect external loading, wrist sensors measure net forces, and tactile sensors resolve local contacts.
Although noise, saturation, hysteresis, and calibration drift limit reliability, combining these signals can recover contact
states that individual sensors cannot observe.
6.2.3
State Estimation
Sensors operate in different coordinate frames, at different rates, and with different delays, requiring a consistent state esti-
mate. Calibration aligns coordinate frames, synchronization aligns measurements in time, and state estimation combines
sensor data with motion models to handle noise, occlusion, and missing observations. For example, when the wrist cam-
era loses sight of the cup, the robot must propagate its estimate using motion information until new observations become
available. The comparison below distinguishes model-based estimation from learned estimation, while allowing hybrid
systems. Contact measurements can constrain the former; history can supply features for the latter. Both can estimate
uncertainty, but neither guarantees reliable uncertainty estimates without validation. Estimates must also account for their
age, since outdated measurements may no longer reflect the current scene.
Calibration and timing errors systematically distort observations and actions. Hand–eye calibration estimates the wrist
camera’s pose relative to the gripper using paired motions satisfying AX = XB. Tsai and Lenz [246] solve rotation before
translation, requiring motions about at least two non-parallel axes. UMI [136] addresses temporal alignment by matching
inference latency to training conditions (Section 4.6.1; Eq. (10)). Policies may adapt to consistent alignment errors during
training but fail when those errors change across robots or setups.
Model-based Estimation. Kalman filtering [247] predicts state and covariance using a motion model and updates them
with measurements. Its uncertainty estimates depend on model accuracy and generally increase during occlusion. Visual–
inertial odometry (VIO) combines slower visual observations with high-rate but drifting IMU measurements. VINS-
Mono [248] uses sliding-window optimization, IMU pre-integration, online calibration, and loop closure to improve estima-
tion and reduce drift. Swift [46] uses 100 Hz VIO for drone racing and corrects motion-blur-induced position and velocity
drift with a Kalman filter using detected race gates, while retaining VIO orientation. Legged robots can use stationary foot
contacts to estimate base motion. The contact-aided invariant extended Kalman filter [249] combines IMU propagation
with leg-kinematic measurements, treating foot slip as contact-velocity noise. Its bias-free formulation provides estimate-
independent linearized error dynamics and observation Jacobians, though IMU biases weaken this property. Experiments
on Cassie demonstrate improved convergence, but significant foot slip can violate the stationary-contact assumption and
degrade estimation.
Learned Estimation. Learned state estimation can use latent representations instead of explicit physical states. Learning
Quadrupedal Locomotion over Challenging Terrain [167] uses proprioceptive history to control ANYmal across unseen ter-
rains, while RMA [37] explicitly estimates latent body and environment conditions from recent states and actions. Both are
trained in simulation. Learning High-Speed Flight in the Wild [250] directly predicts collision-free trajectories from noisy
observations while retaining visual–inertial state estimation. Learned representations can simplify estimation pipelines
but do not guarantee calibrated uncertainty or generalization to unseen dynamics. Runtime monitoring therefore requires
explicit confidence estimates or independent consistency checks.
Object tracking illustrates the remaining limits when observations become occluded. The robot must track the cup’s pose
even under occlusion. FoundationPose [251] estimates and tracks unseen objects’ 6D poses using CAD models or reference
images. After initialization, it refines the previous pose by comparing rendered objects with new RGB-D observations.
However, severe occlusion, weak textures, and poor edge cues can cause tracking failures. Since FoundationPose lacks
explicit object dynamics, maintaining pose estimates through complete occlusion requires additional mechanisms to track
uncertainty and observation age.
Across sensing and estimation choices, effective sensing requires deciding what to measure directly, infer, or regulate
through feedback. Repositioning cameras can resolve occlusion, while touch and force sensing reveal contact states that
vision cannot. Rich tactile policies support complex interactions, whereas simpler force feedback may suffice for narrower
tasks. Sensor transfer depends on calibration and the contact states that must be distinguished. Both explicit and learned
state estimates require validated uncertainty, and neither can recover fundamentally unobservable information. Recogniz-
ing these limits is essential for timely control and safe intervention.
6.3
Action Interfaces
Once the robot estimates the cup’s state, the policy must propose actions that can be interpreted and executed. We first
examine physical action spaces (§ 6.3.1): the quantities supplied at the interface, such as position, velocity, torque, or
impedance targets, assign different responsibilities to the downstream controller, while tokens or latent actions require
decoding before they reach a physical interface. We then specify action conventions (§ 6.3.2), whose frame, scale, timing,
and reference update rule determine how those quantities are interpreted. The runtime determines when targets are used,
and controllers and filters determine the commands ultimately applied. Embodiment interfaces in § 6.1.3 describe or
compensate for body differences across robots; the present section specifies the meaning of each action at a particular
42


execution interface.
6.3.1
Physical Action Spaces
A physical action interface specifies both a coordinate space and the quantity requested from the controller. Joint or
Cartesian coordinates can carry motion targets, while position, velocity, torque, and impedance outputs assign different
feedback responsibilities. These are intersecting choices, not categories determined by vector length. Encoded tokens or
latent actions must ultimately be decoded into such an interface.
Joint-space targets specify robot configurations, leaving tracking and constraints to the controller.
End-effector
targets improve transfer across robots but require inverse kinematics to resolve feasibility, redundancy, and collisions.
VICES [252] compares joint and end-effector control, position and velocity commands, and variable impedance across
free-motion and contact-rich tasks. Its end-effector impedance policies demonstrate cross-robot and sim-to-real transfer.
Similarly, UMI [136] transfers end-effector trajectories from handheld demonstrations to UR5e and Franka arms. While
end-effector control simplifies policy transfer, downstream controllers must still handle joint configurations and collision
avoidance.
Velocity commands specify motion rates but can accumulate position errors without corrective feedback. Diffusion
Policy [103] found that position control consistently outperformed velocity control for its diffusion model, while BCRNN
and BET showed the opposite trend. The authors attribute this difference to reduced error accumulation and diffusion
models’ ability to represent multimodal position actions. Velocity control was also more sensitive to simulated latency.
Torque control places greater responsibility for stabilization and compensation on the policy, requiring it to meet control
deadlines.
Variable-impedance policies output motion targets alongside stiffness and damping, allowing adaptation to contact
forces. Learning Variable Impedance Control [253] compares learned joint-space impedance with torque and fixed-gain
position control in hopping and manipulation tasks. Direct-torque policies failed to learn hopping, while a position-
tracking reward, separate from torque smoothness, helped produce interpretable targets and supported hardware transfer.
VICES [252] found variable impedance particularly useful for wiping, but less necessary for path following and door
opening. This flexibility improves contact handling at the cost of larger action spaces and additional stability constraints.
Encoded actions, including FAST tokens, OpenVLA-OFT continuous outputs, and LAPA and UniVLA latent
actions [104, 105, 121, 130], require decoding into physical commands. Decoding may be deterministic or learned,
introducing potential errors; shared latents need not uniquely specify body-specific actions.
No action space is universally optimal. Position control delegates dynamics to the controller, velocity and torque con-
trol demand more from the policy, and variable impedance improves contact flexibility but adds complexity and stability
constraints. As VICES illustrates, performance depends on the combination of action space and low-level controller, not
the action representation alone.
6.3.2
Action Conventions
Action conventions define the reference frame, scale, timing, and update rule that give policy outputs physical meaning.
The same numerical command can produce different motions depending on whether it uses world, base, or end-effector
coordinates, absolute or relative targets, and per-step or per-chunk updates. Calibration errors shift targets, stale references
offset action chunks, and timing mismatches alter execution speed and potentially the resulting motion. Training and
execution must use consistent conventions, including resampling and time scaling. Action normalization also requires
matching statistics to recover physical units. Importantly, coordinate space (joint or Cartesian) and reference update rules
(absolute, measured-relative, or chunk-relative) are separate choices; absolute joint targets need no global Cartesian frame.
Figure 17 illustrates these dependencies in a planar example. Rotating the tool frame changes the physical direction of an
identically numbered increment. Conversely, two different numerical descriptions can request the same target: from a mea-
sured position of 0.10 m, an absolute target of 0.12 m matches a +0.02 m increment in the same frame. This equivalence
concerns one specified reference, not arbitrary repeated or chunk-relative updates.
Target References. Absolute joint targets, as in ACT, are executed by joint controllers, while absolute Cartesian targets
also require coordinate transformations. Measured-relative targets use the observed pose as their reference. Repeated
relative commands may accumulate errors depending on feedback and reference updates. In contrast, UMI’s delta-action
baseline defines each action relative to the preceding commanded action rather than the measured pose [136]. A trajectory
chunk can instead share one reference pose. SRT [254] predicts waypoints relative to a shared initial end-effector pose,
expressing translation in the endoscope frame and rotation relative to the tool frame. This reduces sensitivity to forward-
kinematics inconsistencies in surgical tasks, though large-scale clinical training remains a proposed extension. UMI [136]
similarly uses a shared reference pose for each action chunk, with latency alignment. Despite differences in coordinate
frames and control interfaces, both approaches remain sensitive to stale or biased reference poses, which affect the entire
action chunk.
Rotation Representations. Orientation representations introduce discontinuities absent from position coordinates. Euler
angles suffer from wrapping and gimbal lock, while quaternions have sign ambiguity, complicating regression. Zhou et
43


Figure 17: Action meaning depends on interface conventions. (a) With a tool frame rotated 90◦from the base frame, the same
positive x increment requests different planar directions; the arrows show alternatives. (b) From x = 0.10 m, an absolute target of
0.12 m and a measured-relative increment of +0.02 m specify the same target. This equivalence assumes the stated reference and units.
(c) The controller must interpret values, frames, units, modes, rates, and gripper conventions consistently. The planar example does not
establish dynamic feasibility or prescribe a full pose representation.
al. [255] show that SO(3) has no globally continuous representation in four or fewer Euclidean dimensions and propose
continuous 5D and 6D alternatives. The 6D representation reconstructs rotations by orthogonalizing two vectors and
computing their cross product, with numerical handling for degenerate cases. Thus, rotation conventions must specify
both the representation, its decoding, and the reference frame.
Policy performance depends on keeping both reference and rotation conventions consistent during execution. OpenVLA-
OFT [104] reports gains under specific embodiment and action interfaces, but changes in control frequency, gripper geom-
etry, or normalization can alter performance. Such sensitivities may remain hidden in benchmark results, just as camera
placement affects observations. Absolute Cartesian targets depend on calibrated transforms, measured-relative targets on
accurate reference updates, and chunk-relative trajectories on their initial reference. None is universally superior. Reliable
execution requires consistent frames, scales, rates, references, and rotation conventions between training and deployment.
Across physical action spaces and conventions, the controller translates policy actions into physical motion. Position
control handles joint dynamics, supporting target reuse across compatible robots, while torque control places greater re-
sponsibility for stabilization on the policy. Impedance and latent actions offer flexibility, but their benefits must be dis-
tinguished from those of the low-level controller. Comparing representations with a fixed controller and interfaces with
a fixed policy isolates different effects. Action meanings must be preserved in dataset records, while feedback control
determines whether the requested actions can be realized.
6.4
Feedback Control
Feedback control determines how actions are executed using updated measurements. We first consider motion and contact
control (§ 6.4.1), which tracks targets and regulates interaction forces, then learning in the control loop (§ 6.4.2), which
examines how learning changes or complements those feedback mechanisms. This order establishes the control responsi-
bilities before examining what learning changes. In the cup task, a controller may track the wrist target and regulate contact
forces; an interface that supplies joint torques assigns more of that responsibility to the policy and its lower-level loops.
Comparing these approaches requires specifying the action interface, base controller, feedback signals, and operating con-
ditions. Figure 16 places action interfaces upstream of feedback control; the interface determines which responsibilities
belong to the policy and controller.
6.4.1
Motion and Contact Control
Feedback control tracks motion targets while regulating contact forces. Stiff position control can produce excessive forces
when pose errors occur, especially when contact dynamics are faster than policy updates. Position control minimizes
tracking errors, force control regulates contact loads, impedance control converts motion errors into forces, and admittance
control converts measured forces into motion. These approaches differ in how they handle contact and distribute control
responsibilities. Direct torque policies assume more of this responsibility, although actuator loops and independent limits
remain.
44


For n revolute joints, let qt ∈Rn denote joint angles and ¯at = qdes
t
the policy’s desired configuration. A stiffness–
damping controller computes joint torques as
ut = K (¯at −qt) + B (˙¯at −˙qt) + ft,
(9)
where ut and ft are torques in N m, K is stiffness in N m/rad, and B is damping in N m s/rad. The feedback terms resist
position and velocity errors, while ft can compensate gravity, friction, or contact loads. The sans-serif symbols distinguish
controller quantities from the executed prefix K in Eq. (2). This formulation does not explicitly shape inertia, and piecewise-
constant targets require a defined desired velocity, such as zero during holds.
Position control reduces tracking errors but can generate excessive contact forces at high stiffness. Force control instead
regulates measured forces through force-error feedback. Variable impedance adjusts K and B alongside the target (§ 6.3.1),
balancing responsiveness and damping. Admittance control converts measured forces into target motion for an inner
position controller, whereas direct torque control lets the policy specify ut. In all cases, stability and constraint satisfaction
depend on the complete feedback system.
Three classical formulations underpin these control laws. Hybrid position/force control [256] separates task directions
into position- and force-controlled subsets, such as tangential motion and normal force during wiping. Impedance con-
trol [257] regulates the dynamic relationship between motion and contact force, with Eq. (9) providing a joint-space
stiffness–damping realization. Operational-space control [258] formulates end-effector dynamics and maps task-space
forces to joint torques through the Jacobian transpose. Hybrid control requires identifying constrained directions, while
operational-space dynamic decoupling relies on an accurate dynamics model. Impedance control’s model requirements
depend on its implementation. Learned approaches retain these structures while adapting targets or gains, as reviewed
in [259].
Legged and humanoid robots require whole-body control to maintain balance while tracking commands. Kim et al. [235]
combine MPC for planning ground reaction forces with whole-body impulse control for generating joint commands.
HOVER [236] instead learns unified humanoid control across navigation, loco-manipulation, and tabletop tasks, allow-
ing high-level commands while handling balance below. Model-based constraints and learned balance provide different
assurances, but neither guarantees arbitrary target feasibility.
Controllers correct tracking and contact errors but cannot resolve task-level mistakes. In the cup task, the policy selects
the target, the controller manages contact, and a separate check verifies the grasp. Control gains, update rates, and enforced
limits therefore matter when comparing systems.
6.4.2
Learning in the Control Loop
Learning can modify three parts of a feedback system: the controller output or its parameters, the targets supplied to a
lower-level controller, or the predictive model used to choose actions. These locations assign different responsibilities
to learning and can coexist. In the cup task, engineered compliance can handle small alignment errors while learned
components address uncertain friction or contact.
Controller Corrections and Parameters. Residual Reinforcement Learning [225] combines conventional feedback con-
trol with learned corrections to handle poorly modeled dynamics, demonstrating contact-rich block assembly with unstable
objects. ASAP [260] instead learns action corrections from real data to improve simulated dynamics before fine-tuning a
humanoid tracking policy. However, adding residual corrections does not guarantee stability. Formal assurances require
explicit assumptions about system dynamics, feedback, and residual bounds, together with stability or robustness analy-
sis [22]. Instead of adding an output correction, learning can change controller parameters. Parameter learning retains the
control law in Eq. (9) while learning its gains, as in VICES [252] and Learning Variable Impedance Control [253]. However,
time-varying stiffness and damping can destabilize the system, even without contact. Kronander and Billard [261] derive
stability constraints linking stiffness, its rate of change, and damping. Learned gains therefore require explicit bounds and
stability analysis rather than relying on fixed-gain guarantees.
Targets for Lower-level Feedback. Hierarchical control separates high-level target generation from low-level feedback
execution. NaVILA [262] uses a VLA model to generate spatial language commands, executed by a learned locomotion
policy using proprioception and LiDAR-based terrain information. HumanPlus [149] similarly combines imitation-learned
skill policies with a simulation-trained whole-body controller. High-level targets must remain within the low-level con-
troller’s validated operating limits, with infeasible commands rejected or replanned. The update rates of both levels deter-
mine how quickly errors can be corrected. The retained feedback layer can also be a conventional servo. RMA [37] outputs
joint-position targets at 100 Hz to the A1 robot’s PD controller, using the estimator described in § 6.2.3. Similarly, VLA
policies often rely on position or impedance controllers for tracking and contact response. This resembles hierarchical
control but differs from residual learning: the retained controller need not accomplish the task independently.
Predictive Models for Control. Model-based learning retains the MPC optimizer while learning its predictive model. TD-
MPC2 [90] optimizes actions in a learned latent dynamics model using receding-horizon planning (Eq. (1)), with results
demonstrated in simulation. PDDM [263] uses an ensemble of learned dynamics models and sampling-based optimization
to control a Shadow Hand at 10 Hz through a 1 kHz position controller, enabling in-hand rotation of two Baoding balls
45


using real-world training data. Unlike residual or parameter learning, model learning changes the predictions guiding
action selection, but inaccurate contact dynamics can lead to planning errors that low-level tracking cannot resolve.
In the cup task, policies select grasp targets, compliant controllers handle contact deviations, and higher-level decisions
manage regrasping. Failures must be traced to perception, calibration, timing, or control rather than inferred from symp-
toms alone. A competent engineered controller supplies the baseline for local, measurable errors; residual learning can
address remaining deviations, while more extensively learned control requires broader validation of complex interactions.
Adaptive compliance and better contact sensing may also improve performance without increasing policy complexity.
Across these choices, low-level feedback, multirate execution, and runtime filtering remain essential.
Across the physical design choices in this chapter, body and control design can improve capability without increasing
policy complexity. Missing contact information may call for better sensing, while reachability or compliance failures may
be addressed through tool, workspace, or controller changes. UMI and VICES illustrate these choices without establishing
a universal preference for engineering over learning. Adapting to new robots requires not only training data but also cali-
bration, control adjustment, and maintenance. Shared representations therefore do not eliminate body-specific engineering.
Design decisions should balance these recurring costs against the benefits of policy robustness.
7
Computing Systems
Computation determines when robot decisions and policy updates become usable, within limits on energy, hardware, and
network resources. Because the physical state continues changing, faster components improve task performance only
when they shorten a relevant response path. The chapter follows the choices in Figure 18: characterize workload and
timing (§ 7.1), optimize inference (§ 7.2), and schedule execution (§ 7.3). Hardware (§ 7.4) and network placement (§ 7.5)
constrain these choices, while learning infrastructure supports collection, training, and policy release (§ 7.6).
Figure 18: Computing for physical systems. Performance characterization guides inference optimization and real-time execution,
which constrain one another. Hardware, distributed deployment, and learning infrastructure provide the supporting resources and
organization.
7.1
Performance Characterization
Profiling connects computation to physical response: workload structure identifies what runs and its dependencies (§ 7.1.1),
timing establishes when results remain useful (§ 7.1.2), and resource analysis locates the bottleneck (§ 7.1.3). Planning,
control, and monitoring may require different rates while competing for the same resources.
Figure 19 shows why available actions can still be based on outdated observations and how local feedback supplies a
separate correction path.
7.1.1
Workload Structure
Sensing, estimation, memory, prediction, planning, monitoring, and action generation may share a model or run across
services. Their timing requirements and resource demands differ: contact control needs fresh estimates, whereas planning
may tolerate slower updates supported by local feedback. Quantitative evidence here focuses mainly on action generation
and learning. VLA-Perf [264] separates visual encoding, the vision–language backbone, and action generation. Its models
connect architecture, context length, and execution strategy to deployment costs; accelerating one stage can expose another
bottleneck. ROSA [265] organizes factory tasks as models and services with distinct rates, dependencies, and failure
handling. Profiling inputs, invocation triggers, queueing, and shared resources identifies the critical path and contention
outside it. Equal model-call latencies can therefore have different effects on physical progress.
7.1.2
Latency and Freshness
The response path includes sensing, preprocessing, transmission, queueing, inference, handoff, and actuation. Timestamps
distinguish processing delays from waiting and communication. Latency measures response time, observation age mea-
sures information freshness, and deadline misses identify late responses; action throughput alone establishes none of these.
46


Figure 19: Freshness and execution timing. (a) A moving mug leaves the location associated with an earlier observation. (b)
Observation, result readiness, and execution start distinguish processing latency from observation age. (c) Separate execution
examples show that buffered commands may remain available while their observation ages; fresh local sensing can support corrections
within controller authority. These rows are not event-aligned continuations of the timeline. Correction feasibility and physical
deadlines depend on the task; continuous action supply alone establishes neither freshness nor safe execution.

Physical dynamics set response deadlines: a mobile robot must stop within free space despite sensing delay, braking
distance, and tracking error, while a manipulator must correct contact before forces exceed tolerance. MAVBench and
Krishnan et al. illustrate compute–motion constraints in aerial robots [266, 267]. Rare delays matter even when average
latency is low. For a serial, non-overlapping sensor-to-actuator path, total latency and the deadline requirement are

Rare delays can dominate task risk [21, 268], while ROS 2 middleware introduces additional latency and jitter [269].
For chunked VLAs, FASTER [270] shows that reaction time depends on both time to first action and the executed chunk
horizon K (Eq. (2)), producing a distribution of response times. Profiling should therefore report latency percentiles,
deadline-miss rates, observation age, disturbance-to-response time, and closed-loop accuracy under realistic load.
7.1.3
Resource Bottlenecks
Profiling distinguishes compute limits, memory-bandwidth limits, and launch or synchronization overhead. These require
different remedies, and parameter count alone predicts none of them. Observation processing may dominate computation
and activation memory, while repeated action generation can be limited by weight traffic [21]. Batch size, precision,
sequence length, caching, and concurrent workloads can change the limiting resource. MemGuard provides CPU DRAM
47


bandwidth reservations, but its guarantees do not extend directly to GPUs or NPUs [271]. Roofline analysis supplies an
idealized bound that must be checked against measured stage costs.
The roofline model relates arithmetic intensity I = F/B, measured in FLOPs F per byte B transferred, to an idealized
throughput bound:
P(I) = min
 Ppeak, βI

.
(12)
Here, Ppeak is peak compute throughput and β is memory bandwidth. A kernel is memory-bound when I < Ppeak/β
and compute-bound otherwise; actual throughput may be lower due to utilization and overhead [272]. Prefix–expert poli-
cies process observation conditioning once and repeatedly evaluate an action expert. VLA-Perf models these architecture-
dependent costs [264]; other architectures require their own execution decomposition. Roofline limits do not capture
utilization, launches, synchronization, or overlap.
Analytical models identify potential bottlenecks, while profiling validates them. On a Hopper GPU at batch size one,
PhyAI reports that the action expert in π0.5 accounts for only 8.8% of FLOPs but 57.2% of latency; its latency share drops
to 13.5% at batch size 32 as weight traffic is amortized [273]. Similarly, profiling MolmoAct [35] on Jetson Orin and Thor
attributes up to 75% of end-to-end latency to memory-bound action generation [274]. CrossVLA also identifies iterative
denoising as the dominant action-sampling cost, limiting gains from prefix caching alone [275]. These results highlight the
importance of action-generation bottlenecks, but their quantitative implications depend on the model, hardware, precision,
and batch size. VLA-Perf’s reported intensities place prefix components in the compute-bound regime and the action
expert in the memory-bound regime on RTX 4090, A100, H100, and B100 configurations [264]. Its Thor balance point is
excluded because peak-throughput precision and dense/sparse conventions are unclear. Cross-device conclusions require
consistent arithmetic, traffic, and power assumptions, alongside measured phase timings such as those reported for GPUs
and edge NPUs by Zhou et al. [276].
These workload and timing measurements guide optimization: input reduction targets early computation, compression
targets memory traffic, and fewer generation steps reduce repeated work. Yet compression can worsen completion time or
motion quality without changing success rates [277]. Profile again after optimization: the limiting resource may shift, and
queue management or local feedback may improve response more than another reduction in model latency.
7.2
Inference Optimization
Inference optimization targets five costs: inputs (§ 7.2.1), model size and depth (§ 7.2.2), sequential decoding (§ 7.2.3),
repeated computation (§ 7.2.4), and runtime overhead (§ 7.2.5) [278, 279]. These interventions can combine, but speedups
cannot simply be multiplied as bottlenecks shift. Unless noted, results below come from each study’s own tasks, hardware,
and baselines. Scheduling the resulting actions is treated in § 7.3.
7.2.1
Input Reduction
Reducing views, resolution, patches, or tokens saves computation but can omit contact cues or small objects. Relevance-
based selection differs from reuse across calls: an unchanged feature may still be essential.
Fixed-budget Pruning and Merging. FastV prunes visual tokens at a chosen depth and retention ratio, while ToMe
merges similar attention keys with size-weighted averaging [280, 281]. Neither explicitly models changing task-critical
regions, and FastV’s original visual-question-answering evidence requires validation for robotic manipulation.
Task- and Action-aware Selection. EfficientVLA combines relevance and coverage-based token selection with layer
pruning and action-head caching, reporting a 1.93× CogACT speedup with a 0.6% success-rate drop on SIMPLER; to-
ken selection’s contribution is not isolated [282]. SpecPrune-VLA adapts history-, attention-, and layer-based pruning to
end-effector speed, whereas VLA-InfoEntropy uses image and task-related attention entropy without motion signals or
retraining [283, 284].
Both selection strategies need validation of their effect on subsequent decisions. Token selection can introduce closed-
loop failures invisible in single-step evaluations. The Gate, Not the Cache [285] shows that when token selection relies on
previous accelerated outputs, skipped information can cause errors to accumulate. At a 0.9 skip ratio on LIBERO-Object,
success drops from 1.00 (dense) to 0.68 with token reuse and 0.31 with deletion, without detection by the tested action-level
error checks. An unconditional dense pass executed off the critical path refreshes the selection signal, restoring success
to 0.98 while reducing serving latency by 18–22% relative to dense inference. Selection is therefore useful only when
savings exceed its overhead and closed-loop tests confirm that omitted information does not compromise later decisions.
7.2.2
Model Compression
Compression targets memory capacity and weight traffic (Eq. (12)). Quantization lowers precision, compact models re-
duce capacity, and layer skipping changes depth per call. Their latency gains depend on hardware kernels and execution
overhead; their action errors can accumulate through feedback.
48


Quantization. Quantization reduces weight traffic but improves latency only when supported by efficient hardware kernels.
OpenVLA [28] reports that 4-bit quantization preserves real-robot success (71.9% versus 71.3% with bfloat16 across 8
tasks and 80 rollouts per configuration), while 8-bit quantization reduces success to 58.1%. The authors attribute this
decline to quantization overhead that slows inference and alters closed-loop dynamics. GPTQ minimizes calibrated layer-
output errors using approximate second-order information [286]. QuantVLA extends post-training quantization to the
language backbone and diffusion head while retaining floating-point attention projections; SQAP-VLA jointly optimizes
quantization and token pruning to address their incompatibility [287, 288].
Low precision can also be incorporated during training. Quantization-aware imitation learning adapts weights to low
precision for edge deployment [289]. BitVLA combines a ternary backbone and 1.58-bit vision encoder with full-precision
connector and action head, matching full-precision OpenVLA-OFT in its reported evaluation with 11.0× less model mem-
ory [290]. Runtime gains still require efficient low-bit kernels. Within quantization, bit allocation can prioritize action
sensitivity. QVLA assigns channel precision using action sensitivity, retaining 98.9% of OpenVLA-OFT’s LIBERO per-
formance with 29.2% of its VRAM; ActQuant instead allocates precision per matrix and adjusts scales using action-aware
curvature [291, 292]. Both evaluate quantization in action space rather than relying on language-model metrics.
Reduced Execution Depth. EfficientVLA statically removes language layers. Dynamic alternatives include budget-
constrained exits in DeeR-VLA, spatial–temporal routing and self-distillation in MoLe-VLA, and action-aware skipping
in DySL-VLA [282, 293–295]. Variable execution depth makes tail latency and deadline misses essential (Eq. (11)).
Compact Models.
Smaller models reduce deployment cost through transferred knowledge or architecture choices.
TinyVLA pairs a small multimodal backbone with a diffusion decoder; SmolVLA targets single-GPU training and
consumer deployment; TurboVLA replaces the language-model backbone with separate encoders and lightweight
interaction [124, 125, 296].
Compression should be compared by task performance and generalization alongside latency, memory, and training cost.
Unsupported sparsity or dequantization overhead can erase nominal savings, while runtime optimization cannot restore
capacity lost through compaction.
7.2.3
Efficient Action Decoding
Action generation repeats backbone passes for autoregressive heads or expert passes for diffusion and flow heads. Prefix–
expert architectures reuse encoded context (§ 7.1.3), so call count alone does not determine latency. The methods below
shorten dependencies, reduce refinement, or distribute it across calls (Figure 10); continuity additionally depends on exe-
cution handoff.
Shorter Decoding Dependencies. Autoregressive action heads generate tokens sequentially, making decoding latency
depend on sequence length. OpenVLA-OFT [104] replaces sequential decoding with parallel chunk prediction, continuous
actions, and L1 regression. On an A100, its reported request latency decreases from 0.240 to 0.073 seconds, while action
throughput increases from 4.2 to 109.7 actions/s. The larger throughput gain reflects multiple actions per request rather
than an equivalent latency reduction. Other methods retain autoregressive decoding while reducing dependencies. PD-
VLA [107] uses parallel fixed-point iterations, CEED-VLA [297] accelerates their convergence through distillation and
early exit, and Spec-VLA [298] uses speculative decoding with distance-based token acceptance. However, token proximity
may not reflect physical action similarity, requiring task-level validation. FAST accelerates π0 training through frequency-
space action coding but remains sequential and is slower at inference than the compared flow-matching configuration [105].
Training and inference efficiency must therefore be separated.
Fewer Refinement Steps. For diffusion and flow heads, reducing sampling steps decreases the number of expert eval-
uations; the latency benefit depends on how much total inference time the expert consumes. Consistency Policy [299]
distills a pretrained Diffusion Policy into a few-step generator, while One-Step Diffusion Policy (OneDP) [300] uses KL-
divergence-based distillation for single-step generation at 2–10% additional pretraining cost. On a V100, OneDP reduces
encoding-plus-generation latency from 669 ms (100-step DDPM) to 16 ms, supports 20 Hz real-robot control, and achieves
competitive or improved task success. Training-free AdaVLA adapts sampling steps and MLP pruning to flow curvature;
FASTER prioritizes immediate actions so execution starts before the whole chunk is refined [270, 301]. DriftingVLA
instead learns a one-pass noise-to-action map [302]. These approaches respectively adapt work online or move approxi-
mation into training.
Refinement Across Calls. Streaming methods distribute denoising across successive calls rather than reducing total re-
finement steps. Streaming Diffusion Policy [303] maintains a buffer of partially denoised action chunks, executing the
cleanest chunk while refining future ones, although its real-world Push-T evaluation pauses execution during sampling.
FlashVLA [304] extends this approach to flow-matching VLAs using multi-noise-level buffers and chunk-wise causal at-
tention, producing one executable chunk per forward pass. It requires fine-tuning and initial warm-up with safe default
actions, but achieves at least 30 Hz in real deployment. Reflex [305] improves streaming efficiency through incremental
key–value caching, phase-gated normalization for BFloat16 stability, and asynchronous visual encoding. Its evaluations
report faster streaming and lower reaction latency. However, eliminating execution pauses also requires appropriate asyn-
chronous scheduling.
49


7.2.4
Computation Reuse
Computation reuse caches inference intermediates rather than task experiences or skills. Fixed-prefix key–value reuse
within a request can be exact; reuse across denoising steps or observations is generally approximate. The cached tensor,
attention dependencies, and refresh conditions determine validity. Prefix caches are distinct from the representation c in
§ 3.4, and savings shared with decoding optimization must not be counted twice.
Exact Reuse of Fixed Conditioning. In π0, the observation prefix cannot attend to noisy action tokens, so its key–value
tensors remain fixed across flow-matching steps [44]. They are computed once per observation and reused without changing
model outputs; each new observation requires a new prefix cache.
Approximate Reuse Across Denoising Steps. DeepCache reuses high-level U-Net features in image diffusion, while Effi-
cientVLA periodically refreshes cached attention and MLP outputs in CogACT’s action head [282, 306]. The latter reports
a 1.23× caching-only speedup on one SIMPLER task with little FLOP reduction, suggesting memory and overhead savings.
Zhou et al. combine caching with asynchronous GPU/NPU execution [276]. Unlike prefix reuse, these approximations
can alter actions and require comparison with reduced-step sampling across deployment-relevant conditions.
Reuse Across Observations. VLA-Cache recomputes task-relevant tokens while reusing stable visual key–value features;
Adaptive Visual Token Caching learns selection and reuse ratios [307, 308]. ActionCache instead retrieves intermediate
actions from similar contexts to warm-start generation [309]. Savings must include lookup and refresh costs, without
counting warm-start step reductions twice.
Across these reuse scopes, cache validity depends on physical and task changes. Motion, instructions, and task transitions
can invalidate cached information even when pixels or lookup keys remain similar. Excessive refresh erases savings,
while visual caching cannot remove dominant expert costs [275]. Hardware exploiting temporal redundancy faces similar
limits [310–313]. Matched cached/uncached trials should include sudden changes and measure hit rates, bypassed work,
and the combined effects of caches.
7.2.5
Runtime Implementation
At batch size one, launches, allocation, synchronization, and Python dispatch can dominate overhead. Kernel fusion re-
duces traffic, graph replay reduces launches, preallocation avoids repeated allocation, and scheduling overlaps work. Com-
parisons need optimized baselines such as TensorRT or torch.compile, with hardware, shapes, batch size, and preprocessing
specified.
Kernels, Fusion, and Graph Replay. FlashAttention reduces attention memory traffic without changing its mathe-
matical output; its original speedups are not VLA measurements [314]. Batch-one execution also exposes launch over-
head [315, 316]. Ma et al. combine CUDA graphs, graph simplification, and specialized kernels for a π0-level policy,
reporting lower latency than openpi/JAX and naive PyTorch under their tested configuration [316]. Their proposed stream-
ing rate counts buffered action supply, not fresh-observation decisions. ActionFlow [317] instead improves throughput by
overlapping decoding from earlier requests with prefill from new ones. This multi-request scheduling increases OpenVLA-
7B throughput by 2.55× on edge hardware, though it requires concurrent in-flight requests and does not imply an equivalent
reduction in single-request latency.
Runtime optimizations are often deployed together with model-level approximations. FlashDrive combines cross-frame
caching, speculative reasoning, adaptive denoising, quantization, and compiled kernels for a driving VLA [318]. Its end-
to-end gains cannot be attributed to runtime optimization alone, and approximation must be checked for effects on action
quality.
Portable and Unified Runtimes. vla.cpp provides PyTorch-free execution and architecture-specific kernels; Embod-
ied.cpp combines modular execution with multirate control; PhyAI uses model adapters over shared kernels, graph execu-
