tion, and memory management [273, 319, 320]. They report lower latency or memory use than their respective baselines,
though specialized runtimes can be faster [316]. Portability still requires fixed-input and closed-loop checks: vla.cpp iden-
tifies positional-index precision as behaviorally significant in SmolVLA. Benchmarks must separate model execution from
preprocessing and host–device transfers.
Optimizations trade input coverage, precision, capacity, or repeated work against cost. Their value depends on which bot-
tleneck remains and whether approximation errors alter subsequent observations. Matched runtime baselines and closed-
loop trials are therefore complementary; neither operation counts nor fixed-input speedups establish physical gains.
7.3
Real-Time Policy Execution
Execution scheduling determines when computed actions affect the body. Receding-horizon execution chooses how much
of a chunk to use (§ 7.3.1); asynchrony overlaps inference and action supply (§ 7.3.2); handoff connects new and committed
actions (§ 7.3.3); and multirate execution separates slow decisions from fast feedback (§ 7.3.4). Together they govern
continuity and timely response (Figure 19).
50


7.3.1
Receding-Horizon Execution
Action chunks supply motion during inference: ACT predicts coherent sequences and Diffusion Policy generates them
through denoising [102, 103]. Buffered execution avoids waiting but relies on earlier observations. In receding-horizon
execution, the robot executes K of H predicted actions before replanning (1 ≤K ≤H); full-chunk execution corresponds
to K = H. Inference may be synchronous or overlapped with execution. For example, π0 [44] predicts H = 50 actions
but replans after 16 actions at 20 Hz or 25 at 50 Hz, corresponding to 0.8 and 0.5 s. Thus, chunk horizon, executed prefix,
and control rate are distinct. During execution, local feedback and safety monitoring may continue, but new observations
do not alter the high-level action sequence unless it is interrupted or regenerated.
For example, with 100 ms inference latency and a 20 ms action interval, observation ages increase from 100 to 120,
140 ms, and beyond, despite a 50 Hz action rate. Longer executed prefixes amortize inference and reduce synchronous
pauses but delay policy updates, while shorter prefixes improve responsiveness at the risk of discontinuities. RTC [9]
demonstrates this trade-off: its dynamic Kinetix experiments show that maintaining action continuity enables shorter
prefixes and more frequent corrections, improving task success. Evaluation must therefore consider observation age, action
throughput, and task accuracy together. A disturbance’s position within the executed prefix, inference delay, and local
correction determine reaction time [270, 319]. The choice of K balances revision frequency against inference and handoff
costs; a larger K delays updates. PolicyTrim uses RL to lengthen prefixes and reduce task steps, with a small success-rate
decrease in its LIBERO-Object evaluation [321]. The useful prefix therefore depends on both inference cost and available
interruption or feedback.
7.3.2
Asynchronous Execution
Overlapping inference with execution avoids synchronous pauses when buffered actions cover computation and handoff
delays. However, the robot moves while a new chunk is generated, so its actions may no longer match the current state.
Action representation and handoff rules determine the consequences (§ 7.3.3). Coverage must be checked at each handoff
under variable delays. A prefix-preserving schedule must supply both committed actions and their continuation. Sufficient
buffer length alone does not ensure smooth motion: RTC addresses the discontinuities caused by naive overlap through
inpainting [9].
SmolVLA decouples action prediction and execution through a queue [125, 322]. It requests a chunk when the remain-
ing fraction falls below η, then aggregates the response with queued actions. Setting η = 0 gives sequential execution;
earlier requests reduce depletion risk. Near-duplicate observations are skipped unless the queue is empty. Runtimes need
timestamps, bounded queues, cancellation, and expired-result handling. Evaluation should distinguish timely execution,
discarded proposals, and safety-modified actions (§ 9.3.2), alongside sensor-to-actuator delay, task progress, and interven-
tion cost. Longer buffers tolerate delays but can prolong outdated behavior.
7.3.3
Chunk Handoff
During asynchronous execution, a new action chunk must connect with actions already executed or committed while
responding to new observations. Handoff methods intervene at three locations: the input state used for prediction, the
generation or selection of a compatible continuation, and the fusion of output trajectories. Naive replacement can cause
discontinuities, while averaging distinct behavioral modes can produce invalid motion. All three approaches must preserve
actions already executed or committed during inference and supply a compatible continuation; smoothness alone does not
establish correct behavior. Buffer and request timing depend on the schedule and delay assumptions, with remaining
coverage checked at each variable-delay handoff. Predicting from the expected post-prefix state does not remove the need
to match committed motion.
Input-state Prediction. VLASH predicts future proprioceptive state from the preceding chunk and conditions the next
inference on that state [323]. Temporal-offset fine-tuning reduces reaction latency without architectural changes or addi-
tional runtime overhead. Its assumption that preceding actions execute as planned makes it vulnerable to disturbances such
as slipping.
Generation and Selection of Continuations. RTC [9] handles asynchronous handoff through action inpainting, freezing
committed actions and progressively relaxing constraints over the remaining overlap to generate a smooth continuation.
It applies to diffusion- and flow-based VLAs without retraining and demonstrates robustness to inference delays across
twelve dynamic Kinetix tasks and six real bimanual tasks, including precise manipulation such as lighting a match. How-
ever, inpainting adds computation, and its guidance may bias new actions toward outdated trajectories. Bidirectional
Decoding [324] instead maintains continuity through candidate selection. It samples multiple action chunks and selects
one consistent with the previous decision while favoring the trained policy over a weaker policy. Both methods balance
temporal consistency and responsiveness, but Bidirectional Decoding requires generative policies capable of producing
multiple candidates.
Continuity can instead be learned during training. Training-time RTC simulates delays and conditions on action prefixes,
exchanging training for lower handoff overhead. It outperforms inference-time RTC at higher simulated delays and matches
51


its real box-building and espresso performance at lower computational cost [325]. Legato instead trains flow policies with
schedule-dependent mixtures of committed actions and noise; randomized schedules improve continuity under variable
delays [326].
Output-trajectory Fusion. ACT [102] uses temporal ensembling to combine overlapping action predictions through
exponential weights wi = exp(−mi), where w0 represents the oldest prediction and smaller m gives greater weight to
newer observations. It queries the policy at every control step and reports a 3.3-percentage-point improvement across four
tuned ablation settings. However, averaging does not guarantee behavioral consistency, even with deterministic decoding.
The π0 authors found temporal ensembling harmful and instead executed chunks open-loop [44]. VLA-RAIL [327] instead
smooths individual chunks using polynomial fitting and fuses successive trajectories while maintaining position, velocity,
and acceleration continuity. This enables smoother execution without pauses in dynamic simulation and real manipulation.
However, trajectory fusion assumes compatible behaviors and may fail when the policy switches between distinct action
modes.
In a matched-delay comparison, Agouzoul et al. [328] compare handoff methods under delays of up to 20 control steps,
using MLPMixer policies on Kinetix and SmolVLA on LIBERO. A2C2’s per-step correction maintains over 90% success
up to a delay of 8 on Kinetix and leads on LIBERO from a delay of 4. Inference-time RTC performs well at low delays
but degrades with long chunks (H = 30) and high delays. Training-time RTC is the most robust training-based approach
without inference overhead, while VLASH’s performance depends on its fine-tuning delay range. These results favor delay-
aware training and per-step correction over inference-time constraints alone at longer delays, within the evaluated policies,
tasks, and delay ranges. Handoff assessment must therefore test responsiveness and behavioral correctness alongside
trajectory smoothness.
7.3.4
Multirate Execution
Multirate execution separates fast disturbance correction from slower decisions. HiRT and Fast-in-Slow use pathways at
different rates; RMA asynchronously updates environmental representations for a faster locomotion policy [37, 85, 86].
Editing modifies slow outputs, whereas fast inputs or interleaved denoising bring new evidence into policy computation.
An illustrative deployment assigns planning and policy inference to MPUs, GPUs, or NPUs, state and setpoint checks to
an RTOS-based MCU, and current control to motor drives [21]. Rates must follow physical dynamics, not a universal
hierarchy. Runtime assurance separately rejects expired or inadmissible chunks [329–331]; multirate operation does not
itself provide this protection (§ 9.3.2). Each layer must meet its relevant deadline (Eq. (11)).
Real-Time EXPO-FT [332] pairs slower VLA action chunks with a lightweight, reinforcement-learned editor that cor-
rects actions using recent observations. Across four dynamic real-world tasks (30 trials each), successes increased from
50/120 with supervised fine-tuning to 116/120. Online data collection lasted at most ten minutes, excluding prior demon-
strations and setup, and could end earlier upon meeting the success criterion. However, task-specific rewards, human resets,
and independent outcome verification were still required, limiting claims of unattended learning. The results demonstrate
rapid task-specific improvement, but the gains cannot be attributed to the correction layer alone, as both execution and
learning are modified. A2C2 [333] adds a lightweight correction head to an off-the-shelf VLA without retraining the base
policy. At each control step, it uses the latest observation, base action, chunk position, and policy features to generate a
time-aware correction. Under increasing delays and horizons, it improves success over RTC by 23 percentage points on
twelve Kinetix tasks and 7 points on LIBERO Spatial.
πR2 [334] instead separates fast proprioceptive feedback from slower asynchronous vision–language features. Using
diffusion forcing and latency-adaptive flow scheduling, it conditions on in-flight actions and generates outputs in one
denoising step. Applied to GR00T N1.7 on an xArm6 with XHand, it updates actions from fresh observations every 40 ms
on an A5000 GPU, rather than merely executing cached chunks. TIDAL [335] interleaves flow denoising with execution,
caching semantic features at low frequency while updating each denoising step with fresh state and motion cues. Training
with misaligned timing helps compensate for stale intent. In tested settings, it achieves 4× higher feedback frequency
and a 2.5× average gain in dynamic interception over baselines without within-chunk feedback, with slightly lower static
success. Together, πR2 and TIDAL show that fast feedback can be integrated into the policy itself.
Across execution schedules and handoff mechanisms, longer prefixes and overlap sustain action supply, while local
feedback improves disturbance response. Neither guarantees that the high-level goal remains valid or that successive
trajectories are compatible. Evaluation should vary semantic goals, disturbance timing, delays, and network availability;
smooth motion alone cannot establish correct continuation.
7.4
Hardware Platforms
Hardware choices move from distributing workloads across onboard processors (§ 7.4.1) to specialized robotics kernels
(§ 7.4.2) and joint policy–hardware design (§ 7.4.3). Each trades flexibility and coordination against efficiency under
memory, power, and thermal limits. Network placement is addressed separately in § 7.5.
52


7.4.1
Heterogeneous Computing
CPUs support irregular processing and system services; GPUs accelerate dense computation; NPUs favor supported low-
precision operators; MCUs provide predictable control. Splitting a pipeline helps only when gains exceed transfer and
coordination costs. Precision, placement, and shared-resource contention can all move the bottleneck. GRiD generates
parallel rigid-body dynamics and gradient kernels, reporting up to 7.2× speedup over a multithreaded CPU but 2.5× in-
cluding transfers [336]. cuRobo accelerates complete motion generation through collision checking, parallel seeds, and
batched optimization [337]. Policies that output poses can shift substantial costs to these downstream computations. Tem-
poral reuse, sparsity, mixed precision, and locality-aware designs improve onboard feasibility [278, 338, 339]. Device
comparisons need matched precision, power, bandwidth, and operator assumptions (Eq. (12)). Full-task measurements
must capture transfer overhead, contention, and thermal throttling missed by short benchmarks.
7.4.2
Accelerators for Robotics Kernels
Dedicated hardware exploits structure in repeated dynamics, localization, and collision computations. Its benefit depends
on runtime share and transfers, while changing bodies or algorithms can reduce the value of specialization. Robomor-
phic Computing [340] specializes hardware to robot morphology, parallelizing computations across limbs and links while
exploiting joint-specific sparsity and constant link inertias to reduce circuit complexity. Its FPGA implementation accel-
erates rigid-body dynamics gradients, achieving a 1.9–2.9× coprocessor speedup including input/output transfers. Ro-
boShape [341] extends this approach across robot morphologies by exploiting shared topology patterns, while GRiD pro-
vides a GPU-based alternative.
Hardware specialization can target fixed algorithms, algorithm families, or shared representations. Navion accelerates
visual–inertial odometry [342], while other designs target point-cloud search, localization, and collision checking [343–
347]. More flexible approaches include ORIANNA and reconfigurable optimization accelerators [348–350], while GSCore
and related designs specialize in Gaussian-splatting representations [351–353]. Evidence ranges from fabricated chips
(Navion) to FPGA implementations, ASIC synthesis, and simulation, which should not be treated as equivalent. Ultimately,
end-to-end gains depend on the accelerated kernel’s share of execution time, transfer overhead, and continued relevance
as algorithms evolve [354].
7.4.3
Co-Design for Learned Policies
Policy–hardware co-design changes workloads and their implementation together. It must preserve task performance un-
der deployment limits as model changes alter or remove the accelerated computation. Corki predicts short trajectories
and converts them to torque using an FPGA; ReCA targets cooperative embodied agents [96, 355]. SpecVLA co-designs
speculative long-chunk generation and lightweight verification around scene motion, unlike decoding-only Spec-VLA
(§ 7.2.3) [356]. Focus instead reduces redundant VLM tokens through a streaming datapath [357]. DiTPA exploits re-
dundancy across action trajectories, denoising steps, and multimodal features, extending beyond image-diffusion accel-
erators [311–313, 358]. Deltoris targets consecutive observations through bit-level deltas, speculative loading, and cus-
tomized arrays [310]. VQVLA uses motion-adaptive codebooks and reuses centroid products [359]. Their gains depend
on temporal similarity and reliable decisions about when reuse or reduced precision remains appropriate.
RoSÉ and AutoPilot support pre-silicon hardware–software co-design through full-stack simulation and automated SoC
design [360, 361]. However, hardware evidence varies and must distinguish fabricated chips, FPGA implementations,
RTL simulation, and analytical estimates. Corki uses an FPGA, while Deltoris, SpecVLA, VQVLA, and DiTPA report
simulation-based results rather than fabricated chips [310, 356, 358, 359]. These studies primarily measure inference
acceleration, not complete sensor-to-actuator latency (Eq. (11)).
Across hardware platforms, specialization is valuable while its target remains a significant runtime cost after software op-
timization. Reusable primitives tolerate model changes better than fixed decoding or denoising hardware [354]. Compare
hardware acceleration with workload simplification using task performance, sustained energy use, and deadline compli-
ance; kernel speedups and pre-silicon estimates alone do not establish deployment gains.
7.5
Distributed Deployment
Offboard resources relax onboard power and memory limits but add network delay, queues, and failures. We examine com-
putation placement (§ 7.5.1), the information crossing a split-inference link (§ 7.5.2), and fleet-wide scheduling (§ 7.5.3).
These extend the hardware choices of § 7.4 from one robot’s capacity to communication and shared-resource constraints.
Figure 20 connects shared inference to versioned experience and policy release, while separating server validation from
compatibility checks for activation on each robot.
53


Figure 20: Shared inference and versioned deployment. (a) Requests and returned actions identify both the robot and policy version,
while control remains local. (b) Episode records retain collection versions through training and release validation. Per-robot
compatibility checks precede activation at an appropriate handoff; otherwise the current version remains active. Dashed paths are
pending, so robots A and B retain versions v1 and v2. Network availability, buffer coverage, and feasible local fallback require separate
validation.
7.5.1
Computation Placement
Compute placement must deliver sufficiently fresh actions with defined failure handling, subject to compute, memory,
power, traffic, queueing, and network constraints. Server sharing and model partitioning are additional choices: a shared
server may host an entire model or selected stages.
Onboard Execution. Local policies avoid network dependence but face power, thermal, and memory constraints. Cross-
platform VLA measurements show nonlinear effects of power caps and configurations where edge devices match older
datacenter GPUs [362]. Appropriately sized edge devices can improve cost and energy efficiency while meeting control
rates [264, 276].
Edge, Cloud, and Shared Execution. FogROS2 automates cloud placement for SLAM, grasp planning, and motion
planning [363]. Continuous policies additionally need delay-aware execution, such as RTC or VLASH [9, 323]; ROSA
coordinates fleet models on shared GPUs [265]. Faster remote inference must compensate for transmission, queues, and
availability risk.
Across placement options, compare placement, partitioning, and sharing jointly by cost, energy, deadlines, and physical
outcomes under intended load. Split models add representation-dependent transfer costs; local emergency responses must
remain feasible during disconnection. Model settings and platform resources introduce further task-dependent choices
(§ 10.2.3).
7.5.2
Collaborative Inference
Collaborative inference can place the whole policy remotely or divide computation across a feature interface. Intermediate
tensors and task-level features are different interfaces within the latter choice, while compact task messages may connect
separate services rather than layers of one model. The interface changes bandwidth, privacy exposure, latency, and the
capability retained locally during delays.
Remote Inference on Raw Observations. Running the entire policy remotely avoids onboard model storage but makes
decisions depend on image transmission and server response [264]. Local control and protection can remain onboard.
Feature Partitioning. Neurosurgeon selects DNN split points using computation, feature size, bandwidth, energy, and
server load [364]. Robot deployments add physical deadlines. RAPID uses body-motion and contact signals to choose
between remote inference and cached actions [365]; EcoVLA dynamically selects energy-efficient partitions under latency
constraints [366]. Normal-operation gains do not establish resilience to disconnection. A task-level split can preserve a
local action head with access to fresh observations. Peng et al. pair cloud features from delayed observations with current
local vision, training on current/delayed frame pairs with shared action targets [367]. This improves delay tolerance on four
LIBERO suites, provided the slow task context remains valid. FleetAgent uses compact messages for fleet teleoperation
assistance [368].
Intermediate tensors may exceed compressed image sizes, and fewer remote calls can increase staleness. RAPID reports
lower latency for cloud-only than collaborative execution, illustrating the energy–response trade-off. Feasibility depends
54


on both deadlines and fresh information; safe local stopping or holding does not establish autonomous task completion
after disconnection.
7.5.3
Multi-Robot Serving
Shared serving must account for robot deadlines and action-buffer consumption. Clockwork demonstrates predictable
DNN scheduling and deadline-safe batching [369]; robot serving additionally needs physical response requirements and
robot-reported state.
Buffer-aware Scheduling. Armory schedules lookahead inference using buffer levels and consumption rates [370]. Het-
erogeneous execution rates weaken simple arrival-order heuristics; its heterogeneity-aware scheduler improves real-fleet
throughput by up to 18%, with configurable buffer-depletion trade-offs. Coverage must still be distinguished from feedback
freshness.
Task-objective Scheduling and Multi-model Pipelines. ROSA coordinates shared models around factory productivity,
task-specific service objectives, and failure handling [264, 265]. It reports higher productivity than dedicated serving in
real and synthetic evaluations. Scheduling must balance batching with per-robot rates and deadlines, since queued requests
can become stale.
Either scheduling objective also requires reliable admission and failure handling. Admission control protects deadlines,
health checks detect stalled streams, and standby services must retain compatible action interfaces. ROSA handles serving
failures; STRANDS preserves service progress; RLinf-USER preserves asynchronous learning experience [265, 371, 372].
Software restoration alone cannot establish physical resumption. Fault injection must test fresh observations, rejection of
stale results, and the continuation conditions in § 9.3.
Across distributed deployment choices, placement trades local capacity against communication, and scheduling trades
pooling gains against individual urgency. Aggregate throughput can hide starvation or repeated buffer depletion. Compare
per-robot task progress, deadlines, energy, and behavior under disconnection to determine whether shared computation
improves useful service.
7.6
Learning Infrastructure
Learning infrastructure connects rollout collection (§ 7.6.1), training (§ 7.6.2), and policy release and activation (§ 7.6.3).
Stages may overlap, but records and deployed policies must remain traceable across versions. Their joint objective is time
to validated improvement, not optimizer or environment-step throughput alone.
7.6.1
Rollout Infrastructure
Trajectory collection is limited by simulation speed, physical motion and resets, or human attention, depending on the set-
ting. It also requires failure handling and usable records. Useful-episode throughput accounts for rejected and incomplete
data, making it distinct from environment-step and optimizer throughput.
Parallel Simulation. The GPU simulators discussed in § 4.4.1 combine physics, rendering, and learning while reducing
CPU transfers [6, 164–166]. Cheap resets enable scale, but memory and rendering become bottlenecks; RLinf reports
memory-limited parallelism with nearly constant per-step environment time [373].
Autonomous Physical Interaction. Hardware collection is limited by motion, resets, and usable records. SERL integrates
rewards, control, and resets; DayDreamer sustains model-based learning with reduced reset needs in selected tasks [8, 202].
Their learning mechanisms are covered in § 5.3; the infrastructure requirement is keeping collection operational.
Human-supported Operation. HIL-SERL and Learning While Deploying combine ongoing collection with human cor-
rections, while AutoRT explicitly schedules supervision across robots [152, 155, 229]. Throughput must include operator
capacity, preparation, resets, faults, and maintenance, rather than counting only active execution.
7.6.2
Training Pipelines
Training can be limited by data decoding, memory, communication, environment stepping, or checkpoint I/O. Faster input
processing addresses idle accelerators, partitioning addresses capacity, and pipeline scheduling balances stages. Live robot
data adds collection and persistent-storage constraints.
Supervised VLA Training. LeRobot’s video-backed records require temporal frame decoding during batch construc-
tion, which can leave GPUs input-starved [181].
FSDP trades communication for model-state memory by sharding
parameters, gradients, and optimizer states; OpenVLA combines it with mixed precision and FlashAttention [28, 374].
KAITIAN addresses heterogeneous accelerator clusters through performance-aware work allocation and communication
protocols [375].
Reinforcement-learning Systems. RLinf separates workflow definition from device placement and uses profiling-guided
scheduling to overlap environment stepping, inference, and updates [373]. Its reported 1.07–2.43× training-throughput
55


gains depend on the relative stage costs; configurations optimized for simulation must be reassessed with physical inter-
action and reset delays. Physical interaction adds persistent storage requirements to the learning pipeline. RLinf-USER
combines a persistent cache-aware replay buffer, heterogeneous robot interfaces, and adaptive communication for cloud
learning with edge execution [372]. Eviction decisions affect learning as well as throughput when they remove rare fail-
ures or earlier-task experience. End-to-end iteration time should include collection and transport, with pipeline speedups
separated from sample-efficiency gains.
7.6.3
Policy Update Pipelines
Overlapping collection, training, evaluation, and release improves utilization but creates policy lag. Each episode must
identify its behavior policy, and each robot must identify its active configuration; synchronization frequency alone does not
describe end-to-end delay. HIL-SERL decouples execution and learning. RLinf-USER finds that more frequent weight
synchronization does not always improve convergence, partly because updates change the data distribution [155, 372].
IMPALA corrects actor–learner lag with V-trace, requiring behavior-policy probabilities and versioned records [376];
scalable learning from lagged trajectories is distinct from real-time control. Learning While Deploying integrates shared
collection, policy improvement, and redeployment across sixteen dual-arm robots [229]. Its learning workflow is discussed
in § 5.4.7; system coordination must preserve episode provenance while multiple policy versions coexist.
To diagnose delays in this pipeline, observation age measures time since an action’s source observation (Eq. (10)); ex-
perience age measures time since collection; deployed-release age measures time since the executing version was released.
Aligned clocks are required. Age differs from version lag and release-to-activation delay, and does not itself establish fail-
ure: older experience or policies may remain useful. Activation must check observation and action interfaces, controller
conventions, monitors, and filters at controller-consistent boundaries. Rollback must restore a compatible configuration
rather than weights alone. Figure 20 separates release validation from per-robot activation; episode and action version logs
support retention evaluation (§ 8.1.3).
Collection, training, and release improve capability only when experience remains useful and validated policies reach
compatible robots. Evaluate sustained collection, successive updates, retained skills, resumed task progress, and support
costs. These requirements survive changes in model architecture, whereas gains from specialized decoding or denoising
may not. Task-phase-aware computation remains an open comparison against fixed allocation under matched hardware
budgets.
8
Evaluation
Robotic claims should specify capabilities, operating conditions, costs, and human assistance. Since physical testing is slow
and variable, success rates alone can obscure limitations in generalization and sustained autonomy. Evaluation therefore
has four separate decisions, which organize this chapter: what outcome or cost to measure (§ 8.1), where to test it (§ 8.2),
how observed episodes are scored or unobserved outcomes are predicted (§ 8.3), and what protocol makes a comparison
credible (§ 8.4). The discussion moves from defining the claim and its test conditions to examining assessment methods
and the controls needed to interpret results.
Evaluation claims span three scopes: mechanism validation tests components, physical task trials assess integrated
behavior, and sustained operation examines repeated execution, recovery, and long-term support. These scopes differ
from evaluation settings and ablation levels: an offline, simulated, or physical test can support different claims according
to its measured outcome and protocol. Simulation depends on modeling assumptions, physical success does not guarantee
sustained operation, and prediction accuracy does not necessarily improve downstream decisions. Paired comparisons
isolate component effects, while full-system comparisons must account for data, computation, controllers, and human
assistance. Figure 21 connects criteria, settings, model-based assessment, and protocols to the capability claim they jointly
support.
Figure 21: Four evaluation decisions. Criteria determine what to measure, settings where to test, model-based assessment how to
estimate or assess outcomes, and protocols how to compare results. Together they support a credible capability claim; model-based
estimates still require validation against observations.
56


8.1
Evaluation Criteria
Evaluation criteria should be defined before choosing settings or judges. We move from task performance and its success
or failure definitions (§ 8.1.1), to generalization across changed conditions (§ 8.1.2), to sustained operation and retention
over longer timescales (§ 8.1.3), and finally to resource and human costs (§ 8.1.4). This sequence broadens the assessment
from an individual attempt to deployment conditions and the support required to achieve results. A criterion identifies the
quantity and denominator; the experimental protocol later specifies which conditions and data access make the resulting
claim valid.
8.1.1
Task Performance
Success rates depend on the definition of success. In the cup task, binary outcomes may hide wrong placements, retries,
assistance, excessive force, or partial progress. Evaluations should therefore specify completion criteria, time limits, and
the treatment of retries, aborts, and assistance. A 90% success rate with retries is not equivalent to 90% first-attempt
success.
Completion and Partial Progress. Manipulation benchmarks measure success through goal predicates. LIBERO [177]
reports episode success rates, while CALVIN [377] evaluates five-instruction sequences using consecutive completion
rates, whose sum gives the average chain length. BEHAVIOR [378] and BEHAVIOR-1K [163] measure partial progress
as the maximum fraction of satisfied ground literals across valid goal conjunctions. These metrics capture achieved states
rather than time or motion quality. For tasks requiring every stage to succeed, overall success depends on the probabil-
ity of completing each stage conditional on reaching it; high success on individual stages can still yield low end-to-end
completion.
Continuous Tracking and Constraint Satisfaction. Control tasks evaluate trajectories beyond final outcomes. safe-
control-gym [379] measures tracking accuracy, data efficiency, and constraint violations in cart-pole and quadrotor tasks
under repeatable disturbances. In the cup task, constraints may include grip force and end-effector speed near the sink.
Goal completion, tracking accuracy, and constraint satisfaction measure distinct properties: accurate tracking does not
ensure safety.
Beyond scoring the outcome, failure attribution asks which component caused it. Failure categories complement success
rates by revealing distinct failure modes. Robot Learning as an Empirical Science [380] recommends quantitative metrics
alongside qualitative failure descriptions. In the cup task, wrong-object grasps, drops, and operator interventions suggest
different causes but do not confirm them. Component tests can diagnose state estimates, predictions, and memory opera-
tions, but only task outcomes establish downstream effectiveness. Automated failure labels also require validation. Failure
descriptions should therefore accompany completion criteria, denominators, progress measures, and constraint violations,
so that success rates can be interpreted alongside their causes and costs.
8.1.2
Generalization
Success rates reflect only tested conditions, while deployment may introduce changes in objects, cameras, environments,
instructions, or hardware. Generalization tests should specify what changes, by how much, and what was covered during
training to distinguish new settings, skill compositions, and unseen tasks. Data splits, perturbation tests, and shortcut
audits assess complementary aspects of generalization.
Declared Held-out Splits and Levels. Generalization tests should withhold specific training factors. CALVIN’s ABC→D
split tests five-step sequences of known tasks in an unseen environment with different textures, object positions, and instruc-
tions. VIMA-Bench [381] defines four levels: new placements (L1), new combinations (L2), unseen objects and textures
(L3), and unseen tasks and prompts (L4), with four of 17 tasks held out. These distinguish new conditions, compositional
generalization, and zero-shot tasks. In contrast, LIBERO [177] evaluates knowledge transfer and forgetting across spatial,
object, and goal suites. Its standard protocol tests learned tasks under benchmark initial states, not necessarily held-out
factors. Thus, its findings on visual encoders concern these learning protocols rather than unseen-factor generalization.
Perturbation Suites.
Perturbation tests measure generalization through performance degradation under controlled
changes. THE COLOSSEUM [382] evaluates 20 tasks across 14 factors, reporting 30–50% relative success degradation
across five models and at least 75% under combined perturbations. For PerAct, simulated and real-world responses
show moderate agreement across four tasks ( ¯R2 = 0.614), indicating sim-to-real consistency rather than model-ranking
correlation. LIBERO-Plus [243] tests seven factors, identifying strong sensitivity to camera viewpoint and robot initial
state. Camera perturbations reduce third-view-only OpenVLA-OFT success from 95.3% to 16.8% (Table 1). Weak
sensitivity to language changes, supported by further experiments, suggests that models often ignore instructions.
Shortcut Audits. Shortcut audits test whether benchmark scores reflect intended capabilities. LIBERO-PRO [383] varies
objects, initial states, instructions, and environments, reducing models’ standard LIBERO success rates from above 90%
to 0.0% under generalized conditions. Unchanged actions despite altered inputs suggest memorized sequences. A broader
audit [384] identifies shortcut solvability, statistical insignificance, overfitting, and data-source dependence, finding that
a 90-million-parameter model without a language encoder approaches state-of-the-art LIBERO performance. However,
57


audits cannot rule out all shortcuts.
Generalization claims must match tested shifts: held-out splits test unseen conditions, perturbations measure sensitiv-
ity, and audits expose shortcuts. CortexBench [184] finds no universally dominant visual encoder across 17 embodied
tasks. Robustness to individual shifts does not ensure robustness to combinations, and predicting transfer remains an open
challenge. Section 8.4.1 addresses the separate protocol question of whether training and test access actually preserve the
claimed boundary.
8.1.3
Long-Term Performance
Most benchmarks last seconds or minutes, while deployed robots must handle long tasks, multiple skills, and days of op-
eration. These timescales introduce distinct failures: task-level tracking and recovery errors, forgetting previously learned
skills, and deployment disruptions involving batteries, networks, or environments. Reliability at one timescale does not
guarantee reliability at another, requiring separate evaluations.
Information Retention Within a Task. Long-term memory evaluation should test whether past observations improve
future actions. OpenEQA [61] assesses recognition and retrieval but not downstream action. RoboMemArena [62] eval-
uates closed-loop manipulation across 26 simulated tasks averaging over 1,000 steps, with 68.9% of subtasks requiring
memory and paired real-world tests. Although reactive π0.5 struggles with memory-dependent tasks, it outperforms Mem-
oryVLA overall in simulation (21.5% vs. 15.0%). Establishing memory benefits therefore requires matched ablations of
task outcomes with and without memory, supported by subtask-level failure analysis.
Capability Retention Across Tasks. Continual-learning evaluation measures retention of prior knowledge after learning
new tasks, whether stored in model weights, memory, or skill mechanisms. LIBERO [177] evaluates knowledge transfer
and forgetting across task sequences, finding that sequential fine-tuning improves forward transfer over existing lifelong-
learning methods, while naive supervised pretraining can hinder learning. Forward transfer, backward transfer, and for-
getting depend on task order and reset protocols (Section 8.4.1). Final average performance may hide skills forgotten and
later relearned.
Sustained Operation. Sustained-service evaluation extends beyond task completion. STRANDS [371] reports 104 com-
bined days of autonomous operation across four deployments, including improvement during service. Herzog et al. [385]
report up to 53% reduction in waste contamination (39% under higher occupancy). The π∗
0.6 report [132] describes 13
hours of continuous espresso preparation and over two hours of laundry folding in a new home. However, duration and
outcomes alone do not establish uptime or intervention costs. Evaluations must account for failures, maintenance, and
human support.
Metrics should match timescales: task-level completion and recovery, continual-learning transfer and forgetting, and
deployment uptime, exceptions, and interventions per operating hour. Explicit denominators prevent downtime and assis-
tance from being obscured.
8.1.4
Resource and Human Costs
Policies with similar success rates may differ in execution costs and in the learning or adaptation costs incurred before de-
ployment. Single-robot and shared-compute measurements describe different execution settings; human effort contributes
to both stages. Comparisons therefore need explicit accounting boundaries, including failed attempts and the reuse of
trained policies across tasks.
Execution Costs. For a single robot, efficiency depends on closed-loop latency and energy, not inference alone. RTC [9]
addresses asynchronous action execution, while FASTER [270] links reaction time to time to first action and action prefix
K. Embodied-efficiency studies [277] show that reducing model size, computation, or inference latency may increase
end-to-end costs or degrade motion quality without changing task success. System-level metrics such as completion time,
trajectory smoothness, cumulative joint rotation, and motion energy better capture these trade-offs. Shared execution
additionally incurs fleet-level resource and scheduling costs. For multi-robot serving, efficiency depends on fleet-wide task
completion within deadlines. Armory [370] and ROSA [265] examine latency–throughput trade-offs. Since request latency,
server throughput, and task completion rate are distinct metrics, evaluations must specify the optimization objective.
Learning and Adaptation Costs. Training and adaptation costs include robot-hours for data collection and practice,
human-hours for demonstrations and interventions, and GPU-hours for training. Time to target performance also captures
pipeline delays. Since methods trade off these resources, evaluations should report all costs and amortize one-time expenses
across robots and tasks reusing the policy. Total and per-task costs may lead to different comparisons.
Human effort cuts across learning and execution. Human assistance adds operational costs through resets, interventions,
tool changes, takeovers, and maintenance. Sirius [156] records interventions as both operational costs and corrective
training data. Excluding human effort overstates autonomy, while handover delays further limit intervention effectiveness.
Resource metrics should include failures, retries, and fallback costs within clear measurement boundaries. Energy per
attempt differs from total energy per successful completion, which includes failed attempts and support costs. Module-
level measurements identify cost sources but cannot replace end-to-end evaluation. Bench tests may also overlook resource
contention from concurrent robot processes.
58


For both cost categories, consistent accounting requires a complete closed-loop record. Policies with similar success
rates may differ in latency, deadline misses, motion smoothness, energy, and human assistance. The reported measures
should reflect the task and platform. Success rates need confidence intervals; timing reports should give latency percentiles
and the fraction of required invocations that miss declared deadlines or time out under load, including failed attempts.
Latency and deadline measurements must share endpoints, with cancellations and discarded results reported separately.
When motion smoothness matters, the chosen measure needs explicit coordinates, units, sampling, filtering, and duration.
Energy per successful completion should include failed attempts and is undefined if no attempt succeeds. Intervention
rates need a stated denominator, with resets and assisted successes reported separately. A cost-per-success measure should
aggregate these task-level resources, failed attempts, and amortized learning costs within explicit accounting boundaries.
Evaluation criteria use different denominators: episodes, distribution shifts, task sequences, operating hours, or costs
per attempt or completion. Gains in one metric may not transfer to others, making a single score insufficient to capture
trade-offs among methods.
8.2
Evaluation Settings
Evaluation settings are ordered by the interaction and physical evidence they provide: offline evaluation uses logged data
without new robot interaction (§ 8.2.1), simulation enables modeled closed-loop tests (§ 8.2.2), and real-world evaluation
captures physical execution at higher trial and reset costs (§ 8.2.3). These are settings for collecting evidence, not inter-
changeable measures of capability. A task criterion from § 8.1 can be applied in more than one setting, but its interpretation
changes with data coverage, simulator validity, and physical support costs.
8.2.1
Offline Evaluation
Offline evaluation uses demonstrations, logged episodes, or held-out trajectories without new robot interaction. Shared
test data supports efficient diagnosis and ablation but cannot capture action-dependent state changes, error accumulation,
recovery, or physical contact. Thus, low action error on cup-task demonstrations does not guarantee success when the
policy deviates from recorded trajectories.
Action Fitting and Implementation Diagnosis. The robomimic study [186] evaluates six offline algorithms across five
simulated and three real-world manipulation tasks, highlighting the effects of algorithm design, demonstration quality, and
checkpoint selection. Validation action loss is inexpensive but may poorly predict rollout success when multiple actions are
valid. SIMPLER [386] reports weak correlation between negative action MSE and real-world success across six Google-
robot checkpoints and three task categories (average Pearson r = 0.308), compared with r = 0.924 for visual-matching
simulation. MSE was computed from 25 sampled training demonstrations without a public validation split. Thus, action
loss alone is insufficient for reliable checkpoint selection.
Off-policy Evaluation. Off-policy evaluation (OPE) estimates target-policy returns from logged data without new roll-
outs. Importance sampling reweights returns by target-to-logging action-probability ratios, offering unbiased estimates
under correct ratios and sufficient support but potentially high variance. Fitted Q evaluation uses Bellman updates but
risks approximation and extrapolation errors. Doubly robust estimators [387] combine value predictions with importance-
weighted corrections, providing unbiasedness under correct ratios and independently fitted models while potentially re-
ducing variance. All methods require adequate data coverage or additional assumptions. For robotic grasping, off-policy
classification [388] predicts success using learned Q-values. Across 15 models with real success rates of 17–91%, SoftOPC
achieves a Spearman correlation of 0.94 with real success, versus 0.48 for temporal-difference error. However, DOPE [389]
finds no consistently reliable OPE method across tasks. Estimates improve when logging and target policies are similar,
while only importance sampling consistently achieves positive rank correlation on AntMaze. Thus, OPE supports policy
selection but cannot validate behavior unsupported by logged data.
Offline metrics aid diagnosis and checkpoint selection, but their predictive value depends on data coverage and requires
closed-loop validation. Logged episodes can also validate learned judges before replacing human evaluation.
8.2.2
Simulation Evaluation
Simulation enables scalable, controlled, and repeatable evaluation through inexpensive resets and systematic perturba-
tions. However, results depend on assumptions about perception, dynamics, contact, and control, which may fail for
challenging objects such as wet or transparent surfaces. Benchmarks differ in task diversity and perturbation coverage.
Paired simulation–real-world tests are therefore needed to validate transfer to hardware. Simulation benchmarks stan-
dardize tasks, demonstrations, and success criteria. RLBench [390] provides 100 manipulation tasks with variations and
multimodal observations; CALVIN evaluates long-horizon language-conditioned tasks; and ManiSkill3 [165] supports
GPU-parallel simulation. LIBERO [177] includes three 10-task transfer suites, 90 pretraining tasks, and 10 long-horizon
tasks with demonstrations, subject to limitations in § 8.1.2. Beyond manipulation, Habitat 3.0 [391] supports social nav-
igation and collaborative rearrangement with simulated humanoids or human-controlled avatars, enabling evaluation of
policy rankings in human-interaction tasks.
59


RoboCasa [144] increases scene diversity through varied kitchens, interactive appliances, generated assets, composite
tasks, and automated demonstrations, showing benefits from scaling synthetic data. BEHAVIOR-1K [163] covers 1,000
everyday activities in OmniGibson, including rigid objects, deformable materials, and liquids. It highlights long-horizon
challenges and demonstrates initial sim-to-real transfer in a matched apartment. Both benchmarks emphasize diverse
scenes or tasks rather than controlled perturbations, requiring appropriate evaluation denominators. However, greater
diversity does not eliminate simulator assumptions.
Across simulation suites, paired physical tests are needed to assess whether simulated scores transfer. SIMPLER [386]
reduces visual and control mismatches without requiring full-fidelity digital twins. It validates simulation against real-
world success using Pearson correlation and mean maximum rank violation (MMRV), which measures worst pairwise
ranking errors weighted by real-success differences (lower is better). Its visual-matching simulator achieves r = 0.924 and
MMRV = 0.056 across six Google-robot checkpoints and three task categories, while capturing sensitivity to distribution
shifts. However, agreement is limited to tested policies, tasks, and shifts.
8.2.3
Real-World Evaluation
Physical evaluation captures perception, actuation, contact, and human assistance under real conditions. However, costly
resets, manual scoring, and variations across sites and days limit reproducibility. Centralized facilities, automated evalua-
tion, paired trials, and shared hardware kits address these challenges.
Centralized and Remote Benchmarks. Centralized benchmarks standardize hardware and tasks. The Real Robot Chal-
lenge [392] used eight TriFinger robots at one institute for automated evaluation of submitted code, with random robot
assignment and identical goals. The robots operated for hundreds of hours with minimal failures. This improves compara-
bility but remains limited to one platform design and a narrow task family.
Distributed Paired Evaluation. RoboArena [393] uses distributed, double-blind pairwise evaluations instead of fixed
tasks and sites. Across seven institutions using the DROID platform, it evaluates seven generalist policies in over 600
real-robot episodes and aggregates preferences into rankings, reportedly outperforming centralized evaluation in ranking
accuracy. However, rankings depend on the participating tasks and evaluators, and pairwise preferences do not provide
fixed-task success probabilities needed for deployment decisions.
Across evaluation sites, automation can reduce the cost of repeated testing. AutoEval [394] automates scoring, resets,
and safety monitoring for WidowX arms in BridgeData scenes. It deploys learned success classifiers and reset policies after
exceeding 95% accuracy or success. One cell achieves about 850 drawer-task episodes per day, with three human interven-
tions over 24 hours, and closely matches human-run evaluations (average Pearson r = 0.942). However, classifier errors
may bias success estimates, while reset policies shape the initial-state distribution. Hardware standardization provides
another means of improving comparability across sites. ManipulationNet [395] improves cross-site comparability through
standardized hardware kits and unified task delivery and result collection. It separates manipulation skills (Physical Skills
Track) from multimodal grounding (Embodied Reasoning Track).
Standardized hardware supports absolute capability assessment, paired evaluations reduce local variation, and auto-
mated resets and scoring increase trial counts. Reproducibility also requires documenting initial states, retries, and human
assistance, with confidence intervals and stopping rules predefined for limited trials (§ 8.4.3). Offline evaluation screens
policies using logged data, simulation tests modeled closed-loop behavior, and physical trials assess real-world sensing,
contact, and human-support costs. This staged approach reserves costly hardware trials for cases that cheaper evaluations
cannot reliably resolve.
8.3
Model-Based Assessment
Manual scoring and hardware trials limit evaluation efficiency. We first compare programmed, human, and learned judges
of observed episodes (§ 8.3.1), then examine predictive assessment, which estimates outcomes through modeled rollouts
rather than physical trials of the tested policy (§ 8.3.2). This order separates scoring available evidence from predicting
outcomes that have not been observed; the distinction concerns the source of an outcome, independent of whether the
setting is logged, simulated, or physical. Both approaches risk misclassification or incorrect policy rankings and require
validation.
8.3.1
Learned Outcome Assessment
Evaluation judges differ in their observations and limitations. Programmed predicates assess explicit conditions but may
miss unencoded outcomes or lack real-world state access. Human judges capture broader context but incur costs, fa-
tigue, and disagreement. Learned judges scale scoring from images, videos, or logs but cannot detect unobserved events,
such as hidden force violations or off-camera assistance. Reliable evaluation therefore requires predefined success crite-
ria, sufficient observation coverage, and validated label sources. SuccessVQA [396] uses a fine-tuned vision–language
model (Flamingo) to judge task success from videos across simulated households, real manipulation, and egocentric hu-
man settings. It generalizes better than task-specific detectors to unseen tasks and camera changes, but performs worse in
60


distribution. Its balanced accuracy on egocentric videos drops from 99% on training data to 62% on test data. RoboRe-
ward [194] emphasizes explicit failure examples, finding unreliable reward assignments by tested vision–language models.
OpenEQA [61] similarly uses language models to evaluate open-vocabulary answers about environments.
Judges and predictors are initially validated against labeled outcomes or observed frames, but recorded episodes cover
only observed behavior. High average agreement may hide systematic errors, particularly rare critical failures. Decision
thresholds trade off false accepts and false rejects, while human review improves reliability at additional cost. Evaluations
should report both error rates by failure type at a specified threshold, automatic scoring coverage, and residual errors
after human review. Hidden force violations and operator assistance require additional sensor and intervention records. A
learned judge used as a training reward should not evaluate the final policy, as optimization may exploit its errors. For
example, a policy may make a cup appear clean without actually cleaning it. Final evaluation should use independent
judges, alternative sensors, or sampled human labels. Learned judges can replace human scoring when outcomes are
observable and false-accept and false-reject rates are validated on relevant failures, independently of training rewards.
Otherwise, physical instrumentation or human assessment remains necessary.
8.3.2
Predictive Assessment
Predictive assessment estimates policy outcomes without hardware execution, supporting candidate screening and ranking.
Learned rollout simulators combine transition models with outcome judges, introducing errors from both. Runtime failure
prediction instead guides interventions during execution, as in Model-Based Runtime Monitoring [397] (§ 9.3.1).
Transition Error Under the Policy’s Actions. Predictive evaluation requires world models that accurately follow policy
actions. WorldEval [67] uses Policy2Vec latent-action conditioning to improve action following for policy ranking and
safety assessment. A Veo-based simulator [69] evaluates Gemini Robotics policies through action conditioning, multi-
view consistency, and scene variations. Ctrl-World [68] combines action conditioning, multiview prediction, and memory
retrieval for long rollouts. Its predictions agree with real-world instruction-following results but underestimate execution
success due to contact and motion errors. Out-of-distribution actions may also appear successful despite being physically
infeasible.
Outcome-scoring Error. A second challenge is scoring predicted outcomes. RoboWorld [398] combines a fast autore-
gressive video world model, stabilized by Step Forcing for long rollouts, with task-progress-aware vision–language scoring.
Predictive evaluation inherits both transition-model and judge errors, including false accepts and false rejects. Thus, even
accurately predicted failures may be misclassified as successes.
Both transition and outcome-scoring errors require paired validation. Predictive evaluators require validation against
paired real-world outcomes at clearly defined evaluation units. WorldEval [67] reports per-task Pearson correlations across
four policies, measuring policy-level rather than episode-level agreement. The Veo-based study [69] uses over 1,600 real-
world evaluations of eight checkpoints across five bimanual tasks to assess rankings under nominal and out-of-distribution
conditions and detect safety violations. RoboWorld [398] compares eight policies against RoboArena’s preference-based
ranking. These results demonstrate agreement only for tested policies and metrics, not reliable episode-level predictions
or generalization to unseen policy families. Validation should cover scores, rankings, and failure types, with recalibration
after evaluator changes.
Across learned and predictive assessment, learned judges reduce labeling costs when outcomes are observable, uncertain
cases receive human review, and additional sensors capture hidden conditions. Predictive models reduce rollout costs when
transitions and outcome judgments are validated, with targeted real-world checks for uncertain cases. However, policy
updates may produce states outside the surrogate’s calibration data, requiring renewed validation even without changes to
the surrogate.
8.4
Experimental Protocols
Performance differences between policies may arise from method improvements, information leakage, confounding
changes, or chance. The protocol addresses these threats in order: training–test boundaries establish what information
was available before evaluation (§ 8.4.1), controlled comparisons isolate the conditions behind a difference (§ 8.4.2), and
statistical reporting quantifies uncertainty across trials and other sources of variation (§ 8.4.3). The same safeguards apply
whether evidence comes from logs, simulation, or robots and whether a human or model scores it.
8.4.1
Training and Test Boundaries
New episodes under the same kitchen, objects, and camera setup do not establish generalization beyond those conditions.
Valid generalization claims require separating training and test conditions while accounting for overlap through pretraining,
fine-tuning, and checkpoint selection. For foundation models, even an “unseen cup” may resemble pretraining examples,
making training coverage essential for interpreting results. Evaluation splits must withhold factors relevant to the gener-
alization claim. CALVIN holds out an entire environment, whereas random episode splits may retain the same objects,
scenes, and instructions. Overfitting and data-source dependence can further weaken these boundaries [384]. Evaluations
61


should report training coverage for claimed factors (objects, scenes, instructions, or robot bodies) and describe pretraining
data sources by category.
Repeated checkpoint selection on test data introduces bias, as robomimic [186] shows that stopping criteria affect perfor-
mance. Test-time protocols must specify access to demonstrations, exploration, memory updates, and parameter adaptation.
LIBERO [177] defines continual-learning metrics based on task order, data access, and resets, while RWLA [208] uses
retrieved data for local adaptation, which must count toward the test-time budget. Adapted and non-adapted results should
be reported separately. The resulting generalization claim is therefore bounded by the declared shifts, data partitions, and
target-access budgets.
8.4.2
Controlled Comparisons
Cross-study comparisons often differ in cameras, action rates, observation histories, hardware, and test conditions, compli-
cating attribution. Mechanism comparisons isolate component effects under fixed conditions, while system comparisons as-
sess complete configurations, including costs and support, without attributing gains to individual components. OpenVLA-
OFT [104] illustrates mechanism- and system-level comparisons. On single-image LIBERO, continuous L1 regression
(95.3%) and diffusion (95.4%) perform similarly, outperforming original OpenVLA (76.5%) and supporting simpler de-
coding. Adding wrist images and proprioception raises success to 97.1%, reflecting system-level gains. Table I further
shows the effects of data filtering and inputs: unfiltered multi-input training achieves 94.5%, versus 95.3% for filtered
single-image and 97.1% for filtered multi-input training. Filtering excludes unsuccessful demonstrations and near-zero
actions. Different training budgets and checkpoint selection every 50,000 steps limit equal-compute and independent-test
claims.
Hardware comparisons should control nuisance factors through matched initial states, randomized or interleaved trials,
and operator blinding. RoboArena [393] uses double-blind policy comparisons across sites, while SIMPLER [386] pairs
simulated and real outcomes for the same policies. Fixed setups improve repeatability, while pairing reduces variation and
supports comparisons of absolute outcomes or performance differences. Trial records must include retries and assistance
to distinguish autonomous first-attempt success from assisted or repeated completion, connecting the criteria and protocol
decisions in Figure 21. Policy rankings depend on action rate, hardware, observation history, reset protocols, and trial
count. Changing control rates also alters the evaluated closed-loop system. The performance and cost measures in § 8.1
should be chosen to match the comparison. System comparisons and component ablations must report the conditions under
which these measures were obtained.
Figure 22 connects matched trials to the reported denominator. A completion record must retain whether retries or
human intervention occurred. A protocol may allow autonomous retries within a fixed budget, but that choice must be
explicit, and operator-assisted completion must remain identifiable. Reporting only successful terminal scenes discards
the information needed to interpret a capability claim; Figure 12 shows how to preserve it during collection.
Figure 22: Connecting trial conditions to an evaluation claim. (a) Matched initial conditions and budgets support comparison between
policies. (b) Records distinguish completion without intervention, completion after retry, and assisted completion. These examples
describe provenance rather than a universal set of mutually exclusive outcomes. (c) An illustrative ledger retains failed attempts,
retries, and operator assistance. The success definition, trial unit, retry budget, and denominator must be stated; autonomous retries
and human interventions are distinct. The entries are schematic, not benchmark results.
62


8.4.3
Statistical Reporting
Success rates from few trials are uncertain: 60% versus 70% over 20 trials may reflect sampling noise. Repetition should
match the claim: trials capture execution variability, training seeds capture optimization variability, and scenes, robots,
sites, or operators introduce additional variation. For k successes in n independent trials,
ˆS = k
n,
SE( ˆS) =
s
ˆS(1 −ˆS)
n
.
(13)
At n = 20 and ˆS = 0.5, the SE is approximately 0.11, giving an approximate 95% margin of ±0.22. This approximation
is unreliable near 0 or 1, where Wilson or exact Clopper–Pearson intervals are preferable [399]. With zero failures, the
one-sided 95% upper failure bound is 1 −0.051/n ≈3/n, or 13.9% after 20 trials and 5.8% after 50. For 20 successes
in 20 trials, the two-sided 95% lower success bound is 83.2%. These bounds use different confidence conventions and
do not establish robustness to distribution shifts. Trial counts should reflect expected effect sizes and statistical power.
Distinguishing success rates of 0.6 and 0.7 requires approximately 356 independent trials per policy at 5% significance
and 80% power, before clustering or multiple-comparison adjustments. For matched trials, McNemar’s test uses discordant
pairs, potentially improving efficiency when outcomes are positively correlated.
Trial counts may adapt to intermediate results. A sequential test [400] compares imitation-learning policies using 10–50
trials, enabling early stopping while controlling statistical error and reducing trials by up to 32% at equal correctness and
power. In contrast, Vincent et al. [401] derive fixed-sample bounds from i.i.d. rollouts. Their randomized binomial bound
adds U ∼U[0, 1] to the success count, tightening Clopper–Pearson bounds, while continuous metrics use distribution-
function bounds. For 38 successes in 50 diffusion-policy trials, the one-sided 95% lower bound is 0.641, depending on the
random draw. Unlike sequential tests, these bounds do not support optional stopping.
Robot trials may be correlated through shared setups, operators, or continuous operation. A 1,000-step episode still
counts as one trial, so paired or cluster bootstrapping should resample at the task, run, or site level. Latency-tail es-
timates also require invocation counts and load histories. Deep Reinforcement Learning at the Edge of the Statistical
Precipice [402] highlights the risks of few-run point estimates and recommends confidence intervals, performance pro-
files, and robust aggregates such as the interquartile mean, implemented in rliable. Multitask comparisons should specify
task weights; when site variation dominates, testing more sites or conditions may be more valuable than adding training
seeds.
Robot Learning as an Empirical Science [380] identifies inadequate reporting of trial counts, initial conditions, suc-
cess criteria, uncertainty, and failure modes. A LIBERO audit [384] similarly finds many improvements lack statistical
significance. Such inconclusive differences do not establish equivalence, because precision depends on trial counts and de-
pendencies. Reproducibility requires documenting experimental conditions, metrics, failures, statistical methods, model
and code versions, data subsets, action interfaces, and timing. Changes in control rate or observation history alter the
evaluated system. Rare-event safety claims also require exposure counts and dedicated validation.
Across experimental protocols, method selection requires verifying training data, controlling comparison conditions, and
quantifying statistical uncertainty. Shared implementations and paired trials isolate component effects, while independent
end-to-end replication tests system-level reliability. Mechanism descriptions motivate hypotheses, controlled comparisons
test component effects, and system trials assess integrated performance. Method choices should therefore rely on evidence
appropriate to the intended use. Comparison records should also include task-specific measures such as surrogate agree-
ment (e.g., SIMPLER), failure detection, recovery, and procedural execution. These measures answer different questions
from task success and should retain their own units and denominators.
Across the evaluation criteria, settings, and protocols reviewed in this chapter, the shortcut and perturbation studies in
§ 8.1.2 show that benchmark gains require capability-specific validation, but their findings do not imply that all success-
ful policies lack the claimed capabilities. A score supports a claim only through its criterion, testing setting, outcome
assessment, and comparison protocol together. Interpreting results requires explicit experimental conditions, budgets, and
human assistance. Component-level attribution requires controlled comparisons, cross-study comparisons require compat-
ible conditions, and surrogate evaluations require validated agreement for the relevant policies and conditions.
9
Safety
Robots can cause irreversible harm, such as human injury or equipment damage, or violate task requirements even when mo-
tions execute correctly. These risks arise during learning and operation from sensing, control, or timing failures, learning-
induced changes, and deliberate attacks on inputs, data, or models. Safety requires limiting these risks and intervening
before harm occurs.
Safety constraints can guide learning and planning, while monitoring, action filtering, recovery, and human intervention
protect execution. Defenses against deliberate attacks complement these safeguards, but detection alone cannot prevent
harm without timely and feasible intervention.
63


A hazard is a condition that may cause harm; risk describes its likelihood and severity; a constraint specifies a re-
quirement; and a violation occurs when that requirement is not met. Intervention authority determines who or what can
modify, reject, or replace an action. Safety limits unacceptable physical risks, while security protects against deliberate
compromise. Safety claims therefore require clearly defined hazards, intervention authority, and evidence covering relevant
learning and operating conditions.
Evaluation tests safety claims, whereas safety mechanisms actively limit risk by modifying learning, decisions, or exe-
cution. This chapter first defines hazards and constraints (§ 9.1), establishing what the subsequent safeguards must address.
It then distinguishes protection in learning and planning (§ 9.2) from monitoring and intervention during execution (§ 9.3),
before examining deliberate attacks and defenses (§ 9.4). The organization separates where safeguards act from how ad-
versaries can compromise their assumptions; adversarial threats can affect both learning and execution. Because filters
may alter actions and supervisors may transfer control, their authority and the physical feasibility of intervention must be
explicit.
Figure 23 connects risk formulation to safety during learning, planning, and execution, with adversarial robustness
spanning development and operation.
Figure 23: Safety across development and operation. Risk formulation informs learning and planning as well as runtime safety.
Adversarial robustness spans both, connecting the four subsections without prescribing a single protection architecture.
9.1
Risk Formulation
Before a learner, planner, or filter can ensure safety, the harms to prevent must be expressed as checkable requirements.
Crushing a cup, grasping at an outdated location, and performing a prohibited action, such as mixing incompatible cleaning
agents, involve different causes, signals, and safeguards. The distinction here is between identifying physical and opera-
tional hazards, their causes, and observable signals (§ 9.1.1), and representing the resulting requirements as constraints
that can be checked (§ 9.1.2).
9.1.1
Physical and Operational Risks
Safety analysis begins by identifying events that can cause harm. Physical hazards include collisions, excessive force
or speed, and instability. Operational hazards include stale state estimates, sensor or service failures, and failed control
handoffs. Crushing a cup and executing an outdated grasp may cause similar damage but require different safeguards.
A hazard record specifies the affected person, object, or system; triggering conditions; observable signals; and potential
severity or cost. Force sensors can detect overload, while timestamps reveal stale estimates, but neither verifies correct
task interpretation. Tracing causal paths also distinguishes hazards caused by unsafe plans from those caused by delayed
execution, helping identify where intervention is needed.
System-Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA) [403] systematically identifies unsafe control actions by examining con-
trollers, commands, and feedback. It considers commands that are omitted, issued unsafely, mistimed or misordered, or
applied for an incorrect duration. For example, a grasp based on an outdated pose estimate may become unsafe when a
hand enters the workspace. STPA traces such actions to causes such as delayed feedback or incorrect models and derives
corresponding constraints, although it cannot guarantee model completeness.
Standards provide measurable limits for specific applications. ISO 10218-1:2025 and ISO 10218-2:2025 address indus-
trial robots and their applications, respectively [404, 405]. ISO/TS 15066:2016 addresses collaborative industrial robot
systems [406]. Speed and separation monitoring maintains protective distances accounting for motion, reaction and stop-
ping times, intrusion, and uncertainty. Power and force limiting controls contact through mechanical design or active
control. ISO/TS 15066 distinguishes transient and quasi-static contact, with Annex A relating transient-contact speed to
force or pressure limits through effective mass, contact area, and body stiffness. Active contact protection also requires
reliable detection. Haddadin et al. [244] review proprioceptive collision detection and identification methods. Generalized-
momentum observers estimate external joint torques, while contact localization and force reconstruction require additional
assumptions.
ISO 13482:2014 instead addresses personal-care robots [407]. For a kitchen robot, applicable requirements depend on
intended use and identified hazards. Assessing those hazards requires defined events, causal paths, observable signals,
and applicable measurable limits; task success alone does not establish safety. Learned policies, semantic guardrails, and
64


control-barrier methods provide component-level evidence, but standards conformity must be assessed for the integrated
system under its operating conditions.
9.1.2
Constraint Representations
Once a hazard is identified, its requirement must be formulated in a checkable form. Average episode-level bounds differ
from conditions that must hold at every step. Requirements may specify numerical limits, such as workspace boundaries or
contact forces, or contextual rules involving action order and authorization. Each requires observable evidence or justified
judgment to verify compliance.
State Constraints and Safe Sets. The simplest representation defines admissible states or commands, but satisfying a
constraint does not guarantee future safety. For example, a robot within a position limit may be moving too fast to stop
before crossing it. A barrier-based filter therefore requires a valid barrier whose nonnegative set satisfies the constraints and
remains invariant under available controls (Eq. (15)). safe-control-gym [379] supports reproducible comparisons using
explicit constraints, symbolic dynamics, and disturbances. Such comparisons require consistent constraints and satisfaction
criteria, whether stepwise or in expectation.
Cost Functions and Risk Budgets. A second representation assigns scalar costs to unsafe events, such as collisions, ex-
cessive force, or forbidden-region entry, and bounds their expected cumulative cost over an episode. This budget quantifies
the trade-off between task performance and safety and underlies constrained reinforcement learning (RL; Section 9.2.1).
However, satisfying an expected budget does not guarantee safety on individual trajectories, motivating step-wise runtime
protection.
Formal Rules and Semantic Labels. Some safety requirements concern task rules rather than numerical limits. Temporal
logic expresses ordering and prohibitions, while RoboGuard [408] grounds such rules in the environment to check plans.
Semantic labels capture undesirable situations, as evaluated by ASIMOV [409]. Safety for generalist robots must also
account for context, user intent, and difficult-to-model consequences [410], such as maintenance-specific permissions or
unsafe chemical mixing. Importantly, exact verification of a temporal-logic formula does not guarantee correct grounding
of its predicates. Perception and semantic judgments can produce false accepts or rejects, and these errors must be included
in safety claims.
Each safety representation defines the scope of its guarantees. Extending them beyond collision and force constraints
remains an open challenge. Representing a hazard also leaves an evidence problem: rare violations may not appear in
ordinary trials. Whichever constraint representation is used, rare failures require dedicated validation. Rare failures are
difficult to assess through ordinary trials because accurate Monte Carlo estimates require many samples. Black-box safety
validation [411] addresses this by searching simulated disturbances for failures (falsification) or the most likely failure
(adaptive stress testing). Importance sampling can estimate failure probabilities more efficiently by oversampling failures
and reweighting results. However, probability estimation requires a defined disturbance distribution and statistically valid
sampling, while all findings depend on the simulator’s dynamics and disturbance model. These tests probe the represented
hazard; they do not expand the protection supplied by a learner, plan checker, or runtime filter.
Across risk formulations, timing and shared infrastructure can undermine multiple safeguards, such as when a stale
state estimate affects both planning and filtering. Each safety requirement must therefore specify its observation and
resource dependencies and feasible responses to their failure. These dependencies inform learning and planning and
runtime protection.
9.2
Safety in Learning and Planning
High task rewards do not guarantee safe actions, and language-model-generated plans may violate requirements before
execution. The two approaches differ in where they limit risk: constrained learning (§ 9.2.1) limits risk across policy
training and exploration, while plan validation (§ 9.2.2) checks a particular proposed sequence against rules, information,
and feasibility. Shields and interventions used during exploration cross into runtime protection when they restrict each
executed action. Runtime protection remains necessary because constrained policies may still propose unsafe actions, and
valid plans may lose their preconditions during execution.
9.2.1
Constrained Learning
Task-only objectives may allow learners to trade safety for reward. In Safety Gym [412], unconstrained reinforcement
learning achieved high returns while violating safety costs. Exploration introduces further risks, as unsafe interactions
can damage objects or hardware before a reliable policy emerges. Safety-aware learning addresses these problems by
constraining costs during optimization and limiting unsafe exploration, as reviewed in [22].
Budgeted Constrained Optimization. A constrained Markov decision process (CMDP) maximizes task return subject to
an expected cumulative risk budget:
65
