(14)
Here, JR is the task return of Eq. (7), ct is the risk cost, d is the permitted budget, and γC and TC define cost dis-
counting and horizon, which may differ from those of the reward. Setting γC = 1 gives an undiscounted budget, while
discounting penalizes later violations less. Importantly, an expected budget does not guarantee safety on individual trajec-
tories. Two common approaches solve this CMDP. Lagrangian methods optimize maxθ minλ≥0{JR(θ) −λ[JC(θ) −d]}
with an adaptive penalty, increasing λ when estimated cost exceeds the budget. PPO-Lagrangian applies this approach to
PPO. Constrained Policy Optimization (CPO) [413] instead uses trust-region updates to limit policy changes and main-
tain near-constraint satisfaction theoretically, though practical implementations rely on approximations. In Safety Gym,
Lagrangian baselines enforced cost constraints more consistently than CPO under the evaluated settings [412]. Neither
method guarantees trajectory-level safety, and approximation or sampling errors may cause expected-cost violations.
SafeVLA [414] extends CMDP-based safe learning to vision–language–action (VLA) models. It constructs diverse risk
scenarios and uses alternating policy and multiplier updates to penalize unsafe behavior. On Safety-CHORES, SafeVLA
reports reduced cumulative safety costs while maintaining task success, alongside generalization to out-of-distribution
perturbations and real-world Safety-PickUp transfer cases. These findings apply to evaluated hazards and do not establish
safety against untested risks.
Restricted Exploration and Intervention. Other approaches limit unsafe exploration directly. Shielding [415] uses a
safety game derived from temporal-logic specifications and abstract dynamics to restrict available actions (preemptive
shielding) or correct unsafe proposals (post-posed shielding). It can enforce safety during both learning and execution, but
its guarantees depend on abstraction fidelity, which is challenging for contact-rich manipulation. Recovery RL [416] uses
offline data to identify risky states and separates reward optimization from safety control. A task policy pursues rewards,
while a recovery policy intervenes when predicted violation risk becomes high. Evaluations across six simulated domains
and a physical obstacle-avoidance task reported improved safety–success trade-offs over compared baselines. Human
intervention, as in Sirius [156], allows operators to take control in difficult situations and reuse interventions as training
data, though without formal safety guarantees.
Constrained learning reduces unsafe proposals and reliance on runtime intervention. However, expected-cost constraints
cannot certify individual actions, while shielding guarantees depend on valid dynamics abstractions and continuous en-
forcement.
9.2.2
Plan Validation
A plan may fail before execution by violating task rules, exceeding physical capabilities, or relying on missing information.
For “wash the cup,” mixing incompatible cleaning agents violates a rule, placing the cup out of reach is infeasible, and
an ambiguous cup reference leaves the target unknown. These failures require different responses: rule checking and plan
repair, feasibility-based replanning, or additional observation or clarification.
Semantic Rules and Plan Constraints.
RoboGuard [408] uses two stages to protect language-model-enabled
robots.
First, a root-of-trust language model, isolated from user prompts, grounds predefined safety rules in the
current world model and generates context-dependent temporal-logic constraints. For example, if a person occupies
ground_21, the rule “do not harm anyone” prohibits entering that region, rejecting a plan such as [goto(ground_21),
explore_region(ground_21)]. Second, temporal-logic control synthesis resolves conflicts between plans and safety
rules while minimizing violations of user preferences.
In non-adaptive RoboPAIR tests, RoboGuard reduces attack
success from 92.3% to 2.3% while preserving performance on safe tasks. Adaptive attack success ranges from 2.5% to
5.2%, and real-robot experiments demonstrate its operation. However, its protection depends on complete safety rules
and accurate environmental descriptions; missing hazards or incorrect scene information can invalidate the generated
constraints.
Robot Constitutions [409] uses generated and refined constitutions to identify undesirable situations in ASIMOV, eval-
uated against human judgments. It assesses semantic safety judgments rather than physical constraint satisfaction or
adaptive-attack robustness. Such checks complement geometric safety filters but depend on accurate scene grounding
and valid rules.
Feasibility Checks. A rule-compliant plan may still be physically infeasible due to unreachable grasps, collisions, or de-
pendencies between steps. Per-skill feasibility estimates, as in SayCan, may overlook such dependencies. Text2Motion [78]
addresses this by using language-model-generated skills and learned Q-functions to guide planning, followed by sequence-
level geometric feasibility checks. For example, it can select a grasp that enables a later pull to bring an object within reach.
The resulting plan is verified against an inferred symbolic goal. However, these checks establish feasibility only under the
learned skill models and scene representation, without guaranteeing rule compliance or successful execution.
Calibrated Help-seeking. KnowNo [94] addresses missing information by deciding when to request human clarifica-
tion. A language-model planner scores candidate actions, including a not-listed option, and uses conformal prediction to
calibrate a candidate set with a target coverage probability. The robot acts when one candidate remains, asks a human
66


when several remain, and requests a missing action when needed. Sequence-level calibration extends this approach to
multi-step tasks. KnowNo provides a high-probability coverage guarantee over calibration sets for new tasks drawn from
the same distribution, not for individual instructions. Task completion additionally depends on correct human responses
and successful execution. Its guarantee does not certify physical safety and requires renewed validation under distribution
shifts.
Ultimately, plan validity depends on the rules, models, and information used for verification remaining valid during
execution. This is why a plan-level check cannot replace command-level protection when observations or physical condi-
tions change. Constrained learning and plan validation reduce risk at different stages but depend on assumptions that must
remain valid during execution. Broader risk costs and human intervention data can reduce unsafe policy proposals, while
verified shields provide stronger protection against specific hazards at the cost of restricting behavior. Violations must
therefore be attributed separately to the policy and intervening safeguards. Because scene changes can invalidate plans,
shorter reactive plans and step-wise checks can reduce reliance on persistent assumptions.
9.3
Runtime Safety
Training and plan checks cannot anticipate execution-time changes, such as slipping objects, human intrusion, or sensor fail-
ures. Runtime protection must detect these changes and respond before intervention becomes infeasible. The subsections
follow the intervention decision: monitoring (§ 9.3.1) identifies invalid assumptions or emerging failures; action filtering
(§ 9.3.2) grants, modifies, or withholds permission for a command; recovery or human takeover (§ 9.3.3) determines what
follows when execution cannot continue as proposed. Detection alone cannot prevent harm, and safe stopping does not
ensure task recovery. Figure 24 separates command permission, a feasible fallback, and the subsequent check of whether
the task can continue. Because shared failures can compromise multiple safeguards, both individual and integrated testing
are necessary.
Figure 24: Runtime permission, fallback, and reassessment. (a) Fresh state and constraints can withhold a proposed motion into a
restricted area. (b) Supporting the carried mug is one conditional fallback, requiring a suitable surface and feasible approach. (c) Area
clearance, load stability, and the remaining goal must be checked before proposing another action; assistance or termination may be
necessary. A modified admissible command can also execute directly. Shared sensing, model, or timing failures can invalidate both
permission and fallback assumptions; reaching a safe state does not establish task completion.
9.3.1
Monitoring
Monitoring checks whether execution remains within its intended conditions and whether the task is progressing. The
evidence may concern system health, observed behavior and progress, or predicted future outcomes. Learned detectors
and explanations can support these roles; they are not a separate monitoring target. Signals differ in cost and warning time,
and no single signal proves task failure.
Freshness and System-health Monitoring. Lightweight monitors check sensor data and system health rather than policy
behavior. They detect stale observations, action discontinuities, thermal throttling, calibration drift, and failed control
handoffs. Observations exceeding the task-specific freshness limit (§ 7.3.2) can invalidate downstream decisions even
when processed correctly. Safety surveys [417, 418] emphasize combining physical constraints with health, freshness,
and uncertainty monitoring, while highlighting real-time latency and long-horizon error propagation as VLA challenges.
Timestamp and heartbeat checks are inexpensive, whereas calibration and uncertainty estimates vary in cost and reliability.
67


However, these monitors cannot verify task semantics: grasping the wrong cup may trigger no system-health alarm.
Consistency and Progress Monitoring. Sentinel [419] monitors policy outputs and task progress using two complemen-
tary detectors. A lightweight statistical detector identifies erratic behavior through inconsistencies between successive ac-
tion distributions, while a vision–language model detects task-progression failures where consistent actions fail to achieve
the goal. The latter is invoked only for less time-sensitive cases to reduce computational cost. Evaluations of diffusion
policies for simulated and real-world mobile manipulation show that combining both detectors identifies 18% more failures
than either alone.
Observed execution histories can also support explanations for diagnosis and correction. REFLECT [93] summarizes
multisensory execution histories hierarchically and uses a language model to explain failures for planner correction, evalu-
ated on RoboFail. AHA [420] trains a vision–language model to detect and explain manipulation failures using trajectories
generated by FailGen, which perturbs successful simulated demonstrations. It reports generalization to real-world failures
and unseen tasks. In Manipulate-Anything, AHA verifies subtask completion before proceeding or retrying. However,
useful explanations do not guarantee timely intervention; response latency and available correction time remain critical.
Predictive Monitoring. Model-Based Runtime Monitoring [397] predicts failures using a learned latent-space dynamics
model and failure classifier. It simulates future action outcomes to identify out-of-distribution or high-risk states before
failure occurs. Trained through interactive imitation learning, the monitor continually improves using human–robot experi-
ence. Predictive monitoring provides earlier warnings, but its effectiveness depends on model accuracy and computational
latency.
Threshold-based monitors raise an alarm when a risk statistic exceeds a selected threshold. Lead time is the interval
between the alarm and a specified failure event; it is meaningful only when both events are identified. False alarms require
independent failure or precursor labels. An intervention that prevents a failure does not by itself make the preceding alarm
false. Lower thresholds increase potential false alarms, while higher thresholds risk missed failures. Stale observations
and computationally expensive monitors can delay detection. Effective intervention requires sufficient lead time for control
switching and corrective action, as illustrated by Sentinel’s lightweight consistency detector.
Monitoring methods have complementary limitations: consistency checks observe policy outputs, progress monitors
require task understanding, and predictive monitors depend on learned dynamics. No single method detects every failure.
Evaluation must therefore consider missed failures, false alarms, lead time, and intervention costs, rather than detection
accuracy alone.
9.3.2
Action Filtering
An alarm cannot prevent unsafe execution without a component authorized to intervene. A safety filter provides this pro-
tection by checking each proposed command against defined constraints and modifying or replacing unsafe actions, even
without an alarm. Effective filtering requires meeting control deadlines, using sufficiently fresh state estimates, and provid-
ing feasible alternatives. A learned policy proposes actions, while an independent mechanism authorizes execution based
on freshness and admissibility. This technical permission remains necessary because expected-cost constraints cannot guar-
antee individual actions. Each proposed action chunk has a validity lease; expired chunks are withheld unless revalidated,
with a feasible fallback handling interruptions. Permission also requires fresh state estimates with justified uncertainty
bounds. In the multirate architecture of § 7.3.4, the policy generates proposals at a slower rate, while a faster protection
layer checks them. This separation is effective only when the checker has valid constraints, meets timing requirements,
and has sufficiently independent failure modes. Learned permission checks additionally require evidence of calibration,
coverage, and timing.
Command Checks. A unified view of safety filters [331] identifies two components: a monitor that evaluates proposed
commands and an intervention mechanism that replaces unsafe ones. The main approaches differ in how safety is assessed.
Reachability-based filters use dynamics and bounded disturbances to identify states from which violations remain avoidable.
With the value function positive inside the safe set, a minimally restrictive filter accepts nominal commands in the interior
and invokes a safety controller at the boundary. However, grid-based Hamilton–Jacobi methods scale exponentially with
state dimension, while approximations may weaken safety guarantees. Control barrier functions (CBFs) [330] constrain
how quickly the system approaches unsafe states. For a safe set C = {x : b(x) ≥0} and control-affine dynamics
˙x = f(x) + G(x)u, a CBF filter solves
u⋆= arg min
u∈U
1
2∥u −unom∥2
s.t.
Lfb(x) + LGb(x) u ≥−κ
 b(x)

.
(15)
Here, x is the modeled physical state, f and G define the dynamics, b defines the safe set, Lfb and LGb are its derivatives
along the dynamics, U contains feasible inputs, and κ is an extended class-K function. The filter minimally modifies the
nominal command unom, leaving it unchanged when admissible. Forward invariance requires an initially safe state, valid
barrier and dynamics, feasible controls, suitable regularity, and consistent timing. The control layer also matters: position
constraints may have relative degree one for velocity inputs but two for torque inputs, requiring higher-order barriers or
equivalent constructions [421]. Continuous-time guarantees do not automatically extend to sampled or delayed execution.
Predictive safety filters [422] instead check whether a proposed command admits a feasible backup trajectory satisfying
68


state and input constraints and reaching a terminal safe set. If not, they select the closest command with a feasible backup.
Under exact dynamics, the remaining backup supports feasibility over a shrinking horizon. With uncertainty, constraint
tightening, confidence sets, and stabilizability assumptions provide probabilistic constraint satisfaction from initially fea-
sible states, followed by a terminal safety policy. These guarantees require timely optimization and do not cover arbitrary
solver failures.
Command Modification and Controller Switching. Command projection, as in CBF filters, modifies proposed actions
while retaining the policy as the nominal command source. Simplex architectures [329] instead transfer control from an
advanced controller to a verified backup when needed. Safe switching requires a justified recoverable region, sufficient lead
time for sensing and switching delays, and rules for returning control without compromising safety or causing excessive
switching. Bak et al. [423] combine an offline recoverable region with online reachability analysis to verify switching for
a saturated inverted-pendulum model. Their guarantee is model-specific, illustrating how reachable states, backup control,
and switching deadlines must be linked to establish safe intervention.
Both checking and intervention depend on implementation conditions. A safety filter requires more than correct opti-
mization: it needs fresh state estimates with justified uncertainty bounds, feasible corrective actions, and a fallback when
optimization fails. The CBF-QP (Eq. (15)) may become infeasible near constraint boundaries. Assumption monitors
should therefore check sensor freshness, estimator uncertainty, model residuals, and command feasibility, with calibrated
thresholds and responses. Small tracking errors do not prove model validity, and excessive perception latency can invalidate
physical safety guarantees.
Resource independence also matters. Shared scheduling, memory, power, or state-estimation failures can disable both
the policy and its safeguards. Isolation may use reserved memory bandwidth, as in MemGuard [271], or separate real-time
controllers. CREATE [424] further shows how voltage-induced bit errors can threaten task safety, highlighting shared
power-related failure risks even in otherwise isolated components. A separate enforcement mechanism can constrain a
general-purpose policy within a narrower validated safety scope. Its interface must specify enforced constraints, sensing
and dynamics assumptions, response deadlines, and required resources. Broader hazards involving context and user intent
may require additional safeguards beyond collision and force limits [410].
9.3.3
Recovery and Human Takeover
When monitoring raises an alarm or filtering finds no admissible command, the robot must still respond safely. Three
outcomes differ: safe-state recovery removes immediate hazards, task recovery restores conditions for achieving the goal,
and task resumption continues execution. Achieving one does not guarantee the next. Responses range from deterministic
stops to learned recovery and human takeover, depending on the robot’s physical state. Importantly, stopping is not always
safe: freezing a legged robot mid-stride may cause a fall.
Stops and Fallback Ladders. Machinery standards distinguish stops by motion control and actuator power. IEC 60204-
1 [425] defines category 0 as uncontrolled stopping through immediate power removal, category 1 as controlled stopping
followed by power removal, and category 2 as controlled stopping with power retained. IEC 60204-1:2016 generally
requires emergency stops to use category 0 or 1 based on risk assessment, with exceptions when retaining power prevents
additional hazards and monitored standstill is provided. ISO/TS 15066 [406] specifies safety-rated monitored stops during
operator presence, permitting automatic resumption under defined conditions. A fallback ladder prioritizes responses that
remain feasible without relying on failed components. Possible responses include action projection, controlled deceleration,
isolated braking, and power removal, subject to verified switching and stopping conditions. IEC stop categories apply
only when the corresponding power and motion sequences are satisfied. Because power removal may release a load or
destabilize the robot, fallback selection requires system-specific hazard analysis and verification of sensing, actuation, and
load-support capabilities.
Learned Recovery. A learned recovery policy can intervene before a predicted violation if its switching rule and recovery
region are validated. Recovery RL activates a recovery policy when estimated risk becomes high. Its reliability depends
on coverage of recovery states and calibration of the switching rule, including missed detections and false alarms.
Human Assistance and Takeover. ThriftyDAgger [189] requests human intervention when states are novel, measured
by policy-ensemble disagreement, or risky, measured by a learned estimate of task-failure probability. It sets intervention
thresholds using quantiles of observed novelty and risk under a supervision budget, and returns control when the robot’s
action agrees with the supervisor’s and risk falls below a second threshold. In a study involving ten participants supervising
simulated peg-insertion robots while performing a memory-card task, ThriftyDAgger improved both robot-task completion
and human task performance relative to the respective next-best baselines, while reducing intervention burden. However,
its risk estimate concerns task success rather than physical harm, so human takeover complements rather than replaces
physical safety mechanisms.
Human takeover requires a defined control transition and bounded handover delay. If the delay exceeds the remaining
safety margin, the robot needs a safe waiting behavior. As in Sirius, interventions can also provide training data. Avoiding
violations does not guarantee task completion, so autonomous and human-assisted outcomes must be evaluated separately.
Software restart differs from physical-state recovery. Saved models and task estimates support restarting, but fresh
observations must validate the robot and environment states before plans can be reused. Task recovery requires detecting
69


deviations, interrupting stale actions, reaching a valid continuation state, and verifying resumption feasibility. Reaching
a safe state does not establish that the remaining task is feasible (Figure 24). When correction or validated fallback is
unavailable, the limitation must be recorded and assistance sought where possible; an alert alone does not establish safety.
Across monitoring, filtering, and recovery, task-aware monitoring and selective sensing can improve useful warning
time, while robust control may remove a failure the detector would otherwise have to predict. Recovery is useful when the
robot can reach a physical state from which the remaining goal can be pursued; human takeover can supply capabilities
or information absent from autonomous recovery. Reducing human support per completed task must preserve protection
during recovery and handoff, linking timely execution and control to long-horizon autonomy.
9.4
Adversarial Robustness
Adversaries can induce unsafe behavior by manipulating inputs, data, or messages, requiring adversarial testing beyond
natural-failure evaluation. The risk definitions of § 9.1 still apply, but an attacker can intentionally target the observations,
models, or communication on which learning, planning, and runtime safeguards depend. The analysis separates what an
attacker can do and aims to induce (§ 9.4.1), where a compromise enters the system (§ 9.4.2), and which defenses can limit
its effects at those boundaries (§ 9.4.3).
9.4.1
Threat Models
Attack success rates are meaningful only under a defined threat model. Attackers may manipulate visual or language inputs,
poison training data, implant backdoors, disrupt communication or timing, or compromise updates. Their capabilities
differ in access, model knowledge, persistence, and physical proximity. Inference-time attacks require deployment access,
whereas training-time backdoors are implanted earlier and activated by later triggers. Attack objectives also vary, from task
failure and dangerous actions to attacker-targeted trajectories. Each requires different capabilities and evaluation criteria.
Consequently, success rates cannot be directly compared across threat models or objectives: targeted backdoor success and
patch-induced task degradation measure different risks. Defenses must therefore be evaluated against explicitly defined
attacker capabilities and goals.
9.4.2
Attack Surfaces
Attack surfaces differ both in the information channel being manipulated and in the stage at which the attacker gains
access. We first examine visual and language inputs, then training and runtime infrastructure. These dimensions intersect:
a training-time backdoor can later be activated through a visual input, while a compromised service can corrupt either
observations or timing. Defenses must detect or limit effects downstream of the compromise.
Observation and Instruction Inputs. Adversarial vulnerabilities in VLA models [426] include two untargeted objectives
that destabilize actions and a targeted objective that redirects trajectories. A small visual patch can induce these attacks
in digital and physical settings, reducing task success by up to 100% across simulated tasks. The attacker requires access
to the camera view and, for physical attacks, the scene, but not training data. EDPA [427] develops a model-agnostic
patch that disrupts vision–language semantic alignment and increases differences between clean and adversarial visual
representations. On LIBERO, it induces repeated incorrect actions and task failures. Unlike attacks targeting specific
action architectures, EDPA exploits shared backbone features. However, it still assumes access to encoder representations,
so model-agnostic does not imply fully black-box access.
Language instructions provide another input channel. RoboPAIR [428] demonstrates how adversarial instructions can
induce harmful actions in language-model-controlled robots. It evaluates three access settings: white-box attacks on a
self-driving language model, gray-box attacks on a wheeled robot using GPT-4o, and black-box query-only attacks on a
commercial quadruped. Across three harmful-action datasets, RoboPAIR and static baselines rapidly achieve high attack
success rates, often reaching 100%. These results concern adversarial task rewriting, which requires separate evaluation
from plan compliance under benign instructions.
Training and Runtime Infrastructure. AttackVLA [429] examines inference-time attacks and training-time backdoors
across the VLA lifecycle. It evaluates existing VLA and adapted vision–language attacks across action tokenizers in
simulation and on real robots, finding that most cause untargeted failures or frozen actions. Its BackdoorVLA instead
uses a trigger to induce attacker-specified long-horizon action sequences, achieving an average targeted success rate of
58.4% across simulated and real-world evaluations. Because backdoored models may behave normally without the trigger,
benign testing may fail to reveal the threat. The VLA safety survey [418] further identifies data poisoning, backdoors, and
data-supply-chain vulnerabilities as training-time security risks.
Execution-time attacks can compromise communication, timing, services, or model updates without altering training
data. Delayed or replayed sensor streams may appear valid without trusted timestamps and source authentication, making
freshness checks (§ 9.3.1) and action leases (§ 9.3.2) important security controls.
70


9.4.3
Defense Mechanisms
The entry points above require defenses at different interfaces: visual perturbations call for input or representation protec-
tion, adversarial instructions require task-permission and plan checks, and replayed sensor streams require freshness and
source checks. No single mechanism covers all of these threats, so protection combines checks across interfaces, with
trade-offs in accuracy, task acceptance, and latency. Each defense must be evaluated against adaptive attackers and on
benign tasks, accounting for its checked properties, attack objectives, and assumptions about input integrity. Hardening
reduces attack sensitivity, while guardrails and safety filters limit harmful consequences within their specified constraints
and validated threat models.
Representation Hardening. EDPA [427] also proposes adversarial fine-tuning to align visual representations of clean
and perturbed inputs, reducing patch-induced task failures on LIBERO. This strengthens the encoder without manual
input screening but protects primarily against the trained perturbation family. Because encoder changes affect downstream
skills, performance on benign tasks must also be evaluated under varied objects, scenes, and camera conditions. Runtime
adversarial-input detection provides complementary protection but remains subject to missed detections and false alarms.
Plan-level Guardrails. RoboGuard evaluates defense effectiveness against both non-adaptive and adaptive jailbreaks while
measuring performance on benign tasks. Its planner-to-executor guardrail restricts unsafe plans only when safety rules,
scene grounding, and enforcement remain trustworthy. Attacks that corrupt upstream scene descriptions can invalidate its
constraints, while attacks bypassing the planning interface, such as visual patches targeting reactive policies, fall outside
its protection.
Action Filtering and Isolation. Safety filters limit unsafe physical actions without identifying the underlying attack, while
isolated runtime mechanisms reduce reliance on compromised policy processors within their trusted boundaries. However,
they cannot prevent attacks that satisfy the enforced constraints: a robot may obey force and speed limits yet carry a cup
to an attacker-chosen destination.
Provenance and Authentication. Training-time and service attacks require system-level defenses beyond the model. Data
provenance and signature verification help prevent unauthorized datasets or weights from entering deployment, while back-
door screening provides limited assurance because triggers may remain untested. SROS2 [430] supports DDS-Security
configuration in ROS 2, including certificate-based authentication, signed permissions, and AES-GCM authenticated en-
cryption for protected traffic. These mechanisms verify message origin, integrity, and access permissions, but not freshness,
correctness, or safety. Freshness requires trusted timestamps and validity checks, such as action leases (§ 9.3.2), while au-
thenticated but compromised components can still transmit false information.
Residual threats include corrupted shared state estimates, undetected backdoors, compromised authenticated compo-
nents, and malicious objectives that satisfy enforced constraints. Certifying robustness across complete embodied tra-
jectories remains an open challenge. Across adversarial defenses, attacks that persist in memory or activate later can
evade single-input checks. Layered defenses limit their effects only when relevant signals are observable and trustworthy;
authenticated provenance does not guarantee physical correctness. Evaluation must therefore trace attack propagation,
shared dependencies, and feasible responses after detection.
Safety interventions also require reassessment. For example, stopping an arm holding a cup changes the physical state
and may invalidate assumptions needed for subsequent actions. Evaluating combined safeguards must therefore consider
false rejections, task costs, shared failures, and recovery. Open challenges include protection against shared state-estimation
errors and determining which safety guarantees require revalidation after policy, model, or hardware updates.
10
Open Challenges
The preceding chapters expose a gap between component performance under tested conditions and sustained task comple-
tion as tasks, bodies, and environments change. The research questions concern useful state estimates and transfer (§ 10.1),
data and computation budgets (§ 10.2), safety during adaptation (§ 10.3), and continued operation and learning (§ 10.4).
These motivate joint design (§ 10.5) and evidence for sustained capability (§ 10.6). Figure 25 connects these limitations;
each must be assessed by the capability preserved and the total cost incurred.
Figure 25: From open problems to joint design. State and transfer, data and compute, safety during adaptation, and continuation and
cumulative learning motivate joint design. Evidence for sustained capability tests these choices and informs their revision.
71


10.1
State Estimation and Transfer under Change
Changes can invalidate information, behavior, or physical feasibility. The subsections ask what information a decision
requires (§ 10.1.1), how simultaneous shifts interact (§ 10.1.2), and what must change for a new body (§ 10.1.3). Tests
must isolate these causes while counting adaptation effort.
10.1.1
Information for Physical Decisions
Vision–language correspondence, spatial structure, and predicted transitions support physical decisions, but their value
depends on the action. Recognizing a cup identifies the target, while grasping a wet cup may require additional contact
information. The key question is what information each action needs, at what accuracy, and for how long it remains reliable.
World-model comparisons distinguish training-time information from deployment-time prediction. Fast-WAM examines
this distinction with and without test-time future generation [123]. Simpler deployment may suffice in some cases, while
delayed or irreversible decisions may require explicit lookahead. Representation failures may result from missing physical
information or poor use of available information. Active observation and probing can help recover hidden contact states.
However, tactile representations require calibration to provide accurate force or contact measurements; usefulness for
control alone is insufficient. Joint-torque residuals cannot detect all contact wrenches, and estimates maintained through
occlusion must account for age and uncertainty.
10.1.2
Combined Changes in Tasks and Conditions
Benchmarks reviewed in § 8.1.2 test different forms of reuse, but combined changes can be more damaging than isolated
ones, as THE COLOSSEUM shows [382]. Appearance robustness may fail when a different body also changes grasp
geometry. Shortcut and instruction-following tests further show that nominal success can conceal missing capabilities [243,
384]. The unresolved issue is how to preserve capability across interacting shifts. Factor-specific and combined trials
should separate fixed-policy behavior, memory updates, and fine-tuning (§ 8.4.1), identifying when failures require new
representations, broader experience, or physical feedback.
10.1.3
Transfer to New Bodies
The transfer routes in § 5.5.3 leave a central question: which body differences can calibration and control absorb, and
which require new behavior? Reachability, compliance, grasp geometry, and payload dynamics can invalidate actions even
with identical dimensions. Tests should separate similar-hardware transfer from changes in morphology, vary interfaces
and policies independently and jointly, and retest earlier robots for capability loss. Tools or controllers may resolve some
mismatches more efficiently than policy relearning.
10.2
Data and Computation Budgets
Resource allocation concerns which experience to acquire or reuse (§ 10.2.1), how to diagnose failures (§ 10.2.2), how
much computation buys timely feedback (§ 10.2.3), and how to share it across robots (§ 10.2.4). Comparisons must include
learning, calibration, validation, and human costs within the boundaries of § 8.1.4.
10.2.1
Choosing and Scaling Experience
The data sources in Table 1 and allocation methods in § 5.4 do not establish a universal substitution rate between non-
robot data and physical interaction. Additional demonstrations, novel conditions, and reweighting offer different benefits
depending on current coverage and model capacity. The open question is when to reuse, augment, or collect experience.
Allocation should target missing contact or recovery states while counting generation, verification, correction, calibration,
and reset costs. It must generalize across tasks and learners, rather than optimize one dataset; training-throughput gains
must also be separated from data-efficiency gains.
10.2.2
Learning from Failures
A missed grasp such as E1 may result from localization errors, missing corrective behavior, contact or frame mismatches, or
delays. Each cause suggests a different remedy: improved observations, corrective demonstrations, calibration or control
changes, or fresher feedback. Sirius, HIL-SERL, and ThriftyDAgger target difficult, novel, or risky states for human
assistance [155, 156, 189], while broader system perspectives question whether model scaling alone addresses physical
limitations [431]. However, identifying difficult states does not establish their causes.
Selecting effective corrections after failure requires evidence about the failed component. Additional demonstrations
cannot necessarily resolve runtime delays, and new images may not reveal hidden contact properties. Diagnosis is uncertain
and may require expert effort. Observed outcomes constrain possible explanations, but do not identify causes or validate
72


corrective actions by themselves. Component tests can support the proposed correction mechanism; claims of improved
physical task performance additionally require trials that measure the relevant error together with assistance and reset costs.
Effective diagnosis should reduce both failures and the costs of intervention, demonstrations, resets, and expert analysis.
Choosing between targeted data collection and direct system repair requires considering diagnostic uncertainty, potential
errors, and total correction costs rather than defaulting to more data.
10.2.3
Computation for Timely Feedback
The timing analysis in §§ 7.1.2 and 7.3 leaves an allocation problem: how much computation should each task phase
receive? Contact transitions and moving targets require fresh feedback, whereas slower motions may tolerate longer ac-
tion prefixes. Faster inference alone need not improve task performance [277]. Jointly vary generation cost, executed
prefix, feedback frequency, and controller settings under fixed task requirements and resource budgets. Measure dead-
line reliability, completion time, motion quality, and energy or human effort per completed task, including retries and
resets. Cross-family validation matters because simpler generation may remove the workload that specialized hardware
accelerates [354].
10.2.4
Shared Computing Across Robots
Shared-serving mechanisms in § 7.5.3 leave reliable physical performance under contention unresolved. Scheduling
changes observation age and action quality differently across task phases, while aggregate throughput can hide repeated
stalls. Tests should link resource allocation to each robot’s deadlines, buffer depletion, success, energy, and fallback under
disconnection, with explicit fairness criteria. RobotPerf supplies pipeline measurements, but these alone do not establish
fleet-level task reliability [432].
10.3
Safety during Adaptation
An update or shared failure can invalidate several safeguards at once. The questions are how to compose compatible inter-
ventions under joint failures (§ 10.3.1) and which safety claims require renewed testing after updates (§ 10.3.2), building
on the mechanisms in § 9.
10.3.1
Combining Safety Mechanisms
The learning, planning, monitoring, filtering, and recovery mechanisms in § 9 protect different boundaries. Combining
them improves coverage only if their assumptions remain compatible and their interventions remain physically feasible.
The key challenge is maintaining safety guarantees under changing conditions and combined failures. Payload changes,
occlusion, unavailable operators, hardware faults, and adversarial attacks can invalidate multiple safety mechanisms simul-
taneously [424, 433]. Semantic hazards and compromised inputs may also escape existing constraints [409, 410, 426, 428].
Safety composition must therefore identify invalid assumptions and preserve feasible responses, including under persis-
tent attacks. Reliable safety requires timely detection, valid fallback actions, and verification under joint failures. Safety
coverage and task completion must be evaluated separately, since successful intervention does not guarantee task com-
pletion. Rare-event testing through simulation or importance sampling remains limited by the modeled dynamics and
disturbances [411].
10.3.2
Maintaining Safety after Updates
Safety claims apply to specific policies, robot bodies, and operating conditions. Adaptation can invalidate these claims
by changing policy behavior, monitor calibration, or filter compatibility. The key challenge is maintaining valid safety
constraints and interventions as the system evolves. Each update must identify affected estimates, dynamics assumptions,
and intervention conditions, and determine which safety checks require repetition. Improved task performance alone does
not establish safety, and previous guarantees remain valid only within their original assumptions. A particular challenge
is preserving stability and safety when learned impedance gains or residual corrections change, even if the underlying
controller structure remains unchanged. Release tests therefore need to exercise the updated policy together with its monitor,
filter, and fallback under the changed conditions.
10.4
Task Continuation and Cumulative Learning
Sustained autonomy extends from valid task state and memory (§ 10.4.1), through plan revision (§ 10.4.2) and recovery
(§ 10.4.3), to continuity across faults (§ 10.4.4) and retention across updates (§ 10.4.5). These require distinct evidence
and support-cost measures; justified help requests or termination can remain necessary.
73


10.4.1
Maintaining Task-State Estimates and Memory
The unresolved issue is distinguishing valid current information from historical records. Past failures should remain avail-
able, while locations and skill preconditions may need revision after environmental or body changes. Memory maintenance
(§ 2.3.4) addresses parts of this problem, but hidden motion can leave stored locations incorrect [57]. Revisits, hidden mo-
tion, and execution disturbances should distinguish retrieval failures from stale-memory errors. The research trade-off is
between storage, retrieval, and re-observation as tasks lengthen, rather than memory capacity alone.
10.4.2
Revising Task Plans
Corrections must target the responsible decision level: gripper misalignment may need local control, whereas a missing
object requires search. Feedback-driven planning (§ 3.3.4) and world–action prediction [434] leave open when to switch
levels or trust a forecast. Selective reasoning may focus effort on difficult decisions [435], but plausible generated futures
need not preserve physical consistency [436, 437]. Evaluation should test whether selecting correction levels reduces
unnecessary retries and replanning while improving completion, and whether prediction’s benefit justifies its latency and
computational cost.
10.4.3
Recovery with Less Human Assistance
Safe-state recovery, recovery of task feasibility, and task resumption have different success conditions (§ 9.3.3). Existing
correction methods address parts of this process, including specialized surgical workflows [438]. The challenge is connect-
ing these stages after a failure changes the physical state and remaining goal. Compare methods from identical failed states
through resumption, assistance, or justified termination without intervening resets. Learned recovery, explicit precondi-
tions, and environment redesign address different limitations. Measure the remaining goal completed and the human/reset
costs incurred. Feasible recovery also enables repeated learning experience, connecting continuation to the data-budget
challenges in Figure 25.
10.4.4
Sustained Operation
Sustained operation must preserve useful output across updates and faults (§ 8.1.3). Rollback may not restore earlier
behavior after physical changes, and restarts can retain stale memory or invalid safety assumptions. The update pipeline in
§ 7.6.3 must therefore be tested as a complete configuration of models, calibration, controllers, and hardware. Track lost
progress, restored task execution, and support costs across repeated faults and updates. The open problem is cumulative
improvement with bounded verification costs and without sacrificing service continuity or safety.
10.4.5
Improvement Without Forgetting
The storage choices in § 5.6 create an unresolved allocation problem: where should reusable experience reside to limit
interference, staleness, and maintenance? General skills and temporary task information may need different representations.
Even replay eviction can discard valuable past behavior. Follow one evolving system through new and retained tasks,
measuring retrieval latency, storage, composition, and validation costs. This establishes whether modular storage reduces
maintenance or merely postpones consolidation; local adaptation alone does not demonstrate cumulative improvement.
10.5
Joint Design Choices
Joint design considers dependencies among feedback, learning, recovery, and safety (§ 10.5.1), how task and body design
can change them (§ 10.5.2), and alternative allocations of generation and experience (§ 10.5.3). A larger model may
improve decisions while delaying their use; a recovery method may reduce assistance only within a feasible physical
workspace.
10.5.1
Dependencies Across Aspects
Completing more tasks with less human assistance requires compatible representations, feasible actions, valid memory,
safe recovery, reliable feedback, and continual learning without losing existing capabilities. Safety and evaluation must
also remain valid after system updates. Two dependencies may become critical even as policy generation improves. First,
learning requires trustworthy outcome verification: plausible plans or videos cannot establish task success without ap-
propriate measurements. Second, physical experience must be collected repeatedly at acceptable cost, including damage,
resets, assistance, and equipment availability. Better sensing, verification, recovery, and data collection may therefore mat-
ter as much as improved learning algorithms. Progress toward autonomous self-improvement should be evaluated through
these dependencies, individually and jointly. Both modular, task-specific systems and unified approaches may offer viable
paths, with different trade-offs in capability, cost, and engineering complexity.
74


10.5.2
Task, Body, and Control Design
Task and environment design can improve robustness, efficiency, safety, and autonomy simultaneously. Suitable grippers,
accessible workspaces, reliable fixtures, and clear completion signals simplify perception, control, and verification. The
resource and human costs discussed in § 8.1.4 require evaluating the complete workflow, including engineering, mainte-
nance, reconfiguration, and human effort. Shifting costs to operators or equipment does not necessarily improve efficiency.
Skill interfaces, explicit task-state estimates, improved controllers, and task–environment co-design can reduce reliance
on general policies. These approaches may improve task-specific performance without increasing generality. Their value
depends on sustained output and lower total costs across deployments, while extensive task-specific redesign may limit
scalability. Model size and inference speed alone cannot capture these trade-offs.
10.5.3
Alternative Approaches
Three alternatives could reduce the costs of repeated generation, physical data collection, and interaction learning. Their
value depends on the capabilities preserved and the total costs reduced.
OpenVLA-OFT’s comparison of L1 and diffusion heads and Fast-WAM’s variants with and without test-time future
generation suggest opportunities to simplify action generation, within the tested tasks, training conditions, and inference
settings [104, 123]. If simpler methods maintain performance on diverse real tasks under matched data and training
budgets, resources could shift toward sensing, control, and deployment. However, tasks requiring multimodal actions or
consequential lookahead may still benefit from complex generation. Selective generation may therefore be preferable to
universal replacement; hardware specialized for a particular decoder must accommodate such changes.
Human video, simulation, generated trajectories, and data selection may reduce the need for physical interaction [66,
135, 142, 212]. Real experience could focus on calibration, action correspondence, and difficult failures, while other
sources provide broader coverage. However, existing evidence does not establish a universal substitution rate. The critical
test is whether comparable capability can be achieved with lower total interaction and human costs, including generation,
filtering, selection, and validation. Failures under new contact conditions or embodiments would reveal where physical
experience remains essential.
Reliable outcome verification, automatic resets, and inexpensive corrections may improve interaction learning more than
faster optimization. Memory and skill updates may also support cumulative learning without immediate consolidation
into shared weights. Existing studies provide partial mechanisms [59, 155, 208, 372], while Learning While Deploying
integrates fleet experience, interventions, and shared-policy redeployment [229]. Stronger evidence requires sustained
learning with lower human and reset costs, retained capabilities, and independently validated outcomes. Greater rollout
throughput alone is insufficient, particularly when evaluation errors are correlated with policy errors or maintenance costs
increase.
10.6
Evidence for Sustained Capability
Policy improvement changes data distributions, intervention demand, and evaluator validity. Extending the protocols of § 8
therefore requires comparable physical tests and costs (§ 10.6.1), renewed surrogate validation (§ 10.6.2), and milestones
tracked across successive updates (§ 10.6.3).
10.6.1
Comparable Evidence
Similarly named tasks can differ in physical difficulty and human support. Beyond the controls of § 8.4, the challenge
is identifying omitted conditions that change conclusions across bodies and deployments. Paired tasks and independent
replication should preserve physical diversity while specifying target access, update persistence, and intervention, reset,
maintenance, and learning costs.
10.6.2
Validity of Surrogate Evaluation
As policies improve, they may encounter unsupported states or exploit evaluator weaknesses. Shared assumptions between
training and assessment can create correlated errors, so the surrogates in § 8.3 need renewed physical validation even when
the evaluator itself is unchanged. The open question is how to detect validity loss with fewer physical trials. Targeting
new bodies, policy-induced states, and contact failures may outperform uniform sampling. Tests must distinguish correct
policy ranking from reliable absolute release thresholds.
10.6.3
Milestones Toward Sustained Capability
The reviewed evidence, primarily from manipulation and its computing systems, suggests near-term milestones: lower
adaptation cost at fixed performance, fewer manual resets, and sustained task quality with less computation and assistance.
75


These require physical tests of combined mechanisms; benefits cannot be inferred by adding separate component results.
The key limitation is scalability. If each additional task requires extensive reward design, reset engineering, or calibra-
tion, strong individual-task performance may not translate into reusable capability. Evaluation should therefore report the
marginal cost of adding tasks or operating conditions alongside final performance.
Long-term milestones require following one system through successive tasks, updates, physical changes, and operational
faults. Cumulative improvement must preserve earlier capabilities within bounded memory, interaction, and validation
costs, while sustained deployment maintains useful output. A convincing record would pair each new capability with
retained-task tests, safety revalidation, assistance and reset counts, and useful output over operating time; it would also
state where the claim stops when conditions or bodies change.
11
Conclusion
This survey has presented a system-level review of Physical AI organized around the closed-loop process of physical
interaction. It has examined task representations and memory, predictive and action-generating models, data and learning
methods, embodiment and control, computing systems, evaluation, and safety. Manipulation provides the main technical
evidence, with examples from other robotic systems clarifying shared mechanisms and constraints.
A central finding is that physical capability depends on how these components work together through feedback. Ad-
vances in foundation models and robot learning expand what physical agents can perceive, reason about, and do, while
reliable autonomy requires those capabilities to remain effective under partial observability, uncertain dynamics, embod-
iment constraints, and real-time requirements. Representations must support feasible decisions; execution must respond
while observations remain relevant; and observed outcomes must provide reliable evidence for correction and learning.
The practical value of a model therefore depends on the sensing, memory, control, computation, and supervision that sup-
port its use. Evaluating complete systems under stated conditions is essential to establish how component improvements
translate into physical outcomes.
The remaining challenges concern generalization, resource-efficient learning and execution, reliable state tracking,
safety, recovery, and continual adaptation. Progress requires systems that transfer capabilities as tasks, environments,
and bodies change, use limited experience and computation effectively, recover with less human assistance, and acquire
new behaviors while preserving earlier capabilities and safety. Robustness, efficiency, safety, and autonomy provide com-
plementary criteria for assessing this progress, with adaptation costs and operational support made explicit. Advancing
Physical AI requires moving beyond isolated model performance toward integrated systems capable of robust, efficient,
safe, and autonomous physical interaction under changing real-world conditions.
